## Question
When a small neural network learns to add two n-bit numbers, in what order does it learn the output bits, and does it build an internal structure that tracks the carry (a ripple chain, a lookahead, something else)? Bitwise XOR is the same operation with all carries removed, so it is the control.

## Why this question (where the idea came from)
The XOR-versus-addition masking project found that XOR's parity part is learned late. Binary addition differs from XOR only by the carries, so it is the cleanest case to ask what the carry costs. The owner also just learned about adders (ripple-carry and carry-lookahead) and wants to compare what the network builds with the real circuits. Literature check: done by another chat, see literature.md (unverified leads). Learning order, carry probes and long-carry failure have prior work in transformers, RNNs and Neural GPUs; the narrower gap is below. No claim of novelty.

## Plan (predictions written before running; nothing has been run yet)
Task: inputs a, b as n-bit vectors (2n inputs); targets: the n+1 sum bits (addition) or n bits (XOR). n = 4 to 8. For n up to 6 every pair can be enumerated (4096 pairs), so train/test splits are exact. Small MLP, a few layers, binary cross-entropy per output bit. Fixed seeds, several seeds.
- **C-01 Learning order.** Per-bit test accuracy over training steps for addition and XOR. Prediction: for addition, bit i is learned later as i grows (the delay grows with carry-chain depth); for XOR all bits are learned at about the speed of addition's bit 0. If the delay is flat across bits, the carry costs nothing in training time for these sizes.
- **C-02 Carry probe.** After training, fit a linear probe from the hidden layers to "carry into bit i". Prediction: probe accuracy above 95 percent in at least one hidden layer for every i once the sum is learned; for XOR the probe has nothing to find (the carry is not used). Probe accuracy for the carry of high bits rises later in training than for low bits.
- **C-03 Partial data.** Train on a random 50 percent of the pairs. Prediction: test accuracy is lower for high bits (long carry chains) than low bits, and XOR bits generalize equally well.
- **C-04 Size needed.** Smallest hidden width that reaches exact addition on all pairs, for n = 3 to 6, against XOR. Prediction: it grows faster with n for addition than for XOR. (A statement about trained networks, not a proof about minimal circuits.)
What would change my mind: no delay difference across bits in C-01, or probes that find the carry equally well at initialization (then the probe measures the input, not a learned representation; I will include an untrained-network probe as a control).

## What is already answered, and what we add (sources: literature.md; rows marked "opened" were read by me)
| Sub-question | Already reported | What we add |
|---|---|---|
| Q1 learning order of output bits | Naidu 2025 (opened): early output positions learned first in transformers, no XOR control. Rumelhart 1985 (lead): 2-bit addition, second bit lags. | Ordinary MLPs; widths n = 4 to 8; XOR as a matched control; many seeds; timing of each bit measured in steps. |
| Q2 position or carry length | Price 2016 (opened): errors grow with carry length for a Neural GPU, but this is generalization to longer inputs. Not measured in Naidu 2025 (opened). | Per-example carry run length on held-out pairs at fixed bit position, for addition and XOR (the part nobody in the opened papers measured). |
| Q3 carry inside the network | Naidu 2025 (opened): probes and causal ablation in transformers; Michaud 2024 (opened): ripple-adder carry variable extracted from RNNs. | Probes and ablation in plain MLPs, an untrained-network control, and the timing against bit accuracy; a XOR network as a negative control (it should not need a carry). |
| Q4 what kind of circuit | Michaud (opened): ripple adder from RNNs; Maltoni and Ferrara (lead): numerical-value scheme, no ripple or lookahead found; Primozic (lead, blog): analog weighted sum. | Whether MLPs look ripple-like, lookahead-like or value-like, by depth/width scaling with n and by ablation. |

## Narrowed question after the literature check (leads only)
Prior work (unverified by me) covers carry probes and learning order in transformers, a ripple-adder extracted from RNNs, and long-carry failures in Neural GPUs. What I could not find is a matched study in ordinary MLPs that (a) compares addition with XOR on the same inputs and (b) separates the position of an output bit from the actual carry-chain length in each example. That is the version to test.
Added experiment (prediction written before running):
- **C-05 Error against actual carry length.** For a network trained on part of the pairs, take every held-out pair and compute the longest run of consecutive carries that reaches each output bit. Prediction: at fixed bit position, test error rises with the carry run length reaching that bit; for XOR the same split shows no such trend. If error depends on bit position but not on the carry run length, the delay is not about carry chains.


## Design fixed before the first run (2026-10-24)
Task. n-bit unsigned a and b, inputs are the 2n bits (least significant first), values 0 or 1. Targets: addition, n+1 sum bits; XOR, n bits. n = 6 (4096 pairs, all enumerated) and n = 8 (65,536 pairs). Random 50 percent of the pairs for training, the other 50 percent held out (same split for addition and XOR within a seed).
Model. MLP with 3 hidden layers of 128 ReLU units, one logit per output bit, binary cross-entropy summed over bits. Adam, learning rate 1e-3, batch 256, 10,000 steps, 5 seeds (0 to 4), CPU, float32.
Measures. Every 100 steps: accuracy of each output bit on the held-out pairs. Bit learning time = first evaluated step at which that bit's held-out accuracy is at least 0.99 and stays so at the next evaluation.
Carry run length. For a pair, c_i is the carry into bit i (c_0 = 0). The carry run length reaching bit i is the number of consecutive ones in c_i, c_{i-1}, ..., going down from i (0 if c_i = 0). It is computed from the addition of the pair and also used to split the XOR errors, since XOR networks see the same pairs. Held-out error rate is tabulated per (bit, run length) at steps 2,000, 5,000 and 10,000.
Known trivialities to keep in mind: bit 0 of the sum equals bit 0 of XOR (no carry in); the top bit of the sum is the carry out of the whole word.
Before-run predictions are the ones listed under C-01 and C-05 above. Additional numerical predictions: (C-01) for n = 8 and addition, the held-out accuracy of the top three sum bits reaches 0.99 later than that of bits 0 to 2 in at least 4 of 5 seeds; for XOR all bits reach 0.99 within a factor 1.5 of each other's time in at least 4 of 5 seeds. (C-05) at the final step and at fixed bit i of at least 4, held-out error for run lengths of at least 3 exceeds the error for run length 0 in addition for at least 4 of 5 seeds; for XOR no such ordering in more than 2 of 5 seeds. If all bits of addition are learned at about the same time (within a factor 1.5), I will say that the carry costs no extra training time at these sizes.

## C-01 and C-05 results (data c01/curves.csv, c01/by_runlen.csv; 20 runs: n = 6 and 8, addition and XOR, 5 seeds each; design as above)
Final state: with half the pairs for training, every network reached held-out accuracy of at least 0.996 on every output bit, for addition and for XOR, at n = 6 and 8 (n = 8 addition seed 0 had four bits at 0.996 to 0.999; all other runs 1.000 to three decimals). So with 50 percent of the data there is no generalization gap to study at these sizes.
**C-01 learning times** (median over 5 seeds of the first step where held-out accuracy of a bit is at least 0.99 and stays so at the next evaluation; one evaluation every 100 steps):
- n = 8 addition, bits 0 to 8: 100, 500, 1000, 1900, 3300, 2200, 1800, 1000, 500. The time rises from bit 0 to bit 4 (median 3,300; range over seeds 1,800 to 4,100) and then falls again for the higher bits; the top bit (the carry out) is learned at about the same time as bit 1.
- n = 6 addition, bits 0 to 6: 100, 400, 800, 1100, 1100, 600, 300 (same hump shape).
- XOR: every bit at 100 to 200 steps (median), at n = 6 and 8.
- Predictions. XOR bits within a factor 1.5 of each other in at least 4 of 5 seeds: MISSED on the literal ratio (max over min was 2.0 to 3.0 in all 5 seeds, but these are 100 versus 200 to 300 steps, i.e. one or two evaluation intervals, so the evaluation grid is too coarse to judge a ratio). Top three bits of n = 8 addition learned later than bits 0 to 2 in at least 4 of 5 seeds: MISSED (0 of 5); the top bits are learned early. The prediction that bit delay grows monotonically with bit position: MISSED; it grows to the middle bits and then shrinks.
- What it shows: addition's bits are learned much later than XOR's (bit 4 at n = 8: about 3,300 against 100 steps), and the delay peaks in the middle of the word, not at the most significant bit. Rumelhart et al. 1985 reported the overflow bit learned first (report lead, not opened); this agrees in direction.
- What it does not show: why the delay peaks in the middle. I have not tested any explanation.
**C-05 error against carry run length.** The prediction cannot be judged as specified: by step 5,000 held-out errors are 0.000 to 0.001 everywhere (no signal), and at step 2,000 each seed has different bits partway through learning, so a snapshot mixes seeds in different states. Per seed, the number of bits 4 to 8 with higher error at run length 3 or more than at run length 0 was 0.2, 0.6, 0.4, 1.0 and 0.4 of the bits (prediction asked for at least 4 of 5 seeds above 0.5: not met). XOR errors are 0 at every run length, as expected from its learning times.
Descriptive observation (made after seeing the data, so a hypothesis and not a result): pooled over bits 2 to 5 at step 2,000, n = 8 addition held-out error was 0.062 when no carry reaches the bit (run 0), 0.104 at run 1, 0.123 at run 2 and 0.123 at run 3 or more; for some seeds (e.g. seed 0, bit 2: error 0.001 at run 0, 0.49 at run 1, 0.61 at run 2 or more) the bit is right when there is no carry and wrong when there is. This suggests the network learns the carry-free case first and carry presence matters more than carry run length. To be tested on new seeds in C-06.

## C-06 plan: does the carry-free case come first, and what happens with little data? (predictions written before running, 2026-10-24)
Why: C-01 showed hump-shaped learning times and no generalization gap at 50 percent data. C-05's snapshot suggested carry presence matters more than run length (post hoc). C-06 tests that on fresh seeds and adds the missing regime (less training data).
Design: same network and optimizer. n = 8 and 10; fraction of pairs used for training 0.5, 0.2, 0.05; addition and XOR; fresh seeds 5 to 9 (not used in C-01); held-out accuracy and error by carry-run bin (0, 1, 2, 3 or more) recorded at every evaluation (every 100 steps); n = 10 held-out measured on a fixed random 50,000 of the held-out pairs. 10,000 steps.
Predictions.
(1) Hump shape holds at n = 10: the slowest bit (median over seeds, fraction 0.5) is one of bits 3 to 7, and the top bit is learned within a factor 2 of bit 1's time.
(2) At any evaluation where a bit's held-out accuracy of an addition network is between 0.60 and 0.95, error with a carry present (run 1 or more) is higher than with no carry (run 0) in at least 70 percent of such (seed, bit, evaluation) cases. If it is below 55 percent, the "carry-free case first" description is wrong.
(3) With 5 percent of the pairs, the final held-out error of addition at fixed bit rises with run length (error at run 3 or more above error at run 0 for bits 4 and up in at least 4 of 5 seeds at n = 8); XOR final held-out error stays below 0.01 at every run length. If addition networks at 5 percent still reach at least 0.99 on every bit, then the carry costs nothing at this data fraction either, and I will say so.

## C-06 results (data c06/curves.csv, c06/bins.csv; 60 runs: n = 8 and 10, training fractions 0.5, 0.2, 0.05, addition and XOR, fresh seeds 5 to 9, 10,000 steps)
Final held-out accuracy (minimum over bits, mean over seeds, [lowest seed]): n = 8 addition 0.9994 [0.9972] at 50 percent, 0.9982 [0.9954] at 20 percent, 0.9841 [0.9734] at 5 percent; XOR 1.0000 at every fraction. n = 10 addition 0.8898 [0.6507] at 50 percent, 0.9007 [0.6068] at 20 percent, 0.9868 [0.9779] at 5 percent; XOR 1.0000. The n = 10 addition runs at 50 and 20 percent were not finished at 10,000 steps in several seeds (median time to 0.99 of the slowest bits is 6,500 to 7,300 steps, near the budget), so their final accuracies reflect the step budget, not generalization; I do not compare fractions at n = 10 on final accuracy (the 5 percent runs being better than the 50 percent runs is not explained and I did not investigate it).
Predictions.
(1) Hump shape at n = 10 with fraction 0.5: HELD. Median step to 0.99, bits 0 to 10: 100, 600, 1600, 7300, 4700, 6500, 6800, 5600, 3900, 2000, 700. Slowest bit is bit 3 (within 3 to 7), and the top bit (700 steps) is within a factor 2 of bit 1 (600). n = 8: 100, 500, 1200, 3000, 1800, 3100, 1900, 1100, 500. XOR slowest median 200 steps at n = 10.
(2) Carry-free case learned first: HELD on the fresh seeds. In 2,840 (seed, bit, evaluation) cases with held-out bit accuracy between 0.60 and 0.95, error with a carry reaching the bit (run 1 or more) was above error with no carry in 80.3 percent (range over the six n and fraction groups: 75.7 to 88.6 percent; prediction asked for at least 70 percent and said below 55 percent would refute). This confirms the description made after seeing the C-01 data.
(3) With 5 percent of the pairs at n = 8, held-out error rises with carry run length: HELD weakly. Pooled error at run 3 or more against run 0, bits 4 to 8: 0.004 against 0.003, 0.026 against 0.014, 0.009 against 0.006, 0.002 against 0.002, 0.001 against 0.000. Per seed, the fraction of bits 4 to 8 with higher error at run 3 or more than at run 0 was 1.0, 0.8, 1.0, 0.2, 0.6 (4 of 5 seeds above one half). XOR error is exactly 0 at every bin. The absolute effect is small: addition error at most 2.6 percent in any bin.
**What this shows.** (a) Addition is learned in a hump order, XOR all at once. (b) While a bit is being learned, the network is right on carry-free examples and wrong on examples where a carry reaches the bit: carry PRESENCE is the first hurdle. (c) With little data, residual errors are somewhat more frequent for longer carry runs, weakly. 
**What it does not show.** Whether run length or the true dependency length is what matters (only the run length was recorded; see the update above), why the hump has this shape, or what the network computes inside. n = 10 was not trained long enough in some seeds.

## C-07 results (data c07/final_8_*_add_*.npz and xor_*.npz; n = 8, training fractions 0.05 and 0.2, addition and XOR, seeds 10 to 14, 10,000 steps)
Pooled over output bits 4 to 8, a logistic regression of held-out error (per example and bit) on bit fixed effects, carry present, carry run length and dependency length d_i.
- **Fraction 0.05** (982 to 1,860 held-out errors per seed): the log-likelihood gain from adding dependency length (on top of bit effects and carry presence) was 871, 875, 1312, 1660 and 2196 in the five seeds; the gain from adding carry run length instead was 71, 102, 155, 228 and 334; carry presence alone added between -2.7 and 37.9. Coefficient of dependency length: +0.66 to +1.07 per extra propagate position (odds of an error about 2 to 3 times higher per step) in 5 of 5 seeds; with both lengths in the model, the run-length coefficient was -0.29 to +0.03 (about zero). XOR networks made 0 errors in all seeds.
- **Fraction 0.2:** only 5 to 19 held-out errors in four of the five seeds (too few to fit); seed 14 (48 errors) points the same way (gain 33.6 for dependency length against 15.5 for run length).
- Predictions: (1) dependency length has a positive coefficient with carry presence included, at least 4 of 5 seeds at fraction 0.05: HELD (5 of 5). (2) Dependency length improves the fit more than carry run length, at least 4 of 5 seeds: HELD (5 of 5, by a factor of 5 to 13 in log-likelihood gain). (3) XOR has nothing to fit: as expected.
**What this shows.** When the network has little data, the examples it gets wrong are those where the carry into a bit depends on a long run of propagate positions; the number of consecutive carries is a worse description than that dependency length. Together with C-06 (carry presence is the first hurdle during learning), the picture is: first the carry-free case, then carries through short dependencies, and long propagate chains last.
**What it does not show.** That long dependencies are intrinsically harder and not just rarer. In random pairs a dependency of length d has probability 2^-d, so long dependencies are rare in the training set (about 3,300 training pairs at fraction 0.05); rarity and difficulty are the same variable here. A control that equalizes the number of long-dependency training examples is needed (C-08).

## C-08 results (data c08/summary.csv, c08/final_*_*.npz; n = 8, 3,276 training pairs, seeds 30 to 34, addition)
Held-out set of 20,000 random pairs per seed. "Stratified" = equal quotas per longest propagate run M (training counts per M = 0 to 8 for seed 30: stratified 179, 450, 521, 446, 412, 375, 367, 355, 171; random 10, 703, 1197, 776, 339, 129, 85, 25, 12).
- Held-out error on examples with dependency length d_i of at least 4 (bits 5 to 8), random against stratified training, seeds 30 to 34: 0.0815, 0.0363, 0.0392, 0.0254, 0.0943 against 0.0079, 0.0113, 0.0129, 0.0024, 0.0032 (3 to 30 times lower).
- Coefficient of dependency length in the logistic regression (bits 4 to 8, bit effects included): random 0.87, 0.82, 0.74, 0.67, 0.86; stratified 0.30, 0.36, 0.37, 0.21, 0.21.
- Mean accuracy over all bits: random 0.9961, stratified 0.9973 (per seed 0.9957/0.9968, 0.9983/0.9980, 0.9971/0.9958, 0.9970/0.9987, 0.9925/0.9970).
Predictions. (1) Error at d of at least 4 at most half of the random value in at least 4 of 5 seeds: HELD (5 of 5). (2) Dependency coefficient at most half in at least 4 of 5 seeds: HELD (4 of 5; seed 32 has 0.37 against 0.74, a ratio of 0.505, just above one half). (3) Total accuracy not lower with stratified training: HELD (slightly higher on average, within seed-to-seed differences).
**What this shows.** Most of the C-07 relationship between error and dependency length is a matter of how often long dependencies appear in the training set: giving long-dependency examples equal representation, at the same number of training pairs, cuts their error by a factor of 3 to 30 without hurting overall accuracy. A remaining slope persists (coefficient 0.21 to 0.37, i.e. an error odds ratio of about 1.2 to 1.45 per extra step).
**What it does not show.** Whether that remaining slope is intrinsic difficulty or an artefact of how I stratified (by the pair's longest run M, not by each bit's own dependency length), nor how the result changes at other n or training fractions. This agrees in direction with the carry-balanced sampling results reported by Lee et al. 2023 and the rare-long-carry failures of Price et al. 2016 (both unverified leads).

## Update after the second literature report (leads only, see literature.md)
- Closest predecessor in plain MLPs: Cho et al. 2019 (lead, not opened): carry COUNT orders learning time for 4-bit addition. Our additions: per-output-bit timing, per-example dependency length, matched XOR, held-out data, larger n.
- Definition fix: my "carry run length" (consecutive carry ones) can exceed the real dependency length of an output bit. From C-07 on, both are recorded: (a) carry run length, (b) dependency length = 1 + the number of consecutive propagate positions p_{i-1}, p_{i-2}, ... above the nearest generate or kill position (0 when c_i cannot depend on any lower bit). C-01 and C-06 only recorded (a).
- Direction agrees with others: the top (overflow) bit being learned early matches Rumelhart 1985 (lead) and Quirke and Barez 2024 (lead), so that part of C-01 is not surprising in hindsight; it was a missed prediction of mine.

## C-07 plan: dependency length against carry run length (predictions written before running, 2026-10-24)
Why: C-06 used the carry run (consecutive carry ones). The true dependency of carry c_i on lower bits stops at the nearest position that is not a propagate. Define d_i = number of consecutive propagate positions p_{i-1}, p_{i-2}, ... equal to 1 (0 if p_{i-1} = 0). Question: at a fixed output bit, which one tracks held-out error better, after accounting for carry presence?
Design: n = 8; training fractions 0.2 and 0.05 (the regimes with measurable errors); addition (XOR as control); fresh seeds 10 to 14; 10,000 steps; held-out error of every example and bit saved at the final step. For each seed and each bit 4 to 8, a logistic regression of error on three features: carry present (c_i = 1), carry run length, dependency length d_i. 
Predictions: (1) Dependency length has a positive coefficient (more error with longer dependency) with carry presence included, for at least 4 of 5 seeds at fraction 0.05. (2) Adding dependency length improves the fit (log-likelihood) more than adding carry run length does, in at least 4 of 5 seeds. (3) XOR: no error at all, so nothing to fit (expected). If run length wins, the carry-run description is the better one and I will say so.

## C-02 plan: can the carry be read out of the hidden layers? (predictions written before running, 2026-10-24)
Why: Naidu et al. 2025 (opened) found carry features by linear probes in transformers; Michaud et al. 2024 (opened) extracted a carry variable from RNNs. Nobody in what I opened tested plain MLPs, with an XOR network and an untrained network as controls.
Design: n = 8, 50 percent of the pairs for training (the same networks as C-01, new seeds 20 to 24), addition and XOR. At steps 0 (random initialization), 250, 500, ... , 5,000 (every 250 steps) take the activations of the three hidden layers on 20,000 random pairs, fit a logistic-regression probe for the carry into bit i (i = 1 to 8) on 10,000 of them and report its accuracy on the other 10,000. Also report probes on the raw input bits (the "inputs only" baseline) and the majority-class rate. Output-bit held-out accuracy every 250 steps from the same networks.
Predictions.
(1) At step 5,000, in trained addition networks the best layer's probe accuracy for the carry into bit i is at least 0.95 for every i from 1 to 8, in all 5 seeds.
(2) In the random-initialization network (step 0) and in a linear probe on the raw inputs, accuracy for i of at least 3 is at least 0.10 below the trained network's best layer.
(3) In trained XOR networks the best-layer probe accuracy is within 0.03 of the raw-input baseline for i of at least 3 (an XOR network has no reason to compute carries). If it is higher, a carry-like feature appears without being needed, and I will say so.
DEVIATION (compute): the first full run took 82 minutes for one network with four jobs sharing 2 CPUs, so the executed design is lighter than planned: seeds 20 to 22 (3, not 5), checkpoints every 500 steps (not 250), 10,000 probe pairs (5,000 to fit, 5,000 to test; not 20,000). The first run (seed 20, addition, old settings) is kept in c02_old_first_run/ and not used for the predictions.
(4) Timing: for each (seed, bit i), the carry probe for bit i reaches 0.95 at an earlier or equal step than the output bit i reaches 0.99 in at least 70 percent of the cases for addition (quiet-feature-like). If below 50 percent, the carry is not readable before the output is correct in these networks.

## C-08 plan: rarity or difficulty? (predictions written before running, 2026-10-24)
Design: n = 8. A fixed test set of 20,000 random pairs (same for all runs of a seed). Two training sets of equal size 3,276 (5 percent of all pairs) drawn from the remaining pairs: (a) random; (b) stratified by the longest propagate run M in the pair (M = the largest d_i over the output bits), with equal quotas per M where enough pairs exist, the remainder filled randomly. Same network, optimizer and 10,000 steps; seeds 30 to 34; addition only (XOR makes no errors).
Predictions: (1) With stratified training, held-out error on examples with dependency length d_i of at least 4 (bits 5 to 8) is at most half of the random-training value in at least 4 of 5 seeds. (2) The coefficient of dependency length in the same logistic regression falls to at most half of its random-training value in at least 4 of 5 seeds. (3) Total held-out accuracy (all bits) is not lower with stratified training. If error at long dependencies stays above half of the random-training value, long dependencies are harder, not just rarer; if it vanishes, rarity explains the C-07 slope (consistent with Price et al. 2016 and Lee et al. 2023, both leads).

## Status
C-01, C-02, C-05 to C-09 are run and recorded above. C-03 is covered by C-06 (training fraction). C-04 (smallest width) is planned below. The final report is README.md.

### C-02 results (n = 8, MLP 3x128 ReLU, Adam 1e-3, batch 256, half of the 65,536 pairs for training, 3 seeds 20-22 x {add, xor}, 5,000 steps; probe = logistic regression on 10,000 pairs, 5,000 train / 5,000 test; data c02/probes.csv, outbits.csv)

Output-bit test accuracy (add, seed average, steps 500 / 1,000 / 2,000 / 5,000): bit 0 reaches 1.0 at 500, bit 1 at 1,000; bits 2 to 5 are at 0.55 to 0.66 at step 500 and still 0.66 to 0.82 at 2,000 (bit 3 slowest, 0.687 at 2,000), all at 0.999 or above at 5,000; the high bits 7 and 8 are already 0.98 to 0.99 at step 500 (the same hump-shaped order as C-01). XOR: every bit is 1.0 by step 500.

Carry probe, best layer, add at step 5,000: 1.000 for every carry bit 1 to 8 (h1 and h2). XOR nets: 0.99 to 1.000 (h1), bit 8 only 0.981 at h2/h3 0.95.

**Against the predictions.**
1. "Best-layer probe at least 0.95 for every i at step 5,000": HELD (1.000).
2. "Untrained and raw-input probes at least 0.10 below the trained best layer for i >= 3": MISSED. The raw-input probe at step 0 already reads the carry at 1.000 (bits 1 to 5) and 0.996 to 0.998 (bits 6 to 8); an untrained network reads it at 0.90 to 0.99.
3. "XOR nets within 0.03 of the raw-input baseline": HELD, but only because the baseline is itself near 1.
4. "Probe reaches 0.95 no later than output bit reaches 0.99": HELD trivially (the probe is at 0.95 or above at step 0).
**Why prediction 2 failed (my design error, found after the result).** The carry into bit i is [low-i-bit part of a + low-i-bit part of b >= 2^i], a threshold of a weighted sum of the raw input bits, so it is linearly readable from the raw inputs themselves. A high carry-probe accuracy therefore says nothing about whether the network built a carry; the test as designed cannot tell. The 0.998 and below on high bits shows where the linear read fails: examples with the sum close to the threshold, which are the long-dependency cases.
**What this shows.** Only that the trained network's hidden layers contain linearly readable carry information, which the input already has. It does not show a carry circuit. The Naidu et al. style probe needs a baseline that removes this (see C-09).

## C-09 plan: carry probe on the hard cases only (written before running, 2026-10-24)
Idea: C-02 showed the raw-input baseline fails only where the sum is near the threshold. Score the probe on test examples with dependency length d_i >= 2 for carry bit i (these are exactly the near-threshold cases that need the whole chain) and compare hidden layers with the raw input and the untrained net. Same networks and training as C-02 (seeds 20-22), script c09_probe.py (copy of c02_probe.py plus accuracy on the hard subset).
Predictions: (1) raw-input probe accuracy on the hard subset is at least 0.03 below its overall accuracy for some i in 5..8; (2) at step 5,000 the best hidden layer of the add nets beats the raw-input probe on the hard subset by at least 0.05 for i in 5..8; (3) XOR nets are within 0.03 of the raw-input probe on the hard subset; (4) the add-net hard-subset probe for low i (<= 3) is at least 0.95 before the output bit reaches 0.99. A miss of (2) would mean the trained net holds no carry information beyond what the input has, which weakens any "builds a carry" reading.

### C-09 results (same nets and seeds as C-02; probe accuracy on test examples with dependency length d_i >= 2, about 1,200 examples per carry bit; data c09/probes.csv, outbits.csv)

| step 0 / 5,000 | carry bit 2 | 4 | 6 | 8 |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| raw-input probe, hard subset | 1.000 | 1.000 | 0.994 | 0.987 |
| majority rate in hard subset (input row) | 1.000 | 0.63 | 0.53 | 0.515 |
| add net, best layer, step 5,000 | 1.000 | 1.000 | 1.000 | 1.000 |
| xor net, best layer, step 5,000 | 1.000 | 0.995 | 0.975 | 0.964 |

**Against the predictions.** (1) Raw-input probe at least 0.03 below its overall accuracy on the hard subset for some i in 5..8: MISSED (0.987 to 0.994 vs 0.996 to 1.000 overall). (2) Add-net best layer beats the raw-input probe by at least 0.05 on the hard subset for i in 5..8: MISSED (the gain is at most 0.013; the raw input leaves almost nothing to gain). (3) XOR nets within 0.03 of the raw input on the hard subset: HELD (largest gap 0.023, bit 8). (4) Hard-subset probe at 0.95 or above before the output bit reaches 0.99 for low i: HELD, trivially (it is at or above 0.99 at step 0 from the raw input).
**What this shows.** Even on the hard cases, a linear probe of the carry cannot tell the trained network from the raw input, because the carry is a threshold function of the weighted input sum and logistic regression can fit it. In this setting, linear carry probes of the hidden layers are not evidence of a built carry. The untrained and XOR nets also retain it, only slightly less.
**What it does not show.** That the network has no carry circuit. It only shows this probe is the wrong instrument. A test that could discriminate would need a target that is not linear in the input (for example the propagate-and-carry product p_i AND c_i, or the dependency length) or a causal intervention, neither of which was run. This also means the Naidu et al. style probe result is not by itself evidence of a carry representation in an MLP like ours; whether it is for their transformers is outside what I opened.

## C-04 plan: smallest width that fits addition exactly (predictions written before running, 2026-10-25)
Question: how many hidden units does a trained ReLU MLP need to get every one of the 2^(2n) pairs right, for addition versus XOR, n = 3 to 6? This is a statement about what Adam finds, not a lower bound (no proof of minimality; theory sources are leads only).
Design: all pairs are training data (fit, not generalization). One hidden layer (depth 1) and two hidden layers of equal width (depth 2); widths 2, 3, 4, 6, 8, 12, 16, 24, 32, 64, 128; full-batch Adam lr 1e-2 constant, 20,000 steps; 5 seeds (40 to 44). Success = every output bit right on every pair. Report for each (op, n, depth, width) the number of seeds that succeed; "smallest width" = smallest width with at least 3 of 5 seeds succeeding.
Predictions. (1) At every n and depth, the smallest width for addition is at least as large as for XOR. (2) For depth 1 the smallest width for addition grows with n by at least a factor 2 from n = 3 to n = 6. (3) For XOR, depth 1, the smallest width at n = 6 is at most 2 times the one at n = 3 (XOR bits are independent 2-input parities, so the width needed per bit is constant, but the outputs share hidden units, so total width may grow; if it grows faster, I will say so). (4) Depth 2 needs a smaller or equal width than depth 1 for addition at n = 6. If a prediction fails I report it as missed.

### C-04 results (all pairs as training data, 5 seeds 40-44, widths 2 to 128, depth 1 and 2, full-batch Adam 1e-2, 20,000 steps; data c04a/runs.csv for n = 3 to 5, c04b/runs.csv for n = 6)
Smallest width on the grid with at least 3 of 5 seeds exact on all pairs:

| op, depth | n = 3 | n = 4 | n = 5 | n = 6 |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| add, depth 1 | 12 | 16 | 24 | 64 |
| add, depth 2 | 6 | 12 | 16 | 24 |
| xor, depth 1 | 6 | 8 | 12 | 16 |
| xor, depth 2 | 6 | 12 | 12 | 12 |

**Against the predictions.** (1) Addition needs at least the XOR width at every n and depth: HELD (equal in 2 of 8 cells). (2) Addition depth 1 grows by at least a factor 2 from n = 3 to 6: HELD (12 to 64, 5.3 times). (3) XOR depth 1 at n = 6 at most 2 times n = 3: MISSED (6 to 16, 2.7 times; XOR total width grows with n roughly in proportion to n). (4) Depth 2 not wider than depth 1 for addition at n = 6: HELD (24 against 64).
**What this shows.** For networks that Adam finds in this setup, exact addition needs more hidden units than XOR and the difference grows with n for one hidden layer (64 against 16 at n = 6); a second layer cuts the addition requirement by 2.7 times at n = 6 (24), consistent with a deeper network handling the carry chain more cheaply. 
**What it does not show.** A minimum: the width grid is coarse (factors of 1.3 to 2), one learning rate and step budget were used, and failures are failures of this optimizer in 20,000 steps, not proofs that a smaller network cannot represent the function. Even at width 128, depth 1, n = 6, one seed of 5 failed (4/5), so success counts also reflect optimization luck. Seeds are few (5). No theory source was opened (Alon and Bruck 1994 and Brent and Kung 1982 are unverified leads), so I make no comparison with known bounds.

## C-10 plan: probes for targets that are NOT linear in the input (written before running, 2026-10-25)
Why: C-02 and C-09 could not separate a built carry from the input, because the carry is a linear threshold of the input bits. A ripple-style solution would need hidden units for the propagate bit p_j = a_j XOR b_j (not linear in the input: a parity of two bits) and for the product p_j AND c_j (the carry passing through position j, also not linear in the input). If the trained addition network uses a carry-like computation, those should be linearly readable from its hidden layers and not from the raw input or an untrained net. If it computes the sum by another route (for example a weighted analog sum, as Primozic 2023 and Maltoni and Ferrara 2024 report for their architectures; leads, not opened), p_j AND c_j need not be readable.
Design: same networks and seeds as C-02 (n = 8, 3x128 ReLU, seeds 20-22, 5,000 steps, probe at step 0 and every 500 steps), addition and XOR; linear logistic-regression probes on 10,000 pairs (5,000 train, 5,000 test) for (a) p_{i-1} and (b) p_{i-1} AND c_{i-1}, for i = 1 to 8, on h1, h2, h3, the raw input and the untrained net (step 0). Script c10_probe.py (copy of c02_probe.py with these targets). Accuracy is compared with the majority rate of each target.
Predictions. (1) p: the XOR net's best layer reads p_{i-1} at 0.95 or above at step 5,000 for all i (it is the output); raw input at most 0.60 (a parity of two bits is not linear). (2) p AND c: the addition net's best layer beats the raw-input probe by at least 0.05 in accuracy above its own majority rate for at least 4 of the 6 bits i = 3..8 at step 5,000. (3) The XOR net does not: its p AND c probe is within 0.03 of the raw input for those bits. (4) The untrained net at step 0 is within 0.03 of the raw input for p AND c. A miss of (2) means no evidence of a ripple-style carry passing in these networks; a hit would be evidence of readable intermediate quantities but still not a causal circuit (no intervention was run).

### C-10 results (seed 20 only; add and XOR, 5,000 steps; seeds 21 and 22 not completed: the job hit its 2-hour limit under CPU sharing; data c10/probes.csv, outbits.csv)
Linear probes at step 0 and 5,000; accuracy by bit i = 1 to 8; the majority rate for "p AND c" is 1.00, 0.88, 0.82, 0.79, 0.76, 0.76, 0.75, 0.75 and 0.50 for the propagate bit.

| target, net, layer (step 5,000) | bits 3 to 8 accuracy |
|---|---|
| propagate bit p, add, h1 / h2 / h3 | 0.97-1.00 for bits 3-6 (h1), 0.80-0.82 at bits 7-8 / 0.84-1.00 / 0.74-0.94 |
| propagate bit p, XOR, h2 and h3 | 1.00 for all bits |
| p AND c, add, best layer | 0.98 to 1.00 |
| p AND c, XOR, h2 | 0.99 to 1.00 |
| p AND c, raw input | 0.75 to 0.81 (close to the majority rate) |
| p AND c, untrained net, h1 | 0.86 to 0.91 |

**Against the predictions.** (1) XOR net reads p at 0.95 or above, raw input at most 0.60: HELD (1.00 and 0.49 to 0.51). (2) Add net beats the raw input by at least 0.05 for p AND c in at least 4 of 6 bits: HELD (about 0.99 against 0.76 to 0.81). (3) XOR net does not (within 0.03 of raw input): MISSED. The XOR net's h2 reads p AND c at 0.99 to 1.00 as well. (4) Untrained net within 0.03 of the raw input: MISSED (0.86 to 0.91 against 0.75 to 0.81; a random 128-unit ReLU layer is a feature expansion, so my choice of raw input as the matching baseline was wrong).
**What this shows.** A network that has no reason to compute a carry (the XOR network) reads p AND c linearly from its second hidden layer as well as the addition network does, and even a random layer reads it much better than the raw input. So reading p AND c from a hidden layer does not show that the network uses a carry. Linear probes are the wrong instrument for the question "does it build a carry".
**What it does not show.** Anything about seeds 21 and 22 (not completed). One seed per cell, so the numbers are descriptive.

## C-11 plan: causal test, remove the carry direction from a hidden layer (written before running, 2026-10-25)
Why: C-02, C-09 and C-10 show linear probes cannot tell a built carry from rich features (the XOR network and even a random layer read the carry-related quantities too). A causal test asks whether the trained addition network USES a carry direction: project that direction out of a hidden layer and see which outputs break. This is the style of ablation reported by Naidu et al. 2025 (opened) for transformers, applied here to MLPs with matched controls.
Design. Same nets as C-02 (n = 8, 3x128 ReLU, Adam 1e-3, batch 256, half of the pairs for training, 5,000 steps), retrained and saved: addition and XOR, seeds 20 to 22. For each hidden layer L = 1, 2, 3 (post-ReLU activations) and each carry bit i = 2 to 8: fit a logistic-regression probe for c_i on 5,000 training pairs; take its weight vector as the direction w; replace h by h - (h . w_unit) w_unit and run the rest of the network. Controls with the same procedure: (a) the probe direction for the input bit a_{i-1} (a linearly readable quantity that is not a carry); (b) a random unit direction (mean over 20). Measure on the 32,768 held-out pairs the accuracy of every output bit, and for sum bit i separately on examples with c_i = 1 and c_i = 0.
Predictions. (1) In the addition net, ablating the carry direction at its best layer lowers the accuracy of sum bit i by at least 0.05 more than the random-direction mean, for at least 4 of the 7 bits i = 2..8 (averaged over seeds). (2) Where (1) holds, the drop is concentrated where the carry is present: drop on c_i = 1 examples at least 2 times the drop on c_i = 0 examples. (3) The carry direction ablation beats the input-bit-direction control by at least 0.03 on average over those bits. (4) In the XOR net, ablating the carry direction changes no output bit's accuracy by more than 0.02. Any of these failing is reported as missed; if (1) fails, a single linear carry direction is not needed for the output in these networks (the computation may be distributed or use other directions), which would not show that there is no carry computation.

### C-11 results (ablate the probe direction of c_i, i = 2 to 8, in hidden layer 1, 2 or 3; addition and XOR nets, seeds 20 to 22; all 6 nets finished; data c11/ablation.csv; mean over seeds; "drop" = loss of accuracy of sum bit i on the 32,768 held-out pairs)
Note: output bit 8 of the sum IS the carry c_8, so its row is trivially affected by removing the c_8 direction; I do not count i = 8 as evidence for layers 2 and 3.

| drop in sum bit i, addition net | i=2 | 3 | 4 | 5 | 6 | 7 |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| layer 1, carry direction | 0.140 | 0.020 | 0.030 | 0.148 | 0.417 | 0.332 |
| layer 1, input-bit direction (control) | 0.044 | 0.009 | 0.027 | 0.015 | 0.145 | 0.012 |
| layer 1, random direction (control, mean of 20) | 0.020 | 0.034 | 0.111 | 0.057 | 0.022 | 0.016 |
| layers 2 and 3, carry direction | 0.000 to 0.015 for all i = 2 to 7 | | | | | |

At layer 1, on carry-present examples (c_i = 1) the drop is 0.245, 0.036, 0.061, 0.277, 0.676, 0.607 for i = 2..7 against 0.077, 0.008, 0.003, 0.026, 0.166, 0.061 on carry-absent examples. In the XOR nets, ablating the carry direction at layer 1 also breaks output bits: the largest single drops are 0.70 (seed 20, i = 5, output bit 4), 0.68, 0.67, 0.59; at layers 2 and 3 no XOR output bit changes. Random directions change XOR outputs by at most 0.004.

**Against the predictions.** (1) Carry-direction ablation lowers sum bit i by at least 0.05 more than random for at least 4 of 7 bits: HELD at layer 1 only (i = 2, 5, 6, 7, plus the trivial i = 8; not i = 3, 4); MISSED at layers 2 and 3 (only the trivial i = 8). (2) Drop at least 2 times larger on carry-present examples where (1) holds: HELD (ratios 3 to 20 at layer 1). (3) Beats the input-bit control by at least 0.03 on average: HELD at layer 1 (by 0.10, 0.13, 0.27, 0.32 for i = 2, 5, 6, 7). (4) XOR net: no output bit changes by more than 0.02: MISSED at layer 1 (drops up to 0.70).
**What this shows.** In the addition network a direction in the first hidden layer, found by a carry probe, carries information the sum bits need, most strongly the high bits and where a carry is present, and it matters far more than a random or input-bit direction. But the same procedure breaks the XOR network's outputs at layer 1 too. So this direction is not specific to carrying: it is probably a large-weight input direction (carry is a weighted-sum threshold of the input), which both networks use. In layers 2 and 3 no single direction is needed for the sum bits, so the information there is spread over several directions.
**What it does not show.** A carry circuit. The test cannot separate "the network uses a carry" from "the network uses a weighted analog sum of the low bits", which would produce the same direction (Primozic 2023 and Maltoni and Ferrara 2024 describe such a scheme for their models; leads, not opened). Single-direction ablation also can miss a distributed carry. Three seeds, and the per-seed spread is large (for example i = 6: 0.53, 0.24, 0.48).
**Conclusion for the project question "does the network build a carry chain?".** Four instruments (C-02, C-09, C-10, C-11) were tried. None separates a carry-style computation from a weighted-sum computation in these MLPs. The question stays open here; what is established is the learning-order and error-structure results above.

Exploratory look after C-11 (post hoc, no prediction, not a result): in the saved addition nets (seeds 20 to 22) the first-layer weights of single units do not show a clean "weight grows with bit position" (powers of two) pattern: mean correlation of log|weight| with bit position is -0.02 to 0.06, and a-weights and b-weights of a unit correlate only 0.1 to 0.23. So a simple per-unit analog-sum structure is not visible this way; a distributed one is not excluded. Not analysed further.

# Phase 2: answering the open questions rigorously (protocol written 2026-10-25, before any of the work below)

Rules for every item from here on:
1. Prediction and pass/fail rule are written in this file before the run. Each item is marked CONFIRMATORY (rule fixed in advance) or EXPLORATORY (looked at after the data; never used as a result, only to design the next confirmatory item).
2. At least 10 seeds for a confirmatory training experiment where compute allows; fewer is stated in the result. Intervals are bootstrap 95 percent over seeds (or over examples where the seed is fixed); only intervals, not single means, are compared.
3. A positive claim needs a control that could have shown the opposite (a null network, a shuffled label, an alternative explanation tested in the same run).
4. If two explanations make the same prediction on the measured quantity, I say the question is not identifiable from that quantity and design a measurement where they differ; I do not pick one.
5. Missed predictions and failed controls are reported in the same table as hits.

Open questions being attacked (from the list given to the owner): A-Q1 why the hump; A-Q2 carry chain or weighted sum; A-Q3 is the residual slope after balancing real; A-Q4 does it hold at larger n and other optimizers. XOR project: B-Q1 functional form of the delay; B-Q2 the 64,000-step worsening; B-Q3 another leakage model.

## R-1 (CONFIRMATORY, no training): is "carry chain" distinguishable from "weighted sum" by error structure at all?
The carry into bit i is [S_i >= 2^i] with S_i = (low i bits of a) + (low i bits of b). Define the margin m_i = S_i - (2^i - 1/2). Claim to check: if the dependency length d_i (consecutive propagate positions p_{i-1}, p_{i-2}, ... equal to 1) is at least k, then |m_i| < 2^(i-k) (by writing S_i = 2^i - 2^(i-k) + S_lower).
Checks on all 65,536 pairs, n = 8, bits i = 2..8. Predictions: (1) the inequality holds for all pairs (pass: 0 violations). (2) Spearman correlation between d_i and -log2(|m_i|+1) is at least 0.8 for i >= 5 (pooled over pairs). (3) Consequence if (1) and (2) hold: an error-versus-dependency-length analysis (C-07, C-08) cannot separate a ripple-carry computation from a weighted-sum-threshold computation, because a threshold network fails exactly near the margin. So the question must be answered with a different measurement (see R-5).

## R-2 (CONFIRMATORY, uses saved curves): is the hump an artefact of the 0.99 accuracy threshold?
Data: c01/curves.csv (n = 6, 8; 5 seeds) and c06/curves.csv (n = 8, 10 at fraction 0.5, seeds 5 to 9). For thresholds 0.90, 0.95, 0.99 and 0.999, learning time of a bit = first step at which held-out accuracy >= threshold and stays so at the next evaluation (runs that never reach it count as the maximum step, and the number is reported). Prediction: at every threshold, for n = 8 and 10, the slowest bit (median over seeds) is among bits 3 to 6 and the top bit's time is at most half the slowest bit's time. If it fails at some threshold, the hump is partly a threshold artefact and I report where it breaks.

### R-1 result (all 65,536 pairs, n = 8)
(1) Violations of "d_i >= k implies |m_i| < 2^(i-k)": 0 of all checked pairs (bits 2 to 8, all k): HELD. This is also a short proof (S_i = 2^i - 2^(i-k) + S_lower with S_lower in [0, 2^(i-k+1) - 2]).
(2) Spearman correlation of d_i with -log2(|m_i|+1): 0.735, 0.784, 0.797, 0.801, 0.802, 0.802, 0.802 for i = 2..8: HELD for i >= 5 but only just (0.80); the correlation is capped because many pairs have d_i = 0 with very different margins.
**What this shows.** A long propagate chain forces the weighted sum of the low bits to sit very close to the carry threshold (within 2^(i-k)), so error against dependency length and error against distance from the threshold are strongly tied. The one-directional implication is proven; the converse (near threshold implies long chain) is not exact. C-07 and C-08 therefore cannot separate "the network computes a chain" from "the network computes a thresholded weighted sum that fails near the threshold". That is a limit of the measurement, not a finding about the networks.
**What it does not show.** That the converse never helps: I did not run a fit that uses the margin as a separate predictor, because the saved C-07 and C-08 files do not contain the inputs (a, b). The separating measurement is planned in R-5.

### R-2 result (threshold sensitivity of the hump; medians over seeds; data c01/curves.csv, c06/curves.csv)
Slowest bit and (top bit time) / (slowest bit time), n = 8 and 10 from C-06 (fraction 0.5, 5 seeds), n = 6 and 8 from C-01 (5 seeds):
| thresholds 0.90 / 0.95 / 0.99 / 0.999 | slowest bit | top / slowest |
|---|---|---|
| C-01, n = 6 | 3 / 3 / 3 / 4 | 0.12 / 0.11 / 0.27 / 0.32 |
| C-01, n = 8 | 4 / 4 / 4 / 4 | 0.04 / 0.04 / 0.15 / 0.32 |
| C-06, n = 8 | 3 / 3 / 5 / 5 | 0.04 / 0.04 / 0.16 / 0.22 |
| C-06, n = 10 | 3 / 3 / 7 / 5 | 0.01 / 0.01 / 0.08 / 0.38 |
**Against the prediction.** Slowest bit among bits 3 to 6 in all settings except C-06 n = 10 at threshold 0.99 (bit 7; 5 of the bit-times never reached the threshold within 10,000 steps and are counted as the budget, so this setting is censored); top bit at most half of the slowest: HELD in all 16 settings. So the hump is not an artefact of the 0.99 threshold: it is present at 0.90 to 0.999 and at n = 6, 8, 10; the n = 10 position at 0.99 and 0.999 is uncertain because of censoring (16 of the bit-times at 0.999 never reached the threshold).

## R-5 / C-13 plan (CONFIRMATORY): chain or thresholded weighted sum? A measurement where they differ (written before running, 2026-10-25)
R-1 showed that a long propagate chain implies a small margin, so error against chain length cannot separate the two ideas. They do differ on examples where the carry is decided at once by the top position but the weighted sum is close to the threshold: a generate (a_{i-1} = b_{i-1} = 1) or kill (a_{i-1} = b_{i-1} = 0) at the top position gives d_i = 0, yet the low-bit weighted sum S_i can lie within 2^(i-2) of the threshold 2^i. A ripple/lookahead computation sees the generate or kill and is done; a network that thresholds a weighted sum is exposed to the margin.
Design. n = 8, random 5 percent of the pairs for training (3,276), MLP 3x128 ReLU, Adam 1e-3, batch 256, 10,000 steps (same as C-08), 10 fresh seeds (100 to 109), a held-out set of 20,000 random pairs per seed, addition only. Save per-example errors and the held-out indices. For each output bit i = 5 to 8 and each seed, define sets with d_i = 0: SMALL = |m_i| <= 2^(i-2); LARGE = |m_i| > 2^(i-1). Compare the error rate of output bit i on SMALL and LARGE.
Pass rules (fixed now). Threshold-like: error(SMALL) / error(LARGE) >= 2 AND error(SMALL) >= 0.01, pooled over bits 5 to 8, in at least 8 of 10 seeds. Chain-like: the ratio is below 1.5 in at least 8 of 10 seeds. Otherwise: mixed or inconclusive. Prediction (mine, before running): threshold-like. Control: the same split on XOR targets (a network trained on XOR) must give error 0 in both sets, as a check that the sets themselves do not create errors.
Limits to state: the network is trained on a random 5 percent, so errors measure generalisation from a sparse sample; it says nothing about a network trained on all pairs.

### R-5 / C-13 result (n = 8, random 5% training, 10 seeds 100 to 109, 10,000 steps, 20,000 held-out pairs per seed; bits 5 to 8 pooled; sets with d_i = 0; data c13a/, c13b/ (errors, held-out indices))
Error rate of the output bit on SMALL (|m_i| <= 2^(i-2), about 5,000 held-out examples per seed) and LARGE (|m_i| > 2^(i-1), about 20,000):

| seed | SMALL | LARGE | ratio |
|---|---|---|---|
| 100 | 0.0092 | 0.0002 | 37 |
| 101 | 0.0050 | 0.0006 | 8.4 |
| 102 | 0.0072 | 0.0017 | 4.4 |
| 103 | 0.0016 | 0.0000 | 32 |
| 104 | 0.0020 | 0.0011 | 1.8 |
| 105 | 0.0055 | 0.0008 | 6.9 |
| 106 | 0.0059 | 0.0003 | 17 |
| 107 | 0.0077 | 0.0006 | 13 |
| 108 | 0.0195 | 0.0023 | 8.5 |
| 109 | 0.0034 | 0.0007 | 5.2 |

**Against the pre-registered rule.** Threshold-like required ratio >= 2 AND error(SMALL) >= 0.01 in at least 8 of 10 seeds: the ratio condition holds in 9 of 10 seeds, but error(SMALL) is at least 0.01 in only 1 of 10 (seed 108): the rule is NOT met. Chain-like required ratio < 1.5 in at least 8 of 10: 0 of 10. So by my own rule the outcome is "mixed or inconclusive". My prediction ("threshold-like") is therefore only half supported: the direction is as predicted (errors are 1.8 to 37 times higher near the threshold, in every seed), but the errors are small in absolute terms (0.2 to 2 percent), below the 1 percent level I set.
**What this shows.** For examples where the carry is decided by the top position (a generate or a kill), the trained networks still err more often when the low-bit sum is close to the threshold. A pure ripple or lookahead computation would not depend on that distance; so the network is not computing a clean chain. It does not show that the network is a thresholded weighted sum either: the effect is small in absolute terms and the pre-registered strength criterion failed.
**What it does not show / confounds.** (1) The SMALL set consists of pairs whose low bits are extreme (generate with both low parts small, or kill with both large): these are atypical input patterns, so more errors could come from rarity of the input pattern and not from the distance to a threshold. The data I saved cannot separate those; a follow-up with sets matched on the low-bit patterns is needed. (2) The XOR control (same split on a network trained on XOR, expected error 0) has not been run yet; without it the sets themselves are not shown to be neutral. (3) One architecture, one training-set size.

# Verification of the outside evidence bundle (2026-10-25; checks written before running; my own code, not theirs)
V-1 (CONFIRMATORY, no training): the 3n-1 unit construction. Build the one-hidden-layer ReLU network from the bundle's description (t_i = ReLU(a_i + b_i) for i = 0..n-1; c_1 = ReLU(S_1 - 1); for i >= 2, ReLU(S_i - 2^i + 1) and ReLU(S_i - 2^i); y_i = t_i + c_i - 2 c_{i+1}, y_n = c_n; S_i = sum_{j<i} 2^j (a_j + b_j)) and test every input pair for n = 3 to 8 and 10. Pass: zero mismatches. If it passes, my C-04 widths (64 at n = 6) are not minimum widths and must be described only as what Adam found; I will also state the construction size next to them.
V-2 (CONFIRMATORY, no training): Walsh spectrum. Compute exhaustively at n = 6 the squared-Walsh mass by degree for each ordinary sum bit and the overflow and compare to the bundle's recurrence. Pass: max absolute difference < 1e-9.
V-3 (CONFIRMATORY, my data): the bundle's post-hoc statistic. R2_i = ((1-4^-n) 4^-i + 4^-(n-i) - 2 4^-n) / (1 - 2 4^-n) for ordinary bit i. Prediction rule: Spearman correlation between 1/R2_i and my median 0.99-times (c01 n = 8, c06 n = 8, c06 n = 10, ordinary bits 0..n-1) is at least 0.8 in all three, and exceeds that of mean degree (4 - 2^(1-i)) in all three. This tests their statistic on data it was not built from. If it fails in any, the statistic is a fit to their runs.
V-4 (CONFIRMATORY, C-13 data): margin-matched comparison. Within bins of the margin |m_i| (bins (0,1], (1,2], (2,4], ...), compare the error of output bit i (i = 5..8) between examples with d_i = 0 and d_i >= 3, using bins with at least 50 examples in both groups. A chain computation predicts more errors at d >= 3 at equal margin; a pure threshold predicts equal. Rule: pooled over the usable bins, error(d>=3)/error(d=0) >= 2 in at least 8 of 10 seeds: chain-dependent; ratio between 0.67 and 1.5 in at least 8 of 10: threshold-like; otherwise mixed. My prediction: chain-dependent (the C-07/C-08 slope after balancing).

### V-1 to V-4 results
V-1 (3n - 1 unit construction): zero mismatches for every input pair at n = 3, 4, 5, 6, 8, 10 (units 8, 11, 14, 17, 23, 29; all outputs integral to 1e-9): PASS. So the bundle's claim holds, and an exact one-hidden-layer ReLU network for n = 6 has 17 units. My C-04 widths (64 at n = 6, one layer) are what Adam found in 20,000 steps, not necessary widths. I should not describe the "addition needs more width than XOR" result as a representation fact; the bundle also gives a 2n-unit XOR construction, so the representation gap is 3n - 1 against 2n, much smaller than the measured 64 against 16.
V-2 (Walsh recurrence, exhaustive n = 6): max absolute difference 0: PASS.
V-3 (their post-hoc statistic on my data): Spearman of 1/R2 with my median 0.99-times: c01 n = 8: 0.898; c06 n = 8: 0.762; c06 n = 10: 0.709 (n = 6, c01: 0.841); mean degree: 0.539, 0.524, 0.491. Rule (at least 0.8 in all three, above mean degree in all three): FAILED on the first part (1 of 3), passed on the second (3 of 3). So the statistic ranks better than mean degree everywhere but it is not a stable predictor: the peak bit it predicts (4, 4, 5) differs from the measured one (4, 5, 7) at n = 8 (c06) and n = 10, and the n = 10 times are partly censored.
V-4 (margin-matched comparison, C-13 data): error ratio (d >= 3) / (d = 0) at equal margin, per seed 100 to 109: 0.36, 0.61, 0.43, 0.48, 2.27, 1.37, 0.27, 0.67, 0.74, 0.84 (median 0.64). Chain-dependent (ratio >= 2) in 1 of 10 seeds; threshold-like (ratio 0.67 to 1.5) in 4 of 10; so by the pre-registered rule: mixed. My prediction (chain-dependent) was MISSED. What the numbers say: at the same margin, long chains do NOT err more than generate/kill cases; 9 of 10 seeds have a ratio below 1.5 and 7 below 0.75. The error follows the margin, and the apparent dependency-length slope in C-07 and C-08 is, as far as this goes, a margin effect. What they do not say: margin bins are factors of 2 wide, so within a bin the chain-length groups can differ in margin; the d = 0 group erring more than d >= 3 in 7 seeds may come from the unusual low-bit patterns of generate/kill cases with a small margin (the confound noted for R-5).

## R-8 (CONFIRMATORY for the model behaviours; the trained-network numbers were already seen): what do the two idealised implementations look like under the C-13 and V-4 metrics?
Models on all 65,536 pairs, n = 8: M1 = thresholded weighted sum with noise: carry c_i estimated as 1[S_i + N(0, sigma^2) >= 2^i - 1/2], sum bit i = p_i XOR c_i estimate, top bit = c_n estimate (noise independent per example and bit; sigma chosen so that the overall bit error is about 0.5 percent, as in the trained nets); M2 = truncated ripple: c_i computed from the top K = 3 positions only (c_{i-3} assumed 0).
Metrics: SMALL/LARGE error ratio (R-5 definition, d_i = 0, bits 5 to 8) and the V-4 ratio (d >= 3 over d = 0 at equal margin).
Predictions: M1: SMALL/LARGE >= 5 and V-4 ratio between 0.67 and 1.5. M2: SMALL error = 0 (a top generate or kill is correct) and V-4 ratio >= 2. Reading rule for trained nets (fixed now): "closer to M1" if SMALL/LARGE >= 2 in at least 9 of 10 seeds and V-4 ratio < 1.5 in at least 8 of 10; "closer to M2" if SMALL error < 0.005 in at least 8 of 10 seeds and V-4 ratio >= 2 in at least 8 of 10; otherwise neither.

### R-8 result (all 65,536 pairs, n = 8; bits 5 to 8 pooled; overall error = mean over all 9 output bits)
| model | overall error | SMALL error | LARGE error | V-4 ratio (d >= 3 over d = 0 at equal margin) |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| M1 threshold, sigma 0.5 | 0.029 | 0.0007 | 0.0000 | 16.9 |
| M1, sigma 1 | 0.064 | 0.0026 | 0.0000 | 7.0 |
| M1, sigma 2 | 0.109 | 0.0107 | 0.0000 | 3.2 |
| M1, sigma 4 | 0.157 | 0.0394 | 0.0000 | 1.8 |
| M2 truncated ripple, K = 2 / 3 / 4 | 0.070 / 0.028 / 0.011 | 0 | 0 | infinite (d = 0 has no error) |
| trained MLPs (C-13, 10 seeds) | about 0.004 | 0.002 to 0.020 | 0.000 to 0.002 | median 0.64 (0.27 to 2.27) |
**Against the predictions.** M1: SMALL/LARGE >= 5: HELD (LARGE error is 0). M1: V-4 ratio between 0.67 and 1.5: MISSED (1.8 to 17; the V-4 ratio exceeds 1 for the threshold model because, inside a factor-2 margin bin, long chains have the smaller margins: d >= k forces |m| < 2^(i-k)). M2: SMALL error = 0 and V-4 ratio >= 2: HELD. My reading rule for the trained nets (V-4 < 1.5 means threshold-like) was based on the missed M1 prediction, so it is void.
**What this shows.** The V-4 ratio of the trained networks (median 0.64: at equal margin, short-chain examples err MORE than long-chain ones) is below what either idealisation produces (M1: 1.8 to 17; M2: infinite). On SMALL versus LARGE the networks resemble M1 in direction (more errors near the threshold, few far from it). So neither "ripple/lookahead chain" nor "thresholded weighted sum with noise" reproduces the error structure of the trained networks, and the chain-versus-sum dichotomy is not a good description of what these networks do on held-out pairs. **What it does not show.** What they do instead. A candidate (untested) is coverage: the SMALL and (d = 0) small-margin examples have atypical low-bit patterns that the 5 percent training sample covers poorly. Next check: R-9.

## R-9 (CONFIRMATORY): is the SMALL-set excess explained by how close the example is to a training example?
Regenerate each seed's training set (default_rng(seed); allidx = permutation; te = first 20,000; pool = rest; training = permutation(pool)[:3276], exactly as in adder_learn5.py). For each held-out pair compute its Hamming distance (over the 16 input bits) to the nearest training pair. Fit, pooled over bits 5 to 8 and over d_i = 0 examples, a logistic regression of the error on (bit effects, SMALL indicator) and then on (bit effects, SMALL indicator, nearest-training distance). Prediction (fixed now): the SMALL coefficient shrinks by at least 50 percent once the distance is added, in at least 7 of 10 seeds. If it does not, coverage in this sense does not explain the SMALL-set excess.

### R-9 result (10 seeds; d_i = 0 examples with SMALL or LARGE margin, bits 5 to 8 pooled)
Coefficient of the SMALL indicator in the logistic regression of the error, without and with the nearest-training Hamming distance: seed 100: 3.68 -> 3.47; 101: 2.18 -> 2.17; 102: 1.56 -> 1.51; 103: 2.30 -> 3.15; 104: 0.74 -> 0.75; 105: 1.94 -> 1.91; 106: 2.36 -> 2.83; 107: 2.56 -> 2.52; 108: 2.14 -> 2.12; 109: 1.72 -> 1.77. The correlation between SMALL and the nearest-training distance is about 0 (-0.01 to 0.01) in every seed.
**Against the prediction.** The coefficient shrinks by at least 50 percent in at least 7 of 10 seeds: MISSED (0 of 10). **What this shows.** In this sense (Hamming distance to the nearest training pair over the 16 input bits) the SMALL examples are not less covered by the training set than the LARGE ones, and the excess of errors near the threshold is not explained by it. **What it does not show.** That no coverage-type explanation exists: Hamming distance on the input bits is one crude notion; a distance in the weighted-sum value (the quantity the network may use) or in the top-bit pattern was not tried.
**Current reading of the question "chain or weighted sum?"** Evidence so far: errors depend on the margin (R-5) and not on the chain length at equal margin (V-4); neither idealisation reproduces the V-4 pattern (R-8); a Hamming-coverage explanation fails (R-9). The question remains open, and what has changed is that the dichotomy as posed does not describe the trained networks well. The XOR control for C-13 (expected zero error in both sets) has not been run.

### C-13 XOR control (seeds 100 to 102, same 5% random training, 10,000 steps)
All three XOR networks have 0 errors on all 20,000 held-out pairs and 8 output bits (0 of 160,000 bit predictions each), so the SMALL and LARGE sets (bits 5 to 7 checked) show error 0 in both: PASS as the control. The near-threshold excess in the addition networks is therefore not produced by the sets themselves or by the input patterns alone (the same input patterns give no errors when the target is XOR). Limit: 3 seeds; the control shows the split is neutral for a target without carries, not why the addition networks err there.

# Phase 3 plan (2026-10-25): attacking what is still unknown. Predictions written before each run.

## C-14 (CONFIRMATORY): does the hump hold at larger n and with momentum SGD, with strictly held-out evaluation and long enough runs?
Prompted by the outside chat's own audit (its n = 12, 16 runs were censored at 5,000 steps and its 8,192-pair evaluation panel overlapped with training).
Design. n = 12 and 16, MLP 3x128 ReLU, summed BCE, batch 256, 20,000 steps; Adam lr 1e-3 and momentum SGD lr 0.01 (momentum 0.9); fresh uniform pairs each step with rejection of any pair (and its swap) that is in the evaluation panel; evaluation panel = 8,192 distinct random ordered pairs (fixed per n); accuracy of every output bit every 200 steps; bit time = first evaluated step with accuracy >= 0.99 and still >= 0.99 at the next evaluation, otherwise censored at 20,000; XOR targets as control; 10 seeds (0 to 9). Script c14_scale.py.
Predictions. (1) For Adam and SGD at both n, the median slowest bit among uncensored+censored values is a middle bit (index between n/4 and 3n/4) and the median time of the top (carry-out) bit is at most half the slowest bit's median. (2) Adam at n = 12: at most 20 percent of the addition bit-times are censored at 20,000 steps. (3) XOR: every bit reaches 0.99 within 2,000 steps in at least 90 percent of (seed, bit) cases for Adam at both n. Misses are reported as misses; censoring is shown with every table.

## C-12 (CONFIRMATORY): is the hump intrinsic to each bit or caused by training the bits together?
Design. n = 8, random 50 percent of the pairs for training (as C-01), each of the 9 sum bits trained alone in its own network (one output), same MLP, Adam 1e-3, batch 256, 10,000 steps, 3 seeds (0 to 2), held-out accuracy every 100 steps, same bit-time rule; compared with the joint C-01 medians for n = 8 (100, 500, 1000, 1900, 3300, 2200, 1800, 1000, 500). Script c12_single.py.
Predictions. (1) If the hump is intrinsic: the slowest single-output bit is among bits 3 to 6 and Spearman correlation between single-output median times and the C-01 medians over the 9 bits is at least 0.7. (2) If it is a joint-training effect: single-output times are flat or monotone in the bit position (Spearman with C-01 below 0.4). My prediction: intrinsic (1). The outcome decides which of the explanations (shared features or gradient competition between bits, versus the difficulty of each bit's function) is worth testing next.

### C-14 result (10 seeds each; XOR n=12 had 7 of 10 seeds when scored, final line below if updated). Script c14_scale.py, analysis c14_analyze.py, data c14/.
Median bit-time (steps, censored = 20000), bits 0..n:
- Adam n=12: 200 700 2600 20000 20000 17800 15200 11600 8600 10900 5500 2600 1100. Censored 12.3% of cells (bits 3,4,5 in 6,6,3 of 10 seeds).
- Adam n=16: 200 1100 5400 then 20000 for bits 3..11, 13700 12200 8100 5000 1500. Censored 52.9%.
- SGD n=12: 800 2300 10900 11600 11200 10100 9000 8000 7600 7100 4400 3100 1800. Censored 0%.
- SGD n=16: 1100 3800 then 20000 for bits 2..8, 18800 15000 12300 8600 7400 5300 3400 2300. Censored 42.4%.
- XOR Adam: all bits at 200-700 steps (n=16: 96.9% of cells within 2000; n=12: 100% of 7 seeds).
Scoring of the predictions written beforehand:
1. "Slowest bit index in n/4..3n/4 and top bit at most half the slowest": top-bit part PASSES in all four addition settings (ratios 0.06, 0.08, 0.16, 0.12). The index part passes literally at n=12 (bit 3 = n/4) for both optimizers and FAILS literally at n=16 (argmax = bit 3 for Adam, 2 for SGD, both below n/4 = 4). Caveat: at n=16 most of the middle bits are censored at the same 20000, so the argmax is a tie broken by position and "slowest bit" is not identified; the censored plateau (Adam bits 3..11, SGD bits 2..8) lies mostly in the middle.
2. Adam n=12 censored at most 20%: PASS (12.3%).
3. XOR within 2000 steps in at least 90% of cells: PASS at n=16 (96.9%); n=12 100% of the seeds scored.
What this supports: the qualitative picture (low bits fast, top bit fast, XOR fast for all bits) holds at n=12 and n=16 on strictly held-out pairs, for Adam and momentum SGD. What it does not support: the location of the hump peak at n=16, because more than 40% of addition cells hit the step budget. At n=12 with SGD (no censoring) the profile is a plateau from bit 2 to 5 followed by steady decline, flatter than the Adam hump.

### C-12 result (3 seeds, n=8, 50% of pairs for training, one single-output net per bit; script c12_single.py, data c12/single_n8.csv, log c12.log).
Median steps to 0.99 held-out accuracy, bits 0..8: 100, 200, 200, 200, 300, 400, 600, 800, 700 (every run reached 0.99; seeds agree within one or two grid steps). Joint C-01 medians: 100, 500, 1000, 1900, 3300, 2200, 1800, 1000, 500.
Prediction written beforehand: hump intrinsic (slowest single bit among 3 to 6 and Spearman with joint >= 0.7). MISSED on both counts: slowest single-output bit is 7 (800 steps), and Spearman with the joint medians is 0.23 (p = 0.55, 9 points). The pre-stated alternative (Spearman below 0.4, joint-training effect) holds, except that the single-output times are not flat: they rise with position up to bit 7 and then dip slightly at bit 8.
Reading: the middle-bit hump in C-01 is largely absent when a bit is trained alone; middle bits are 5 to 17 times faster alone (bit 4: 300 vs 3300). Whatever causes the delay in the joint net, it is not the difficulty of the single bit function alone. Caveats: 3 seeds, n=8 only, one learning rate and architecture; the single runs stop as soon as the criterion is met (no later accuracy tracked); the joint and single runs sample batches differently (joint C-01 setup vs with-replacement batches here), so the comparison is of orders of magnitude, not of exact ratios. Not established: whether the cause is gradient interference between bits, shared-feature competition, or the summed loss; that needs a test (e.g. train pairs of bits, or reweight the joint loss).

## C-15 (CONFIRMATORY, follows C-12): which co-trained bits slow down bit 4?
Idea source: C-12 (bit 4 alone 300 steps vs 3300 in the all-bits net).
Design. n=8, same setup as C-12 (50% of pairs, 3x128 ReLU, Adam 1e-3, batch 256, summed BCE over the chosen output bits, 10,000 step cap, 5 seeds 0-4). Output sets: {4}; {4,0}; {4,8}; {3,4,5}; {2,3,4,5,6}; {0..8}. Measure time-to-0.99 of bit 4 only. Script c15_subsets.py.
Predictions. (1) Time of bit 4 rises with set size: median({4}) < median({3,4,5}) < median({2..6}) < median(all) (all three strict inequalities). (2) {3,4,5} is slower than {4,0} and {4,8} (nearby bits hurt more than far ones). (3) the all-bits median reproduces C-01 (within 2x of 3300). Misses reported.

### C-15 result (5 seeds; c15_subsets.py, c15/subsets_n8.csv). Median steps for bit 4 to reach 0.99 held-out, by co-trained output set:
{4}: 300 | {4,0}: 500 | {4,8}: 700 | {3,4,5}: 300 | {2,3,4,5,6}: 400 | all 9 bits: 2600 (runs 4800, 4000, 900, 2500, 2600).
Predictions: (1) strict increase {4} < {3,4,5} < {2..6} < all: MISSED ({4} and {3,4,5} tie at 300; the rest increase). (2) neighbours hurt more than far bits: MISSED, reversed ({3,4,5} 300 vs {4,0} 500 and {4,8} 700). (3) all-bits median within 2x of C-01's 3300: PASS (2600).
Reading: co-training with the neighbouring bits 2 to 6 barely slows bit 4 (300 to 400); a single far bit (0 or 8) slows it a little (500, 700); the large slowdown (about 9x) appears only when all nine bits are present, so it comes from the far bits 1, 7 and the full set, not the near ones. Caveat: 5 seeds, one target bit, n=8; sets were not designed to separate "which far bit" (bits 0,1,7,8 each or in combination were not tested individually beyond 0 and 8), and the all-bits runs vary widely (900 to 4800). Mechanism (gradient interference, capacity, shared features) not tested.
Next test: add far bits one at a time and in pairs ({2..6}+0, +8, +0+8, +1, +7, {1..7}) to find which combination triggers the slowdown.

(C-14 final line, XOR Adam n=12 with all 10 seeds: see c14_analyze.py output; prediction 3 judged on it.)

## C-16 (CONFIRMATORY, follows C-15): which far bits trigger the bit-4 slowdown?
Design as C-15 (bit 4 target, 5 seeds, n=8). Sets: {2-6}+{0}; {2-6}+{8}; {2-6}+{0,8}; {2-6}+{1}; {2-6}+{7}; {1..7} (= {2-6}+{1,7}); {0..8} (control). Script c15_subsets.py with env SETS, output c16/.
Predictions. (1) No single added far bit (0, 8, 1, 7) pushes the median above 800 steps. (2) {2-6}+{0,8} has median at most 1000. (3) {1..7} median at least 1500 (slowdown needs many bits). (4) the control {0..8} median at least 1500. If (1)+(2) hold and (3) fails, the effect is not additive in far bits and needs a count-of-bits test.

(Design correction made before running: {2-6}+{1,7} equals {1..7}, so it is listed once; 7 sets in total.)

### C-16 result (5 seeds; c16/subsets_n8.csv). Median steps for bit 4 to reach 0.99, with bits 2-6 plus:
+{0}: 1000 | +{8}: 1100 | +{0,8}: 1100 | +{1}: 600 | +{7}: 500 | +{1,7} (= bits 1..7): 800 | all 9 bits: 2600 (the all-9 runs are the same seeds as C-15, so it is a repeat, not an independent check).
Predictions: (1) no single added far bit above 800: MISSED (bit 0 gives 1000, bit 8 gives 1100; bits 1 and 7 pass). (2) {2-6}+{0,8} at most 1000: MISSED (1100, narrowly). (3) {1..7} at least 1500: MISSED (800). (4) all-9 at least 1500: PASS (2600).
Reading: with C-15, bit-4 time grows with the number of co-trained bits (1 bit 300; 5 bits 400; 6 bits 500 to 1100; 7 bits 800 to 1100; 9 bits 2600). The outermost bits (0 and 8) cost more than bits 1 and 7. The big jump comes only when all nine bits are present, and the 7-bit sets with 0 and 8 (1100) are well below it, so the effect is not additive. Caveat: 5 seeds, one target bit, n=8; medians of 5 differ from each other by amounts comparable to the seed spread (e.g. 800 to 2600 inside one set), so the ordering among 6 and 7-bit sets is weak evidence. Cause not identified.

## C-17 (EXPLORATORY, follows C-15/C-16): do the output bits' gradients conflict in the shared layers?
Idea source: C-15/C-16 (slowdown only with many far bits). Design. n=8, the all-9-bit joint net as in C-01 (50% of pairs, 3x128 ReLU, Adam 1e-3, batch 256, summed BCE). At steps 0, 100, 300, 1000 (same training run, 5 seeds), compute the gradient of each bit's mean BCE with respect to the hidden-layer parameters (first three Linear layers, output layer excluded) on a fixed batch of 4096 training pairs; report cosine similarity of bit 4's gradient with each other bit's gradient, and with the sum of the other eight. Script c17_grad.py.
Predictions. (1) At steps 100 and 300 the median cosine of bit 4 with the sum of the others is below 0.1. (2) The median cosine with the near bits (3, 5) exceeds 0.1 and exceeds the cosine with the far bits (0, 8) at steps 100 and 300. (3) The far-bit cosines (0 and 8) have median at most 0. Exploratory: cosine alone does not show causation; a null or contrary result means gradient direction conflict is not what slows bit 4.

### C-17 result (exploratory; 5 seeds; c17_grad.py, c17/grad_cos.csv). Median cosine between bit 4's gradient and the others' gradients (hidden layers, all-9-bit net, batch 4096):
step 0: sum of others +0.01; step 100: +0.07; step 300: -0.03; step 1000: +0.13. Single other bits: all between -0.05 and +0.09 at every step (near bits 3,5: +0.04/-0.05 at step 100; far bits 0,8: +0.04/+0.04 at step 100, about 0 at step 300).
Predictions: (1) cosine with the sum of others below 0.1 at steps 100 and 300: PASS (+0.07, -0.03). (2) near bits above 0.1 and above far bits: MISSED (near bits about 0.04 and -0.05). (3) far-bit median at most 0: MISSED at step 100 (+0.04), holds at step 300.
Reading: the gradients are nearly orthogonal, not opposed. So direction conflict between bit 4 and the others is not what I see; the slowdown is not explained by negative gradient alignment. A remaining untested idea (consistent with A-24's Adam finding): the summed loss lets the other eight bits' gradients dominate Adam's step size and noise, so bit 4's signal is diluted. Test C-18: up-weight bit 4's loss.

## C-18 (CONFIRMATORY, follows C-17): is the slowdown dilution of bit 4's signal in the shared Adam step?
Design. Same as C-15 all-9-bit net (n=8, 5 seeds 0-4), summed BCE with weight w on bit 4 and 1 on the others; w in {1, 3, 10, 30}. Measure bit-4 time to 0.99, and time of every other bit (to see what is paid). Script c18_weight.py.
Predictions. (1) bit-4 median at w=10 is at most 1000 steps (w=1 reference 2600 from C-15). (2) bit-4 median decreases monotonically in w over the four values. (3) the median time of some other bit (3 or 5) at w=30 is more than 1.3 times its w=1 time (the cost is paid elsewhere). If (1) fails, the dilution idea is not supported.

## C-19 (CONFIRMATORY, follows C-14): longer budget for n=16 addition (Adam, held-out pairs)
C-14 left 52.9% of n=16 Adam cells censored at 20,000 steps. Design: c14_scale.py with STEPS=60000, N=16, OPT=adam, TASK=add, seeds 0-9, output c19/.
Predictions. (1) censored fraction at most 20%. (2) slowest median bit lies in 4..12. (3) top bit median at most 0.25 of the slowest bit's median. A miss on (1) means a hump peak at n=16 still cannot be located with this budget.

### C-18 result (5 seeds, all-9-bit net, weight w on bit 4's loss; c18_weight.py, c18/weights_n8.csv). Bit-4 time to 0.99 (median; per-seed in the csv):
w=1: 2600 (4800, 4000, 900, 2500, 2600) | w=3: 500 | w=10: 300 | w=30: 300.
Median time of the other bits, w=1 then w=30: bit 0 100 -> 700, bit 1 500 -> 1900, bit 2 800 -> 2300, bit 3 2500 -> 900, bit 5 2900 -> 4200, bit 6 1700 -> 5400, bit 7 900 -> 2000, bit 8 600 -> 700.
Predictions. (1) w=10 median at most 1000: PASS (300). (2) monotone decrease over the four weights: PARTLY (2600, 500, 300, 300: non-increasing, tie between 10 and 30, not strictly decreasing). (3) bit 3 or 5 slower than 1.3 times at w=30: PASS for bit 5 (4200 vs 2900, 1.45 times; bit 3 became faster, 2500 -> 900).
Reading: giving bit 4 three times the loss weight takes it from 2600 to 500 steps, to about its single-bit time (300). So bit 4's slowness in the joint net is not the difficulty of the bit; it is how the shared training effort is split between bits. The cost is paid by the other bits: five of the eight others get slower, bit 6 by 3 times; bit 3 got faster, so it is not a uniform trade. Caveats: 5 seeds, n=8, bit 4 only; weighting also changes Adam's gradient scale, so this shows sensitivity to relative loss weight, not which mechanism (signal share vs update noise) does it. It does NOT test A-24-style update-direction noise directly.

## C-20 (CONFIRMATORY, follows C-18): can bit-loss weights speed up the whole adder?
Idea source: C-18 (weight changes who is slow). Design: n=8, all-9-bit net, 5 seeds (0-4), uniform weights vs profile W = [1,1,2,4,8,4,2,1,1] (larger weight for the bits slow in C-01). Measure the time at which every bit has reached 0.99 (max over bits; cap 10,000 steps, then censored) and per-bit times. Script c18_weight.py with env WPROFILE; output c20/.
Predictions. (1) max-over-bits time under W is lower than uniform in at least 4 of 5 seeds. (2) the median of the max under W is at most 0.7 of uniform. (3) bits 0, 1, 7, 8 are slower under W (median at least 1.3 times). A miss on (1) means the C-18 gain for one bit is paid for by others without a net speed-up.

### C-20 result (5 seeds; c20/weights_n8.csv). Time at which every bit has reached 0.99 (max over bits), seeds 0-4: uniform 4800, 4000, 2600, 2900, 4500; profile W=[1,1,2,4,8,4,2,1,1] 2100, 2500, 3800, 2000, 2000. Median per-bit times, uniform: 100 500 800 2500 2600 2900 1700 900 600; profile: 300 2100 1300 500 300 800 2100 700 300.
Predictions. (1) profile lower in at least 4 of 5 seeds: PASS (4 of 5; seed 2 was higher, 3800 against 2600). (2) median max at most 0.7 of uniform: PASS (2100 against 4000, ratio 0.53). (3) bits 0, 1, 7, 8 slower by 1.3 times: PARTLY (bits 0 and 1 yes, 100 -> 300 and 500 -> 2100; bits 7 and 8 were faster, 900 -> 700 and 600 -> 300).
Reading: a hand-chosen weight profile cut the time for the whole adder to be learned by about half in this setting, paid mostly by the lowest bits (0, 1), which are cheap anyway.
