Seed by seed, p4 forms 0 to 650 steps before bit 4 (600 vs 1000, 1850 vs 2500, 2400 vs 2600); seed 0 reaches neither within 4,000 steps and seed 1 forms p4 at 2700 but bit 4 never persists above 0.99 (final 0.9947).
(2) PASS: single p4 median 50 (<= 600); joint 2400 (>= 3 x 50). Note the single net forms p4 at the first checkpoint, so the factor is at least 48; the single net then needs about 200 more steps for bit 4.
(3) MISS: medians single 50 < lower 600 < upper 500 <= joint 2400. The lower and upper medians are in the wrong order (600 > 500). The bit-4 times do follow the predicted order (250 < 700 < 1950 < 2600). At threshold 0.90 the p4 order is lower 500 > upper 400; at 0.98 it is lower 650 < upper 800 (so the ordering of the two middle nets depends on the threshold and is not a stable result).
(4) PASS by the rule, but UNINFORMATIVE: c4 is already readable at >= 0.95 at step 0 (0.962 at layer 1), so "formation time" is 0 in all 20 net-seeds. The threshold was too weak for c4. This prediction tests nothing.

What this shows, and what it does not:
- In the single and joint nets, p4 becomes linearly readable shortly before bit 4 is solved, which fits "the delay is the time to form a_4 xor b_4". Co-training slows p4 formation: 50 (alone) to about 500-600 (with 4 other bits, lower or upper) to 2400 (all 9 bits). This is a measured association between how many bits are co-trained and p4 formation time. It does not say what in the training dynamics causes it.
- In the upper-only net p4 is readable early (200 to 700) but bit 4 takes 1150 to 2300, a gap of 650 to 1450 steps. So p4 formation does NOT explain the delay there.
- Two seeds have a solved bit 4 (>= 0.99) without a p4 probe reaching 0.95 (lower seed 1: p4 never, bit 4 at 700; joint seed 0 final p4 probe 0.892, bit 4 final 0.9575). A linear probe on one layer tests whether p4 is separately available as a linear feature, not whether the net uses it, so p4 as a separate linear feature is not necessary.
EXPLORATORY (not a pre-written prediction; threshold chosen after seeing the data): at probe threshold 0.99, c4 forms at 150 to 300 steps in the single and lower nets and at 200 to 1150 in the joint net, but never within 4,000 steps in the upper-only net, where bit 4 is solved at 1150 to 2300. The lower net, which has bits 0..3, has c4 early and bit 4 follows p4; the upper net lacks the bits that teach the carry, and its c4 is not separately readable. This is a lead to be tested with a pre-written prediction (next step), not a finding.

## C-59 (CONFIRMATORY): does the slowdown of bit 4 come from the GRADIENT that the other outputs send into the shared layers?
Idea source: C-58 (p4 forms about 10 times later with 4 co-trained bits, about 50 times later with all 8) and C-18 (weights on bit 4 shorten its time). Not yet separated: (a) the other outputs' gradients into the shared layers interfere with bit 4's, or (b) something else about having more outputs (for example only the extra head weights).
Design. Joint net as in C-58 (n=8, 3x128 ReLU, Adam lr 1e-3, batch 256, 50 percent pairs, seeds 0-4, same splits, same initial weights and data order as C-58). One change: the last hidden layer feeds bit 4's output as usual, but for the 8 other outputs it is fed through h_mix = lam*h + (1-lam)*stopgrad(h). The forward values are identical, the heads of all 9 outputs train with full gradient, and the gradient that the 8 other outputs send back into the shared layers is multiplied by lam. lam in {0, 0.03, 0.1, 0.3, 1}. Time to 0.99 as in C-49 (first of two consecutive evaluations at >= 0.99), but evaluated every 50 steps; 6,000 steps maximum; stops when all 9 bits are reached. Script c59_gradshare.py, output c59/gradshare_n8.csv, log c59.log. Timing: 300 steps take 3 s.
Predictions (guesses, not derived).
(0) check: lam=1 reproduces the C-58 joint net, so bit 4 at 1000, 2500, 2600 for seeds 2, 3, 4 (within one evaluation interval, 100 steps). If not, the new script differs from the old one and nothing else in this experiment is interpreted.
(1) lam=0: the median bit-4 time is at most 400 (the single net in C-58 had median 250); the shared layers get only bit 4's gradient.
(2) lam=1: the median bit-4 time is at least 2000 (C-58 joint: 2600).
(3) The medians are non-decreasing in lam over the five values.
(4) lam=0.1: the median bit-4 time is at most 3 times the lam=0 median. If it is larger, the slowdown is not simply the relative size of the other outputs' gradient; if it is smaller, the effect is a matter of magnitude.
Record whatever the other eight bits do (their times) without a prediction.

### C-59 RESULT (5 seeds, 25 runs; c59/gradshare_n8.csv, c59.log). Bit-4 time to 0.99 (50-step evaluation), per seed 0..4, then median:
lam=0: 200 200 250 250 300 | 250
lam=0.03: 200 250 250 250 300 | 250
lam=0.1: 300 250 250 300 350 | 300
lam=0.3: 450 450 300 450 750 | 450
lam=1: 4400 5250 1000 2450 2550 | 2550
Median times of the other bits (bit 0..8):
lam=0: all censored except bit 4 (never learned in 6,000 steps, since the shared layers get no gradient from them)
lam=0.03: 850 2100 1950 850 [250] 5000 5050 2250 800
lam=0.1: 300 1550 1650 850 [300] 3500 3250 1100 350
lam=0.3: 150 700 1200 1500 [450] 3550 2050 750 300
lam=1: 100 450 850 2500 [2550] 2450 1650 1000 550
Scoring against the predictions written before the run:
(0) check: PASS. lam=1 bit 4 for seeds 2, 3, 4 is 1000, 2450, 2550 against C-58's joint 1000, 2500, 2600 (within one 50-step interval). For seeds 0 and 1 C-58 did not reach 0.99 within 4,000 steps and this run (6,000 steps) reaches it at 4400 and 5250. The new script reproduces the old one.
(1) PASS: lam=0 median 250 (limit 400).
(2) PASS: lam=1 median 2550 (limit 2000).
(3) PASS: 250, 250, 300, 450, 2550 is non-decreasing.
(4) PASS: lam=0.1 median 300 (limit 3 x 250 = 750).
Reading. The 10-fold slowdown of bit 4 in the joint net comes through the gradient that the other eight outputs send into the shared layers, not through their extra output weights: with all nine heads training normally, scaling that gradient to 0.3 leaves bit 4 at 450 (1.8 times the single-net time), to 0.1 at 300, to 0.03 at 250 (equal to alone). The change is steep between 0.3 and 1 (450 -> 2550). At the same time a small share of the other bits' gradient is enough to teach them (all eight bits are learned by about 5,000 steps at lam=0.03), but the middle and upper bits (5, 6) are then much slower than in the standard joint net (5000 against 2450 and 1650 at lam=0.03), so the trade-off between bits is again visible.
Consistency with C-18, noticed after the run: with Adam, weighting bit 4's loss by w and scaling the other bits' gradient into the shared layers by 1/w give the same direction of update for the shared layers (Adam's step does not depend on an overall factor). The numbers agree: C-18 w=3 gave 500 and w=10 gave 300; C-59 lam=0.3 (about w=3.3) gives 450 and lam=0.1 gives 300. So C-59 is largely a second view of C-18, with one addition: the heads of the other bits are not what matters, only the gradient that reaches the shared layers. It does not separate the two explanations still open: (a) Adam's per-parameter scaling dilutes bit 4's step because the other bits' gradient is added to the second-moment estimate (C-17 found the gradients nearly orthogonal, which fits this), or (b) the other bits' features change the shared layers' state in a way that makes p4 harder to form.
Next test (C-60): replace Adam by momentum SGD. Under (a), with nearly orthogonal gradients and no per-parameter normalisation, bit 4's time should depend much less on lam.

## C-60 (CONFIRMATORY, follows C-59, C-17, C-18): is the slowdown a property of Adam's per-parameter scaling?
Idea source: C-17 (gradients of bit 4 and of the other bits are nearly orthogonal in the shared layers) and the Adam dilution idea in C-18. If the slowdown comes from the other bits' gradients inflating Adam's second-moment estimate, then plain momentum SGD, which has no per-parameter normalisation, should show much less dependence of bit 4's time on the share lam of the others' gradient.
Design. Exactly C-59 (lam in {0, 0.03, 0.1, 0.3, 1}, seeds 0-4, same splits and initial weights, eval every 50 steps) but momentum SGD (lr 0.01, momentum 0.9, as in C-14) instead of Adam, 10,000 steps maximum, stop when all 9 bits are reached. Script c60_gradshare_opt.py (c59 plus env OPT=sgd, LR=0.01), output c60/gradshare_sgd_n8.csv, log c60.log.
Disclosure: before writing these predictions I ran a 3,000-step smoke test on seed 0 only: bit 4 at 1800 (lam=0) and 2850 (lam=1). Seed 0 is part of the final run; the predictions below were chosen knowing that single pair of numbers.
Predictions. (1) the ratio of the median bit-4 time at lam=1 to that at lam=0 is at most 2.5 (Adam, C-59: 2550 / 250 = 10.2). (2) in at least 4 of 5 seeds the lam=1 bit-4 time is at most 3 times the lam=0 time (Adam per-seed ratios: 22, 26, 4, 10, 8.5; so at most one of five seeds was under 3). If (1) fails the Adam-scaling account is not supported and the interference is a property of the network and the data, not of the optimizer. Caveats stated in advance: one SGD learning rate; with lam=0 the other bits are expected never to be learned.

### C-60 RESULT (5 seeds, 25 runs, momentum SGD lr 0.01; c60/gradshare_sgd_n8.csv, c60.log). Bit-4 time to 0.99, per seed 0..4, then median:
lam=0: 1800 2100 1700 2400 2250 | 2100
lam=0.03: 1850 2100 1750 2400 2400 | 2100
lam=0.1: 2050 2400 1950 2450 2700 | 2400
lam=0.3: 2600 2550 2300 3000 3500 | 2600
lam=1: 2850 2750 2650 3250 3700 | 2850
Median times of the other bits at lam=1 (bit 0..8): 600 1300 2500 2950 [2850] 3200 2450 1850 850.
Scoring against the predictions written before the run (with the disclosure about the seed-0 smoke test):
(1) PASS: median ratio lam=1 / lam=0 is 2850 / 2100 = 1.36 (limit 2.5; Adam in C-59: 10.2).
(2) PASS: the per-seed ratios are 1.58, 1.31, 1.56, 1.35, 1.64, so all 5 of 5 are at most 3 (needed 4 of 5; Adam: 22, 26, 4, 10, 8.5).
Reading. With plain momentum SGD the other eight outputs slow bit 4 by about 1.4 times, not 10 times, and bit 4's time is monotone but gently dependent on the gradient share (2100 -> 2850). This supports the account that the large slowdown under Adam is a property of Adam's per-parameter scaling and not of the data or network alone. Caveats: (i) only one SGD learning rate was run; SGD alone is 8 times slower than Adam alone (2100 against 250), so the two optimizers are in different regimes and a comparison of ratios at one setting is not a proof; (ii) it shows the optimizer matters, not that second-moment contamination is the specific cause. Next (C-61): measure the factor Adam actually applies, and test whether it quantitatively accounts for the 10-fold slowdown.

## C-61 (CONFIRMATORY, follows C-59 and C-60): does Adam's dilution factor account for the size of the slowdown?
Idea source: the second-moment (dilution) account in C-18 and C-60. Model: in Adam a parameter's step is m / sqrt(v). Bit 4's own contribution is divided by sqrt(v_total) in the joint net, where v_total is the running mean square of the whole gradient, but would be divided by sqrt(v_4) if bit 4 were alone (v_4: running mean square of bit 4's gradient only). So bit 4's signal moves the shared layers at relative speed s(t) = mean over shared-layer parameters of sqrt(v_4 / v_total), both bias-corrected with Adam's beta2 = 0.999. If the slowdown were pure time-rescaling, the accumulated progress I(T) = sum over steps t <= T of s(t) at the time T when bit 4 reaches 0.99 in the joint net should equal the time the single net needs (about 250).
Design. The C-59 runs for lam=1 and lam=0.3, seeds 0-4 (same code path: same splits, initial weights, data order, Adam lr 1e-3, 50-step evaluations), with extra bookkeeping: at every step the gradients of bit 4's loss and of the other eight outputs' loss (after lam scaling) with respect to the shared-layer parameters are computed separately (the update uses their sum, unchanged), v_4 and v_total are tracked as above, and s(t) and I(t) are logged every 50 steps; each run stops when bit 4 has reached 0.99 twice in a row. Script c61_dilution.py, output c61/dilution_n8.csv.
Predictions. (0) check: the bit-4 times equal the C-59 values within 100 steps for all 10 runs. (1) for lam=1, I(T) lies between 0.67 and 1.5 times the same seed's lam=0 time from C-59 (200, 200, 250, 250, 300) in at least 4 of 5 seeds. (2) the same band for lam=0.3 in at least 4 of 5 seeds. (3) the median of s over the first 500 steps is smaller for lam=1 than for lam=0.3 (ordering only). I expect (1) and (2) to fail (I guess s is near 0.3 to 0.6, which would give an accumulated progress of about 1,000 or more), but this is a guess. If (1) and (2) fail, the dilution factor as defined is not the whole story and the slowdown includes a change in the state of the shared layers (more than a rescaling of time).
Note on C-61: the predictions above were written before the script existed. A 400-step smoke test of the finished script (seed 0, lam=1) then showed s about 0.25 (instantaneous, from step 300 onward), which I saw before the full run. Seed 0 stays in the full run.

### C-61 RESULT (5 seeds x lam 1 and 0.3; c61/dilution_n8.csv, c61.log). I(T) = accumulated dilution progress up to the bit-4 time T; base = same seed's lam=0 time from C-59 (200, 200, 250, 250, 300):
lam=1: T = 4400 5250 1000 2450 2550 | I(T) = 1719 1914 321 779 869 | I/base = 8.6 9.6 1.3 3.1 2.9
lam=0.3: T = 450 450 300 450 750 | I(T) = 292 308 213 282 498 | I/base = 1.5 1.5 0.85 1.1 1.7
Median instantaneous s over the first 500 steps: lam=1 0.242, lam=0.3 0.662 (so about 4 times and 1.5 times dilution).
Scoring against the predictions written before the run:
(0) check: PASS. All 10 bit-4 times equal the C-59 values exactly.
(1) MISS: lam=1, I(T) within 0.67 to 1.5 times the base in 1 of 5 seeds (needed 4).
(2) MISS: lam=0.3, in the band in 3 of 5 seeds (needed 4); the two outside are close (308 against a limit of 300, 498 against 450; ratios 1.5 and 1.7).
(3) PASS: 0.242 < 0.662.
Reading. Adam's dilution factor (the fraction sqrt(v_4 / v_total), averaged over the shared-layer parameters) is about 0.24 for the standard joint net in the first 500 steps and 0.66 at lam=0.3. Counting only that factor, the time needed at lam=0.3 is within a factor of 1.1 to 1.7 of the single-net time (3 of 5 seeds within 1.5), so for that setting the dilution accounts for the slowdown to within about 50 percent. For lam=1 it accounts for the whole slowdown in one seed (2: ratio 1.3) and about two thirds of the log-slowdown in two seeds (3 and 4, ratio about 3 left over from 10), but not in the two slowest seeds (0 and 1), where the accumulated progress at the time bit 4 is learned is still 8.6 and 9.6 times what the single net needed. So the dilution is real and large (a 4-fold smaller effective step for bit 4's signal) but it is not the whole story at lam=1: the seed-to-seed spread (1000 to 5250 steps) comes from something else, which these measurements do not identify.
EXPLORATORY and weak: in seeds 0 and 1 (lam=1) the factor s stays near 0.23 to 0.25 for more than 2,000 steps and rises to 0.4 at steps 2450 and 3550, with bit 4 reached 1,950 and 1,700 steps after that; in seed 4 s is above 0.4 from step 150 yet bit 4 takes 2550. So "bit 4 is learned once the other bits' gradients have decayed" is not supported as a general rule by this data.

## C-62 (CONFIRMATORY, follows C-59 to C-61): where does the seed-to-seed spread of bit 4's time in the joint net come from?
Idea source: C-61 (the Adam dilution factor leaves the slow seeds unexplained) and C-59 (lam=1 times 1000 to 5250 across seeds 0-4). A "seed" in the earlier runs sets three things at once: the initial weights, the order of the training batches, and the train/test split. This experiment separates the first two.
Design. The standard joint net (n=8, 3x128 ReLU, Adam lr 1e-3, batch 256, summed BCE, all 9 bits), ONE fixed train/test split (the split of seed 0, permutation 500) for every run. Crossed factors: initial weights (5 values, torch seed i*100+4 for i = 0..4, the same initial weights as the earlier seeds 0-4) times batch order (3 values, numpy generator seed b*1000+7 for b = 0,1,2) = 15 runs. Record the bit-4 time to 0.99 (50-step evaluation, first of two consecutive evaluations; 8,000 steps maximum; each run stops when bit 4 is reached). Script c62_variance.py, output c62/variance_n8.csv, log c62.log.
Predictions. (1) the slowest run takes at least 4 times as long as the fastest (the spread is not a split artefact). (2) in a one-way analysis of variance of log(bit-4 time) with the initial weights as the factor, the between-initial-weights share of the total variance is above 0.5 (the spread mostly comes from the initial weights, not from the batch order). (3) no run is censored at 8,000 steps. I have no strong prior for (2); the alternative (batch order and sampling noise dominate) would mean the slow seeds are partly bad luck in a stochastic plateau escape.

### C-62 RESULT (15 runs, 5 initial weights x 3 batch orders, one fixed split; c62/variance_n8.csv, c62.log). Bit-4 time (rows: initial weights 0..4; columns: batch orders 0, 1, 2):
init 0: 2000 4050 3900 | init 1: 4150 3200 3650 | init 2: 1900 1400 1550 | init 3: 3350 3000 2700 | init 4: 3600 2750 3700. No run censored.
Geometric mean per init: 3161, 3646, 1604, 3005, 3321. Within one init the largest/smallest ratio is 2.0, 1.3, 1.4, 1.2, 1.35.
Scoring against the predictions written before the run:
(1) MISS: largest/smallest over the 15 runs is 3.0 (predicted at least 4). With the fixed split the spread (1400 to 4150) is narrower than across the earlier per-seed splits (1000 to 5250 in C-59 lam=1), so part of the earlier spread may come from the split, which this design does not separate.
(2) PASS: the between-initial-weights share of the variance of log(time) is 0.73 (batch-order share 0.01, residual 0.26); F(4,10) = 6.8, p = 0.007.
(3) PASS: no censoring.
Reading. For a fixed split, the time at which bit 4 is learned is mostly a property of the initial weights: init 2 is fast in all three batch orders (1400 to 1900) and the others take 2700 to 4150, while changing only the batch order changes the time by a factor of at most 2. So the "slow seed" is a property of the initialisation (and perhaps the split), not luck in the sampling. What about the initialisation matters is not identified: a quick look at C-58's step-0 probe accuracies (p4, c4, p3 at each layer) for the same five initial weights shows no clear separation of init 2 (its p4 readability at step 0 is 0.868, 0.791, 0.740, within the range of the others; its p3 readability is the highest of the five at layers 1 and 2, but with five inits this is not evidence). With 5 initial weights only, the variance shares are estimates with wide intervals.

## C-63 (CONFIRMATORY, follows C-35 and C-61): how much of the width effect is the dilution factor?
Idea source: C-35 (the hump shrinks about as 1/width between widths 128 and 1024) and C-61 (the dilution factor s accounts for part of the slowdown at width 128, but not the whole of it for lam=1). Question: does a wider net remove the slowdown by raising s (less dilution), by removing the part that s cannot explain, or both?
Design. C-61 at other widths of the 3 hidden layers: W in {64, 256, 512} (W=128 is C-61/C-59). lam in {1, 0} (lam=0 gives the single-bit reference time T0 for the same seed and width; its s is 1 by construction). Seeds 0-4 (same splits, initial-weight seeds and batch order as C-59/C-61), Adam lr 1e-3, evaluation every 50 steps, 12,000 steps maximum. Bit-4 time, instantaneous s, and accumulated progress I(T) as in C-61. Script c63_dilution_width.py (c61 with env WIDTH), output c63_w{W}/dilution_w{W}_n8.csv. Runs censored at 12,000 steps are excluded from the medians below and counted; if more than 2 of 5 seeds are censored at some width the predictions for that width are not evaluated.
Predictions. (1) the median of s over the first 500 steps at lam=1 increases with width: W=64 < 0.242 (the W=128 value) < W=256 < W=512. (2) the median ratio T(lam=1) / T(lam=0) at W=512 is at most 3 (W=128, C-59: 10.2). (3) the median ratio I(T) / T0 at W=512 is at most 1.5, and at W=64 at least 3 (W=128: 3.1; seeds range 1.3 to 9.6). I have no strong prior for (1) or (3); if (1) fails the width effect does not go through the size of the second-moment dilution, and if (3) fails at W=512 something besides dilution remains at large width.

### C-63 RESULT (lam=1 and lam=0, seeds 0-4, widths 64/256/512; c63_w*/dilution_w*_n8.csv, logs c63_w*.log; W=128 is C-59/C-61). Median s over the first 500 steps at lam=1, bit-4 times at lam=1 (T1) and lam=0 (T0), medians of the per-seed ratios:
W=64: s 0.222 | T1 = censored, 11400, censored, 10800, 11150 (2 of 5 censored at 12,000) | T0 = 300 300 300 400 550 | T1/T0 27 (3 uncensored seeds only) | I(T1)/T0 11.4 (3 seeds)
W=128: s 0.242 | T1/T0 10.2 | I/T0 3.1 (per seed 8.6 9.6 1.3 3.1 2.9)
W=256: s 0.278 | T1 = 1050 900 1050 1000 1500 | T0 = 200 each | T1/T0 5.25 | I/T0 1.68 (2.0 1.5 1.7 1.7 2.6)
W=512: s 0.327 | T1 = 650 450 600 400 500 | T0 = 150 150 250 150 150 | T1/T0 3.0 | I/T0 0.99 (1.5 1.0 0.9 0.9 1.2)
Scoring against the predictions written before the run:
(1) PASS: s increases with width, 0.222 < 0.242 < 0.278 < 0.327.
(2) PASS (on the boundary): the median T1/T0 at W=512 is 3.0 (limit 3).
(3) PASS: I/T0 at W=512 is 0.99 (limit 1.5); at W=64 it is 11.4 (limit at least 3), but on three seeds only, and the two censored seeds are slower, so the W=64 figure is a lower bound for the median.
Reading. Two separate things change the bit-4 time when the other eight bits are trained with it. (i) Adam's dilution factor, about 0.22 to 0.33 at every width tried: it changes only slowly with width and by itself costs a factor of 3 to 4. (ii) A further slowdown that the dilution factor cannot account for, I/T0, which falls from 11 (width 64) to 3 (128), 1.7 (256) and 1.0 (512): at width 512 the whole slowdown (3 times) is accounted for by the dilution, at width 64 about 90 percent of the log-slowdown is not. So the strong effect of width found in C-35 (the hump nearly vanishes between 128 and 1024) works mostly by removing part (ii), and the dilution part (i) remains. What part (ii) is (capacity of the shared layers being used up by the other bits, or a different cause) is not identified by these runs. Caveats: 5 seeds; one learning rate; "I(T)" assumes that progress along bit 4's direction is proportional to the dilution factor, which is a model, not a measurement.

### C-57 RESULT (transformer, 8 propagate tokens added, lr 1e-3, 20,000-step cap, 5 seeds; c57/transformer_p_n8.csv, c57.log; the job ran in one piece, no seed lost). Bit-times per seed, bits 0..8:
seed 0: 400 11000 13000 15700 10200 8100 4100 1500 900 | seed 1: 200 700 3100 4300 8800 4700 2900 1900 1300 | seed 2: 200 600 1800 3300 4100 3200 2400 1400 800 | seed 3: 200 900 2700 2500 1800 2800 1700 1100 1000 | seed 4: 200 900 1800 3900 3000 2600 2100 1300 300. No censored cell.
Median per bit: 200 900 2700 3900 4100 3200 2400 1400 900 (C-54, lr 1e-3, without the propagate tokens: 1900 5700 7800 7200 7700 6500 5300 1200 700). Per-seed maximum: 15700 8800 4100 2800 3900 (median 4100; C-54: 8100).
Scoring against the predictions written before the run:
(1) MISS, narrowly: the median of the per-seed maximum is 4,100 (limit 4,000).
(2) PASS: all five seeds reach 0.99 on all nine bits (C-54: 4 of 5).
(3) PASS: the slowest median bit is bit 4 (C-54: bit 2).
Reading. Supplying a_i xor b_i as extra tokens makes the transformer faster on bits 0 to 6 by 2 to 8 times (for example bit 1: 5,700 to 900, bit 3: 7,200 to 3,900), but it does not remove the hump: the profile still rises from 200 at bit 0 to 4,100 at bit 4 and falls to 900 at bit 8 (ratio 4.6), and seed 0 is a strong outlier (15,700). In the MLP (C-49) the same input removed most of the hump (slowest bit 900 steps). So the XOR features are part of what slows the transformer, but a large part of its delay remains with them given. Caveats: 5 seeds, one size, one learning rate, comparison with C-54 uses the same seeds and splits but a different input length.

## C-64 (CONFIRMATORY, follows C-58, C-61, C-63): which co-trained bits make the dilution factor small, and which add the extra slowdown?
Idea source: C-61/C-63 split the slowdown of bit 4 in the joint net into the dilution factor s (Adam's second moment) and an extra part I/T0 that the factor does not account for. C-58 (nets with only the lower four or only the upper four other bits) and C-15/C-16 (slowdown mainly with several far bits) suggest the amount depends on which bits are present. Here the two parts are measured for chosen subsets.
Design. C-61 with only a chosen set of other bits in the loss (all nine heads exist; bits outside the set get no loss at all), lam = 1, width 128, seeds 0-4 (same splits, initial weights, batch order), 12,000-step cap. Sets: {3}, {5}, {2,6}, {0,1,2,3} (lower), {5,6,7,8} (upper), and the full set of eight (the C-61 lam=1 runs, not rerun). The single-bit reference time T0 for the same seed is the lam=0 value of C-59 (200, 200, 250, 250, 300). Script c64_dilution_subset.py (c61 with env OTH, TAG), output c64_<tag>/.
Predictions. (1) the median s over the first 500 steps decreases with the number of co-trained bits: {3} and {5} (1 bit) > {2,6} (2 bits) > {0,1,2,3} and {5,6,7,8} (4 bits) > the full set (8 bits; 0.242 from C-61). (2) the single-neighbour sets {3} and {5} have a median bit-4 time at most 2 times T0 (that is at most 500). (3) the upper set {5,6,7,8} has a larger median I/T0 than the lower set {0,1,2,3}. (4) the median I/T0 for {0,1,2,3} is at most 1.5 (the extra slowdown appears only with the upper bits); I am least sure about (4).

### C-64 RESULT (lam=1, width 128, subsets of co-trained bits, 5 seeds; c64_*/dilution_*_n8.csv, logs c64_*.log; reference T0 = 200 200 250 250 300). Median s over the first 500 steps, bit-4 times per seed, median T/T0, per-seed I(T)/T0 and its median:
{3}: s 0.686 | 200 200 200 250 300 | 1.0 | 0.75 0.69 0.60 0.72 0.71, median 0.71
{5}: s 0.698 | 300 250 300 350 350 | 1.25 | 1.09 0.89 0.89 1.04 0.85, median 0.89
{2,6}: s 0.560 | 350 350 300 300 350 | 1.2 | 1.05 1.01 0.74 0.65 0.59, median 0.74
{0,1,2,3}: s 0.395 | 850 700 500 700 2450 | 3.5 | 2.05 1.72 0.99 1.16 4.52, median 1.72
{5,6,7,8}: s 0.328 | 1400 1750 1100 2000 1550 | 7.0 | 2.86 3.57 1.75 3.26 2.04, median 2.86
all eight (C-61/C-59, lam=1): s 0.242 | 1000 to 5250, median 2550 | 10.2 | median 3.1
Scoring against the predictions written before the run:
(1) PASS: s decreases with the number of co-trained bits (0.69/0.70 for one bit, 0.56 for two, 0.40/0.33 for four, 0.24 for eight).
(2) PASS: with one neighbouring bit the median bit-4 time is 200 ({3}) and 300 ({5}), at most 500.
(3) PASS: the upper set has the larger median I/T0 (2.86 against 1.72).
(4) MISS: the lower set's median I/T0 is 1.72 (limit 1.5), with one seed at 4.5.
Reading. The dilution factor falls steadily with the number of co-trained bits (0.7 to 0.24), but the extra slowdown (I/T0) is about 1 or below with one or two co-trained bits, about 1.7 with the lower four, and 2.9 to 3.1 with the upper four or all eight: it appears with four or more co-trained bits, is larger for the upper than for the lower bits, and the upper four give almost all of what all eight give. This is a description of when the extra part appears, not an explanation of it.

## C-65 (CONFIRMATORY, follows C-62): is the bit-4 time in the joint net driven by gradient noise, or fixed by the initial weights?
Idea source: C-62 (73 percent of the variance of log(bit-4 time) is between initial weights, 1 percent between batch orders) and the XOR project (A-26/A-27: gradient noise and batch size matter for the XOR escape time). Batch size has not been varied for the adder in this project.
Design. The standard joint net (n=8, 3x128 ReLU, Adam lr 1e-3, all 9 bits, C-59 lam=1 code path) with batch size BS in {64, 1024, 4096}, seeds 0-4 (same splits, initial weights and batch-order seeds as C-59; the batch-256 reference is C-59 lam=1: 4400 5250 1000 2450 2550, median 2550). Learning rate unchanged, so the comparison is per step. 12,000-step cap. Bit-4 time to 0.99 (50-step evaluation) and the other bit times. Script c65_batch.py (c59 with env BS), output c65_bs<BS>/gradshare_bs<BS>_n8.csv.
Predictions. (1) the median bit-4 time at BS 4096 is within a factor 0.7 to 1.4 of the BS 256 value (1785 to 3570), i.e. gradient noise is not what sets the time. (2) the same for BS 1024 and BS 64. (3) the two slowest seeds at BS 256 (seeds 0 and 1) are also the two slowest at BS 4096. If (1) fails with a much shorter time at 4096, sampling noise does play a large role and C-62's batch-order result needs another reading; if the time is much longer, bigger batches hurt per step.

### C-65 RESULT (standard joint net, lam=1, 5 seeds per batch size; c65_bs*/gradshare_bs*_n8.csv, logs c65_bs*.log; batch 256 is C-59). Bit-4 time per seed 0..4, median:
batch 64: 6000 6750 1600 4500 4400 | 4500
batch 256 (C-59): 4400 5250 1000 2450 2550 | 2550
batch 1024: 3550 3950 950 1950 2250 | 2250
batch 4096: 2700 2900 650 1800 2250 | 2250
Control, bit 4 alone (lam=0, same seeds): batch 64: 550 500 600 550 550 | 550; batch 256 (C-59): 200 200 250 250 300 | 250; batch 4096: 100 100 100 100 150 | 100. Joint / single: 8.2 (batch 64), 10.2 (256), 22.5 (4096).
Seed order from fastest to slowest: batch 64: 2, 4, 3, 0, 1; batches 256, 1024 and 4096: 2, 3, 4, 0, 1 (identical).
Scoring against the predictions written before the run:
(1) PASS: batch 4096 median 2250 is 0.88 times the batch-256 value (band 0.7 to 1.4).
(2) PARTLY: batch 1024 passes (0.88); batch 64 misses (1.76 times).
(3) PASS: seeds 0 and 1 are the two slowest at batch 4096 (2700, 2900) as at batch 256.
Reading. Above batch size about 256 to 1024 the joint-net bit-4 time stops improving (2550, 2250, 2250 steps), while the single-bit time keeps falling (550, 250, 100). The ordering of the seeds is the same at batches 256, 1024 and 4096. So the slowdown of bit 4 in the joint net is not mainly sampling noise: with large batches (approximately deterministic dynamics) it is still 2,000 or more steps and still ordered by seed, i.e. by initial weights and split. Noise matters for the joint net below about batch 256 (4500 steps at batch 64). Caveats: fixed lr 1e-3 (steps, not epochs or samples); 5 seeds; the batch-4096 single-bit time (100) is at the resolution of the evaluation interval (50); the first control run was started with a 12,000-step cap and stopped by PID after two seeds, the remaining seeds were run with a 1,500-step cap, and one duplicate row (batch 64, seed 1) from a stale process was removed (identical values).

## C-66 (CONFIRMATORY, follows C-62 and C-65): does the plateau behave like an escape from a saddle (time depends on the scale of the initial weights)?
Idea source: C-62 and C-65 show a bit-4 time that is fixed by initial weights and split, nearly independent of batch noise above batch 256, and Adam dilution (C-61) does not account for all of it. If the plateau is an escape from a near-stationary point, its length should depend on how close the initial weights are to it: smaller initial weights, longer plateau (the time grows with log(1/scale) in standard analyses of deep linear and low-rank networks). If instead the plateau is a matter of slow feature formation at a fixed rate, the initial scale should not matter much.
Design. The C-59/C-65 code path at batch 4096 (nearly deterministic; lr 1e-3, Adam, 3x128 ReLU, seeds 0-4, same splits and batch-order seeds) with every initial weight and bias multiplied by a factor c in {0.5, 1, 2, 4} after the default (PyTorch) initialisation; c = 1 is the C-65 batch-4096 run. lam = 1 (joint, 12,000-step cap) and lam = 0 (bit 4 alone with all heads present, 1,500-step cap). Script c66_initscale.py, output c66_c<c>/.
Predictions. (1) the median joint bit-4 time decreases strictly over c = 0.5, 1, 2 (largest at c = 0.5). (2) at c = 0.5 the median joint time is at least 1.3 times the c = 1 value (2250). (3) the ratio of the median bit-4-alone times, c = 0.5 over c = 1, is smaller than the same ratio for the joint net (the initial scale affects the joint plateau more than the single-bit one). I have no strong prior; a flat result (ratios between 0.8 and 1.25 for c = 0.5 and 2) would say the plateau is not a saddle escape in this sense.

### C-66 RESULT (batch 4096, Adam lr 1e-3, all initial weights and biases multiplied by c; 5 seeds; c66_c*/initscale_c*_n8.csv, logs c66_c*.log; c = 1 is C-65 batch 4096). Bit-4 time per seed 0..4 (median); time at which every bit is learned (max over bits, median):
c = 0.5: joint 3950 4050 1050 5700 2700 (3950); max 5800 4050 6150 5700 4000 (5700) | bit 4 alone 150 150 100 100 100 (100)
c = 1: joint 2700 2900 650 1800 2250 (2250); max 2700 2900 1550 2000 2450 (2450) | alone 100 100 100 100 150 (100)
c = 2: joint 450 350 350 700 550 (450); max 500 550 550 700 550 (550) | alone 150 150 150 150 150 (150)
c = 4: joint 550 550 600 650 700 (600); max 800 750 700 850 750 (750) | alone 350 300 300 350 400 (350)
Joint / alone: 39.5 (c = 0.5), 22.5 (1), 3.0 (2), 1.7 (4).
Scoring against the predictions written before the run:
(1) PASS: the joint median decreases strictly over c = 0.5, 1, 2 (3950, 2250, 450).
(2) PASS: T(c=0.5) = 3950 is 1.76 times T(c=1) (limit 1.3).
(3) PASS: the alone-time ratio c = 0.5 / c = 1 is 1.0, smaller than the joint ratio 1.76.
Reading. The initial scale of the weights changes the joint bit-4 time more than any other knob tried so far, and almost not the bit-4-alone time: doubling the scale takes the joint net from 2250 to 450 steps and the full adder from 2450 to 550 steps, with a nearly flat per-bit profile, while the net trained on bit 4 alone stays at 100 to 150 steps (and becomes slower, 350, at c = 4). The effect saturates: c = 4 (600) is not faster than c = 2 (450). Halving the scale slows the joint net 1.8 times. So the long plateau of the joint net is not a fixed cost of the task; it depends strongly on how large the initial weights are, as it does on learning rate (C-36) and width (C-35). Whether this is a movement from a lazy regime (little change of the weights) to a rich one is not tested here (next: C-67). Caveats: 5 seeds; one lr; c scales weights and biases of all layers; the default initialisation scale is already 1/sqrt(fan-in).

## C-67 (CONFIRMATORY, follows C-66): is a large initial scale a lazy-regime effect (little change of the weights)?
Idea source: C-66. A standard diagnostic: in a "lazy" (kernel-like) regime the weights move little relative to their initial values while the output changes a lot, and larger initial scale pushes a net toward that regime; in a "rich" regime the weights (and so the features) change by amounts comparable to the initial weights. If the plateau of the joint net is the time needed to learn new features, a larger initial scale should replace feature learning by reading existing random features, so the relative change of the weights at the time bit 4 is solved should shrink as c grows.
Design. C-66 joint runs (lam = 1, batch 4096, c in {0.5, 1, 2, 4}, seeds 0-4; same code path) recording, at the step when bit 4 is first confirmed (persisting at two consecutive evaluations), the relative change ||W - W0||_F / ||W0||_F of each of the four weight matrices (three hidden layers, output layer). Script c67_lazy.py, output c67_c<c>/. Only for c = 1 the C-65 runs are repeated (identical times expected; this is also a check).
Predictions. (1) the median relative change of the first hidden layer's weights at the bit-4 time decreases strictly with c over 0.5, 1, 2, 4. (2) the c = 4 median is at most half the c = 1 median. (3) for c = 1 the times equal the C-65 batch-4096 values (2700 2900 650 1800 2250) within 100 steps. If (1) fails, the speed-up from a larger initial scale is not a lazy-regime effect in this sense.

### C-67 RESULT (joint net, batch 4096, initial scale c; relative change ||W - W0||_F / ||W0||_F at the step bit 4 is first confirmed, median over 5 seeds; c67_c*/lazy_c*_n8.csv, logs c67_c*.log):
c = 0.5: bit-4 times 3950 4050 1050 5700 2700 | relative change: hidden layer 1 2.94, layer 2 11.3, layer 3 8.8, output layer 4.5
c = 1: times 2700 2900 650 1800 2250 | 1.47, 4.6, 3.3, 2.4
c = 2: times 450 350 350 700 550 | 0.56, 1.1, 1.2, 1.1
c = 4: times 550 550 600 650 700 | 0.26, 0.58, 0.60, 0.56
Scoring against the predictions written before the run:
(1) PASS: the first-layer median decreases strictly with c (2.94, 1.47, 0.56, 0.26).
(2) PASS: the c = 4 value (0.26) is far below half of the c = 1 value (1.47).
(3) PASS: the c = 1 times equal the C-65 batch-4096 values exactly (2700 2900 650 1800 2250); same code path and seeds reproduce.
Reading. At the default initial scale (c = 1) and below, the weights move by more than their own initial norm before bit 4 is solved (first layer 1.5 times, deeper layers 3 to 11 times), a feature-learning ("rich") regime in which the hidden representation is rebuilt; with larger initial scale the same bit is solved with smaller relative changes (0.56 at c = 2, 0.26 at c = 4), which is what a move toward the lazy, random-feature regime looks like, and the joint-net plateau shrinks from 2250 to 450 and 600 steps. This is consistent with the account that the plateau is the time needed to learn features (here the a_i xor b_i features, C-43 to C-58), not the time to fit an output. It does not prove that the c = 4 net is lazy: the output's sensitivity to the weights was not measured, and the final accuracy criterion is a threshold on held-out accuracy only. The absolute change ||W - W0||_F at the bit-4 time is of similar size at all four scales for the first layer (about 7 to 10; 9.6, 9.6, 7.3, 6.8 for c = 0.5, 1, 2, 4, computed as the median relative change times c times the initial first-layer norm 6.5, which I checked by re-creating the initial weights of the five seeds), so the scale mainly changes how fast the same displacement is reached, not how large it must be; this is a numerical observation from the table above, not a tested explanation.

## C-68 (CONFIRMATORY, follows C-58 and C-66): does a larger initial scale make a_4 XOR b_4 readable from the untrained network?
Idea source: C-66/C-67 (larger initial scale, shorter plateau, smaller relative weight change) and C-58 (at step 0, c = 1, the ridge probe reads p_4 = a_4 XOR b_4 at 0.835 to 0.871 from the best layer). If the larger scale shortens the plateau because the random hidden features already contain p_4, the probe accuracy at step 0 should rise with c.
Design. No training. For c in {0.25, 0.5, 1, 2, 4, 8}, seeds 0-4 (initial weights as in the earlier runs, multiplied by c as in C-66), the C-58 probe (closed-form ridge, lambda 1, standardised features, 5,000 training-split and 5,000 held-out samples from the seed's split) on each of the three hidden layers for p_4, c_4, p_3; report the best layer. Script c68_init_probe.py, output c68/init_probe.csv.
Predictions. (1) the median best-layer accuracy for p_4 at step 0 increases with c over 0.5, 1, 2, 4 (non-decreasing, c = 4 at least 0.03 above c = 1). (2) at c = 4 the median reaches 0.95 (the C-58 formation threshold). If (1) holds and (2) fails, the larger scale helps by something other than making p_4 linearly available.

### C-68 RESULT (no training; c68/init_probe.csv). Median best-layer probe accuracy at step 0 for p_4 / c_4 / p_3 is 0.858 / 0.962 / 0.831 at every c in {0.25, 0.5, 1, 2, 4, 8}: identical to three decimals.
Scoring: (1) MISS (no increase with c). (2) MISS (c = 4 stays at 0.858). 
Why, which I should have seen before running: a ReLU net with all weights and biases multiplied by c has every hidden layer's output multiplied by c^l (positive homogeneity), and the probe standardises its features, so the untrained probe is exactly invariant to c. The test could not have shown a difference; it was a design flaw, not a finding about the net. What it does show: scaling the initial weights by c does not change which functions of the input are linearly available at step 0; it multiplies the initial output logits by c^4 (three hidden layers and the output layer) and changes the training dynamics (sensitivity of the output to each weight, and with Adam a fixed step per weight). So the speed-up of C-66 cannot be explained by 'the random features already contain p_4 more'; it comes through the scale of the output and the gradient, in the same way a larger learning rate or width does (C-35, C-36).

## C-69 (CONFIRMATORY, follows C-66 and C-68): does the initial-scale effect go through the initial logit scale?
Idea source: C-68 (c scales every hidden layer's output by c^l and the logits by c^4, and changes no linear readability). In C-66, c multiplies the three hidden layers and the output layer together. Separate the two: a factor c_h on the three hidden layers (weights and biases) and c_o on the output layer. The initial logits are multiplied by c_h^3 * c_o; with c_h = c_o = 2 that is 16, as in C-66 at c = 2.
Design. C-66 code path (batch 4096, Adam lr 1e-3, lam = 1, 5 seeds, 12,000-step cap, same splits and initial weights) with three settings of (c_h, c_o): A = (2, 1) [logits x8]; B = (1, 8) [logits x8, hidden layers at the default scale]; C = (2, 1/8) [logits x1, hidden layers doubled]. References: (1, 1) = C-65 batch 4096 (median 2250), (2, 2) = C-66 c = 2 (median 450). Script c69_split_scale.py, output c69_<tag>/.
Predictions. (1) A and B have median bit-4 times within a factor 1.5 of each other (if the effect depends on the logit scale only). (2) C has a median at least 3 times that of A (doubling the hidden layers does not help if the logits are kept at the default scale). If (1) fails the effect depends on where the scale sits (hidden features or output), not on the logit scale alone; if (2) fails, the hidden-layer scale itself speeds the plateau.

### C-69 RESULT (joint net, batch 4096, 5 seeds; c69_A, c69_B, c69_C; hidden-layer scale c_h and output-layer scale c_o). Bit-4 time per seed (median):
A (c_h = 2, c_o = 1; initial logits x8): 650 550 400 1100 650 (650)
B (c_h = 1, c_o = 8; initial logits x8): 450 350 300 950 400 (400)
C (c_h = 2, c_o = 1/8; initial logits x1): 2100 2300 800 1500 1300 (1500)
References: (1, 1) 2250 (C-65); (2, 2) [logits x16] 450 (C-66). Full-adder (max over bits) medians, computed from the CSVs: A 750, B 600, C 1550 (an earlier version of this line, written before computing them, gave other numbers and was wrong).
Scoring against the predictions written before the run:
(1) MISS, narrowly: A and B differ by a factor 1.63 (650 against 400; limit 1.5).
(2) MISS: C is 2.3 times A (1500 against 650; limit 3).
Reading. The speed-up comes mostly from the scale of the output layer: multiplying only the output layer by 8 (B) takes the median from 2250 to 400, as much as doubling everything (450). Doubling only the hidden layers while keeping the logits at the default scale (C) helps little (2250 to 1500), and doubling the hidden layers at default output scale (A) gives 650. So the initial logit scale (c_h^3 c_o) orders the results roughly (scale 1: 2250 and 1500; scale 8: 650 and 400; scale 16: 450) but not exactly, and the hidden-layer scale has a smaller extra effect. A plausible reading (not tested): a larger output layer multiplies how much a given change of the hidden features changes the logits, i.e. it acts like a larger learning rate for the hidden layers (consistent with C-36 and with Adam's fixed per-weight step). Next: C-70 compares this with a direct hidden-layer learning-rate multiplier.

## C-70 (CONFIRMATORY, follows C-69 and C-36): is the output-layer-scale effect a larger effective learning rate for the hidden layers?
Idea source: C-69 (output layer x8 alone takes the joint bit-4 median from 2250 to 400) and C-36 (a larger learning rate shortens the plateau). Reading to test: a larger output layer multiplies how much a given hidden-feature change moves the logits, which is what a larger hidden-layer learning rate does under Adam.
Design. C-65 batch-4096 joint code path (default initial weights, c_h = c_o = 1, lam = 1, 5 seeds, 12,000-step cap, same splits and batch orders) with the learning rate of the three hidden layers (weights and biases) multiplied by k in {2, 4, 8} and the output layer's learning rate kept at 1e-3 (Adam, one parameter group each). k = 1 is C-65 (median 2250). Script c70_hidden_lr.py, output c70_k<k>/.
Predictions. (1) the median bit-4 time is non-increasing in k over 1, 2, 4, 8. (2) at k = 8 it is at most 800 (at least 2.8 times faster than k = 1). (3) at k = 8 it is within a factor 2 of the output-layer-x8 value from C-69 (400), i.e. between 200 and 800. If (2) holds but (3) fails, the output-layer scale works through something other than the hidden-layer step size.

### C-70 RESULT (joint net, batch 4096, hidden-layer learning rate multiplied by k, output layer at 1e-3, default initial weights; 5 seeds; c70_k*/hiddenlr_k*_n8.csv). Bit-4 time per seed (median); max over bits (median):
k = 1 (C-65): 2700 2900 650 1800 2250 (2250); 2450
k = 2: 2600 1850 300 1200 1600 (1600); 1700
k = 4: 1350 1450 500 1450 1200 (1350); 1350
k = 8: 950 1150 300 800 650 (800); 800
Scoring against the predictions written before the run:
(1) PASS: 2250, 1600, 1350, 800 is non-increasing in k.
(2) PASS, on the boundary: k = 8 gives 800 (limit 800).
(3) PASS, on the boundary: 800 is within a factor 2 of the output-layer-x8 value 400 (range 200 to 800).
Reading. A larger hidden-layer learning rate shortens the joint bit-4 time gradually (about 1.4 times for k = 2, 1.7 for k = 4, 2.8 for k = 8), which is less than the 5.6 times from scaling the output layer by 8 (C-69 B: 400) and from doubling all layers (C-66: 450). So the output-layer scale acts like a larger hidden-layer step size, as hypothesised, but more strongly than a learning-rate multiplier of the same nominal size. Together C-35, C-36, C-66, C-69 and C-70 show a single pattern: whatever increases how fast the logits respond to changes of the hidden features (width, learning rate, initial scale, output scale) shortens the joint plateau. The mechanism behind that pattern (why the response speed matters for the joint net more than for bit 4 alone) is not isolated. Caveats: 5 seeds; n = 8; batch 4,096 only; boundary passes for (2) and (3); the comparison to the output-layer scale is between a 8x learning rate and a x8 initial scale, which are not the same quantity.

## C-71 (CONFIRMATORY, follows C-61, C-63, C-66): does Adam's dilution factor rise with the initial scale?
Idea source: C-61 (dilution factor s about 0.24 at default settings, batch 256) and C-66 (joint/alone time ratio falls from 22 at c = 1 to 3.0 at c = 2 and 1.7 at c = 4, batch 4096). The dilution model of C-61 says the slowdown caused by Adam's second-moment is about 1/s, so the ratios 3.0 and 1.7 require s of about 0.33 and 0.6. If instead s stays near 0.24 at larger c, the model cannot hold there.
Design. C-61 bookkeeping (separate gradients of bit 4's loss and of the other eight outputs on the shared layers, running second moments v_4 and v_total with beta2 = 0.999, bias-corrected, average of sqrt(v_4 / v_total) over parameters) at batch 4096, lam = 1, initial scale c in {1, 2, 4} (all weights and biases multiplied by c as in C-66), seeds 0-4, 12,000-step cap, stopping when bit 4 is confirmed. Reference times T0 (bit 4 alone, batch 4096) from C-65/C-66: c = 1: 100 100 100 100 150; c = 2: 150 each; c = 4: 350 300 300 350 400. Script c71_dilution_scale.py, output c71_c<c>/.
Predictions. (1) the median of s over the first 500 steps (or until the run stops) increases with c over 1, 2, 4. (2) s at c = 4 is at least 0.45. (3) the median I(T)/T0 is within 0.67 to 1.5 at c = 2 and at c = 4; at c = 1 it is at least 3 (batch 4096, default scale). A miss on (2) with the ratios of C-66 would mean the second-moment dilution is not what limits the joint net at larger scale.

### C-71 RESULT (joint net, batch 4096, initial scale c, dilution bookkeeping; 5 seeds; c71_c*/dilution_c*_n8.csv, logs c71_c*.log). The bit-4 times reproduce C-65/C-66 exactly (c = 1: 2700 2900 650 1800 2250; c = 2: 450 350 350 700 550; c = 4: 550 550 600 650 700).
Median s over the steps up to 500 (or to the stop): c = 1: 0.204 | c = 2: 0.326 | c = 4: 0.320.
Accumulated progress I(T) per seed: c = 1: 1029 1101 222 627 786 | c = 2: 161 102 117 227 199 | c = 4: 201 175 163 208 297.
I(T) / T0 (T0 from C-65/C-66): c = 1: 10.3 11.0 2.2 6.3 5.2 (median 6.3) | c = 2: 1.08 0.68 0.78 1.52 1.33 (median 1.08) | c = 4: 0.57 0.58 0.54 0.59 0.74 (median 0.58). Joint / alone time ratio: 18 (c = 1), 3.0 (c = 2), 1.83 (c = 4); 1/s: 4.9, 3.1, 3.1.
Scoring against the predictions written before the run:
(1) MISS: s does not increase over 1, 2, 4 (0.204, 0.326, 0.320; flat between 2 and 4).
(2) MISS: s at c = 4 is 0.32 (limit 0.45).
(3) PARTLY: I/T0 is within the band at c = 2 (1.08), is at least 3 at c = 1 (6.3), but is below the band at c = 4 (0.58, limit 0.67).
Reading. With a larger initial scale the dilution factor changes only from 0.20 to about 0.32, but the joint/alone ratio collapses from 18 to 3.0 and 1.8; at c = 2 the whole ratio is what the dilution model gives (3.0 against 1/s = 3.1), at c = 4 the joint net is even less slowed than the model says (the net trained on bit 4 alone becomes slower at c = 4, 350 steps against 100 to 150). This repeats the pattern of C-63 (width): the dilution part stays at about 3 to 5 times for every knob tried, and what the knobs remove is the extra part that the dilution does not explain (I/T0: 6.3, 1.1, 0.6). The model I(T) assumes progress proportional to s; it is a simple model and not a measured mechanism. Caveats: 5 seeds; s is an average over parameters of a ratio of running second moments; for c = 2 and 4 the runs stop within 350 to 700 steps, so the 500-step median includes the whole run.

## C-72 (CONFIRMATORY, follows C-66 and C-71): does the initial-scale effect hold at n = 12?
Idea source: C-66/C-67/C-71 (n = 8): a larger initial weight scale shortens the joint plateau 4 to 5 times and removes most of the extra slowdown. Generality check at n = 12, where the C-14 Adam runs (default scale, 10 seeds) have a large hump: 16 of 130 bit-times censored at 20,000 steps, every one of the 10 seeds has at least one censored bit, median per-bit times (censored counted as 20,000): 200 700 2600 20000 20000 17800 15200 11600 8600 10900 5500 2600 1100.
Design. The C-14 pipeline unchanged (n = 12, 3x128 ReLU, summed BCE, Adam lr 1e-3, batch 256 of fresh pairs with the evaluation panel excluded, 20,000 steps, evaluation every 200 steps, seeds 0-9) with every initial weight and bias multiplied by c in {2, 4}. c = 1 is the existing C-14 run (a check run of seed 0 at c = 1 will reproduce its times). Script c72_scale12.py (c14 with env C), output c72_c<c>/.
Predictions. (1) at c = 4 at most 2 of the 10 seeds have any censored bit (C-14: 10 of 10). (2) at c = 4 the median over seeds of the largest bit-time is at most 10,000. (3) the number of censored bit-times is smaller at c = 2 than at c = 1 (16 of 130) and larger than or equal to the number at c = 4. A miss on (1) and (2) would mean the n = 8 initial-scale effect does not carry over to n = 12 at this learning rate and batch size.

### C-72 RESULT (n = 12, Adam lr 1e-3, batch 256, 20,000 steps, seeds 0-9; c72_c2, c72_c4, c72_c1check; logs c72_*.log; c = 1 is C-14). The check run (c = 1, seed 0) reproduces the C-14 times exactly.
Censored bit-times (of 130): c = 1: 16 | c = 2: 0 | c = 4: 0. Seeds with any censored bit: 10 | 0 | 0.
Per-seed largest bit-time: c = 2: 8000 5800 5400 8000 8800 5400 7200 6600 7200 10800 (median 7200) | c = 4: 4800 4200 3800 5400 5600 4400 5200 5800 4600 4200 (median 4700) | c = 1: all 10 censored at 20,000.
Median per bit (bits 0..12): c = 1 (censored counted as 20,000): 200 700 2600 20000 20000 17800 15200 11600 8600 10900 5500 2600 1100 | c = 2: 200 600 800 1300 1900 3000 6600 4200 5400 5500 3000 1600 800 | c = 4: 600 1400 1800 2200 2700 3200 3800 3700 3800 3100 3100 2200 1600.
Scoring against the predictions written before the run:
(1) PASS: 0 of 10 seeds have a censored bit at c = 4 (limit 2).
(2) PASS: the median of the largest bit-time at c = 4 is 4,700 (limit 10,000).
(3) PASS: censored bit-times are 0 at c = 2 (fewer than 16) and equal to the 0 at c = 4.
Reading. At n = 12 the larger initial scale takes the full adder from not finished in 20,000 steps in every seed to finished in 4,700 steps (c = 4) or 7,200 (c = 2). The shape changes: at c = 4 the profile is a broad plateau of 3,000 to 3,800 steps over bits 5 to 10 with the lowest bits slower than before (bit 0: 600 against 200), no more a sharp hump at bits 3 to 5. So the n = 8 result carries over to n = 12 in size and direction. Caveats: 10 seeds; one learning rate and batch size; larger c slows the lowest bits and the bit-4-alone net at n = 8, so 'larger is better' is not claimed beyond c = 4; the final accuracy criterion is the usual 0.99 threshold on a panel of 8,192 pairs.

## INTAKE (2026-10-26, 09:04): results returned from the separate chat that ran the handoff pack (why_research_all.zip, 32 MB, 173 files)
Handling. The archive was treated as untrusted data: copied to its own directory, listed before extraction, extracted with a path check, the 90 pickled model files (.pt) not extracted and not loaded, no script from it run. Only data files and text were read.
VERIFIED BY ME (my own comparison with my stored results):
- the five scripts they ran (c12, c18, c44, c49, c58) and RUN.md are byte-identical to the ones I sent (sha256 prefix compared);
- their raw CSVs reproduce my medians exactly: c12 per-bit 100 200 200 200 300 400 600 800 700; c18 bit 4 by weight 2600/500/300/300; c44 700/2500/300/2900/1800; c49 slowest bit 900 and 5800 (tolerance was factor 1.5 or 300 steps; the difference is 0);
- their full c58 run (10,980 rows) is identical to mine in every row: maximum difference of probe accuracy 0.0 and of bit-4 accuracy 0.0; p4 formation times and bit-4 times per seed are the same lists as in C-58. So C-58 is independently reproduced on another machine (their environment: Python 3.12.14, torch 2.14.1+cpu, Xeon Platinum 8573C; mine differs). Their wall times (c12 59 s, c18 601 s, c44 442 s, c49 239 s, c58 562 s) are on a shared container.
NOT VERIFIED BY ME (their own extension, matched-head experiment, 3 seeds, 2,000 steps, same nine-output head with masked losses): bit-4 times, seeds 0/1/2: only bit 4 200/200/300; bits 0-4 900/600/500; bits 4-8 1300/1800/900; all bits not confirmed/not confirmed/900; all bits with weight 10 on bit 4 300/300/300; detached loss-proportional weights 1400/not confirmed/800. I did not run their script. By eye these agree with my results (C-59 lam = 0: 200 200 250; C-58 lower/upper: 450 to 2200 and 1150 to 2300; C-59 lam = 1 seeds 0-2: 4400, 5250, 1000, i.e. not confirmed by 2,000 steps for seeds 0 and 1; C-18 weight 10: 300; C-38/C-22: adaptive weights not consistent in short runs). Their gradient-cosine logs also show negative cosines in both fast and slow conditions (as C-17: cosines between -0.05 and +0.13).
Their statements worth keeping in mind (they are caveats about my own measurements too): (i) the time-to-0.99 statistic is not exact first passage, permanent convergence, whole-sum accuracy or evidence of a complete adder (their derivation: a truncated carry computation can give 99.6 percent on bit 7 while missing valid carry cases); (ii) a linear probe does not show causal use; (iii) the c49 random-parity condition has one run with two unconfirmed bits (seed 3), kept censored.
LITERATURE LEADS from SOURCES.md (S01 to S18 plus the runtime note T01): all remain UNVERIFIED LEADS except the three I opened myself through WebFetch (the tool returns excerpts and a summary, so my reading depth is limited to what is quoted):
- Chizat, Oyallon, Bach 2019, "On Lazy Training in Differentiable Programming" (arXiv:1812.07956, NeurIPS 2019): as returned by the page reader, Section 1.1 says essentially any parametric model can be trained in a lazy regime "if its output is close to zero at initialization"; Section 1.2 gives for rescaled models a criterion that "decreases as alpha^-1 when alpha grows", leading to "lazy training for large alpha", and for homogeneous models "such networks thus enter the lazy regime as the variance of initialization increases"; Section 2.1 says the dynamics has a proper time parameterisation with the objective scaled by 1/alpha^2; Theorem 2.2 gives parameter movement O(1/alpha). Relevance: the direction of my C-66/C-67/C-69 results (larger output or initial scale, smaller relative weight change, shorter plateau) is the one this theory predicts for a model near its linearisation; it does not apply directly because my scaled networks do not have an output close to zero at initialisation (the logits are multiplied by c^4), and it supplies no formula for the Adam plateau.
- Yu et al. 2020, "Gradient Surgery for Multi-Task Learning" (arXiv:2001.06782, NeurIPS 2020): Section 2.2 says "conflicting gradients are not detrimental on their own" and names, besides conflict, large differences in gradient magnitudes (some task gradients dominating) and high curvature as conditions that make interference harmful. Relevance: consistent with C-17 (nearly orthogonal gradients) and with the gradient-share result C-59 and the dilution measurement C-61 (the magnitude condition), though PCGrad is not tested here.
- Barak et al. 2022, "Hidden Progress in Deep Learning" (arXiv:2207.08799, NeurIPS 2022): the page reader's excerpts say that in sparse-parity learning SGD makes gradual hidden progress that loss and error metrics do not show ("SGD gradually amplifies the sparse solution via a Fourier gap in the population gradient"; sudden jumps after roughly n^O(k) iterations). Relevance: the notion of a plateau with invisible progress that I can look for in my own probe data; the setting (SGD, sparse parity, not an Adam multi-output adder) is different.
Not opened by me and therefore still leads: S03 GradNorm, S04 uncertainty weighting, S05 DWA, S06 Huq and Pervin, S08 gradient starvation, S10 scaling exponents, S11 parity curriculum, S12 to S18 (addition constructions, transformer addition, soft labels, Rao-Blackwell, probe controls, intermediate supervision).
Their suggestion of a next test that I had not yet run: separate Adam moments per task (their next test 2). Planned as C-73.

### C-58 follow-up, EXPLORATORY (no prediction written; prompted by the idea of hidden progress in Barak et al. and by the returned report's remark that the final-layer p4 probe is not monotone; analysis of the stored joint-net probe data only, no new runs). Best-layer p4 probe accuracy (bit-4 held-out accuracy in brackets) of the joint net at steps 0 / 300 / 1000 / 2000 / 4000:
seed 0: 0.858 (0.505) / 0.775 (0.505) / 0.771 (0.509) / 0.799 (0.596) / 0.892 (0.958)
seed 1: 0.871 (0.500) / 0.810 (0.502) / 0.803 (0.513) / 0.843 (0.570) / 0.998 (0.995)
seed 2: 0.868 (0.502) / 0.868 (0.612) / 0.998 (0.996) / 0.996 (1.000) / 0.998 (1.000)
seed 3: 0.845 (0.500) / 0.776 (0.501) / 0.819 (0.540) / 0.962 (0.968) / 0.975 (1.000)
seed 4: 0.835 (0.498) / 0.752 (0.499) / 0.805 (0.599) / 0.910 (0.958) / 0.984 (1.000)
Observation. In four of the five seeds (0, 1, 3, 4) the best-layer p4 readability first FALLS by about 0.05 to 0.11 during the first 300 to 500 steps (to 0.75 to 0.81), stays low while bit 4 is at chance, and rises only shortly before bit 4 is solved; the fast seed (2) does not dip (0.868 at step 300) and p4 reaches 0.997 by step 700, when bit 4 is at 0.964. So in these runs the plateau of bit 4 is not accompanied by a steady rise of the p4 probe (no hidden progress of that kind), and the readability that random features carry at step 0 is partly lost when the shared layers are trained on the other bits. The size of the dip does not rank the seeds by bit-4 time (Spearman 0.3 between the minimum over the first 1,000 steps and the order of bit-4 times; five seeds). Limits: five seeds, one linear probe family, no intervention; a drop in linear readability does not show that the feature is gone from the representation.

## C-73 (CONFIRMATORY, follows C-61 and C-64; the idea of separate moments per task is also the returned report's next test 2, which I had planned independently as the direct causal test of the dilution account)
Question. If Adam's shared second moment (dilution, C-61) were removed, how much of the joint-net slowdown of bit 4 remains? C-61 and C-63 say the dilution accounts for about 3 to 5 times of the 10-fold slowdown at default settings, and an extra part (I/T0 about 3 at width 128) remains.
Design. The C-59 lam = 1 joint net (n = 8, 3x128 ReLU, batch 256, same splits, initial weights and batch order, seeds 0-4, 6,000-step cap, evaluation every 50 steps) trained with a modified optimizer: for the shared (hidden-layer) parameters each of the nine outputs has its own Adam first and second moments (beta 1 = 0.9, beta 2 = 0.999, epsilon 1e-8, bias-corrected) computed from the gradient of that output's batch-mean BCE, and the update is the sum over outputs of m_k / (sqrt(v_k) + eps), times lr 1e-3, times a factor f. The output layer (each of whose rows receives only one output's gradient) is trained with the ordinary Adam. Two variants: S1 f = 1 (each task's own step as large as if it were alone), S3 f = 1/3 (= 1/sqrt(9): the dilution that equal and orthogonal gradients would give in ordinary Adam). Reference: the ordinary joint net (C-59 lam = 1: 4400 5250 1000 2450 2550, median 2550) and bit 4 alone (200 200 250 250 300, median 250). Script c73_sepmoments.py, output c73_f<f>/.
Predictions. (1) S1: the median bit-4 time is at most 1275 (half of the ordinary joint net). (2) S1: the median bit-4 time is at least 500 (twice the single-bit time): the extra part is not removed. (3) S3: the median bit-4 time is within 0.5 to 1.5 times the ordinary joint net (1275 to 3825). A miss on (2) (S1 below 500) would mean the dilution is the whole mechanism at this setting; a miss on (1) would mean the shared second moment is not what slows bit 4.

RESULT C-73 (5 seeds each, n = 8, 6,000-step cap, no censored bit-4 time). Bit-4 times, seeds 0-4:
- S1 (f = 1, separate moments): 800 1200 850 1250 900, median 900. Median of every bit: 100 350 750 850 900 1100 1400 750 350; full adder (largest bit time per seed) 1400 1400 1850 1250 1650, median 1400.
- S3 (f = 1/3, separate moments): 4350 2750 1750 2900 3050, median 2900. Median of every bit: 150 1050 1400 1800 2900 3050 2850 950 550; largest bit time per seed 4350 4100 2700 3050 3100, median 3100.
- References (C-59): ordinary joint 4400 5250 1000 2450 2550 (median 2550); bit 4 alone 200 200 250 250 300 (median 250).
SCORING. (1) S1 median at most 1275: 900, PASS. (2) S1 median at least 500: 900, PASS (the extra part is not removed). (3) S3 within 1275 to 3825: 2900, PASS. No missed prediction.
Reading. Giving each output its own second moment in the shared layers and letting each take its full step takes bit 4 from 2550 to 900 steps (2.8 times faster) but not down to the 250 of the single-bit net: 3.6 times the single-bit time remains, the same size as the residual factor found from the dilution integral (C-63: I/T0 about 3 at width 128). With the step cut to one third, the time (2900) is about that of the ordinary joint net (2550), i.e. in this comparison the ordinary Adam joint net behaves like separate moments with each task's step reduced by about a factor of three, which is what the dilution factor of C-61 (about 0.24 to 0.33) measured. So the causal intervention agrees with the two-part account: the shared second moment costs a factor of about 3 (900 to 2550-2900), and a factor of about 3.6 is left that separate moments do not remove. Caveats: 5 seeds; the median ranking 2550 against 2900 is within the seed spread (ordinary joint 1000 to 5250), so 'same as ordinary' means 'not distinguishable at 5 seeds'; with f = 1 the total shared-layer step is up to 9 times larger than in ordinary Adam, so S1 also tests a larger effective learning rate on the shared layers (C-35/C-36 and C-70 say that a larger learning rate alone shortens the plateau), so the S1 gain is not purely 'dilution removed'; f = 1/3 is one value; the bit profile at S3 has bits 2 to 6 slow (1400 to 3050) like the ordinary joint net. Nothing here identifies what the remaining part is.

## C-74 (CONFIRMATORY, follows C-63 and C-73): is the part that separate moments do not remove a width effect?
Question. C-73 (width 128) leaves about 3.6 times the single-bit time after the shared second moment is removed (900 against 250). C-63 found that the residual factor I/T0 falls with width (11 at 64, 3 at 128, 1.7 at 256, 1.0 at 512). If the residual is a property of the shared layers' size, then with separate moments (S1, f = 1, same script with the width changed, c74_sepwidth.py) the bit-4 time should come close to the single-bit time at width 512 and be much slower than 900 at width 64.
References (C-63, ordinary Adam, joint): W=64 11400 10800 11150 and two seeds not finished in 12,000; W=512 650 450 600 400 500. Bit 4 alone: W=64 300 300 300 400 550; W=512 150 150 250 150 150. Seeds 0-4, evaluation every 50 steps, cap 6,000 steps.
Predictions (written before the runs). (1) W=512, f = 1: median bit-4 time at most 300 (twice the alone median 150). (2) W=64, f = 1: median bit-4 time at least 1,800 (twice the W=128 value 900, counting a seed not finished in 6,000 steps as at least 6,000). A miss on (1) would mean width does not remove the residual once the moments are separate (the residual would be something other than shared-layer size); a miss on (2) would mean that the width dependence found in C-63 belonged to the moment pooling.

RESULT C-74 (separate moments, f = 1, seeds 0-4, cap 6,000 steps; c74_w64/, c74_w512/, logs c74_w64.log, c74_w512.log). Bit-4 times:
- W=64: 3200 5950 1850 3300 2250, median 3200 (no bit-4 censored; other bits not finished in 6,000 steps: bit 5 in seed 0, bits 3 and 6 in seed 1, bit 6 in seeds 2 and 3). Full-width reference: ordinary joint net 11400 10800 11150 and two not finished at 12,000.
- W=512: 300 300 350 350 350, median 350; every bit in every seed finishes by 400 steps (bit 0 at 50 to 200). Ordinary joint net: 650 450 600 400 500 (median 500); bit 4 alone 150 150 250 150 150 (median 150).
- W=128 (C-73, S1): 800 1200 850 1250 900, median 900.
SCORING. (1) W=512 median at most 300: 350, MISS (by one evaluation step; the ratio to the alone median is 2.3 where I had allowed 2.0). (2) W=64 median at least 1,800: 3,200, PASS.
Reading. With separate moments the bit-4 time still depends strongly on width: median 3200, 900, 350 at W = 64, 128, 512 (alone: 300, 250, 150), i.e. 11, 3.6 and 2.3 times the alone time. So the part that remains after the shared second moment is removed is mostly a width effect (that is what C-63 found for the residual of the dilution integral), supporting that the cause of the second part is related to the size of the shared layers, not to Adam's pooled moment. The miss: at W=512 separate moments with full steps gain little on the ordinary joint net (500 to 350), although the dilution integral at W=512 (C-63: I/T0 = 0.99) had said that dilution accounts for all of the slowdown there (500 against 150); so the integral model overpredicts what removing the pooled moment gains (or the nine separately normalised full-size updates, which are added together, interfere or overshoot in a way the model does not include). I did not test which. Caveats: 5 seeds; times are on a 50-step grid (the W=512 times are within 1 to 2 grid steps of each other); same lr 1e-3 at every width; with f = 1 the total shared-layer step is larger than in ordinary Adam.

## C-75 (EXPLORATORY-WITH-PREDICTION, follows C-63, C-74; idea: if the width-dependent part of the bit-4 delay is the time to form a_4 XOR b_4, wider random nets should already carry more of it at initialisation)
Design. Untrained 3-layer ReLU nets (default PyTorch init, same seeds and splits as C-58/C-68) of widths 64, 128, 256, 512, 1024; ridge probe (penalty 1.0, standardised features) on 20,000 training pairs, accuracy on 20,000 other held-out pairs, for p4 = a_4 XOR b_4, c4 (carry into bit 4) and p3; best layer per net; seeds 0-4. Script c75_width_probe.py, output c75/. No training, so this measures only what random features carry.
Predictions (written before running). (1) the median best-layer p4 accuracy rises with width over 64, 128, 512, 1024 (strictly), and (2) W=1024 exceeds W=64 by at least 0.05. If this fails, the width effect on the plateau does not come from random features already carrying p4.

RESULT C-75 (untrained nets, best layer, median of 5 seeds; per-seed values in c75/init_probe.csv). Held-out probe accuracy for p4 = a_4 XOR b_4 by width 64 / 128 / 256 / 512 / 1024: 0.722 / 0.861 / 0.970 / 0.9995 / 1.000 (per-layer medians for p4: layer 1 0.722 0.861 0.970 1.000 1.000; layer 2 0.677 0.802 0.929 0.996 1.000; layer 3 0.646 0.768 0.883 0.980 0.999, so the first layer is best). p3 is the same (0.701 / 0.827 / 0.968 / 0.9994 / 1.000). The carry into bit 4 (c4): 0.947 / 0.969 / 0.964 / 0.969 / 0.977. Baseline with the same ridge probe on the 16 raw input bits (c75_baseline.py, two splits): p4 0.493, p3 0.498 (chance), c4 1.000 (carry is a linear threshold of the input bits: the weighted sum of the low bits is at least 16).
SCORING. (1) strictly rising median with width over 64, 128, 512, 1024: PASS. (2) W=1024 minus W=64 at least 0.05: PASS (0.28).
Reading. An untrained random ReLU layer of width 512 or more already makes a_4 XOR b_4 almost perfectly linearly readable (the XOR needs a non-linear feature that the raw bits do not give), a narrow one (64) only partly (0.72). Bit 4 is p4 XOR c4 with c4 a linear threshold of the inputs, so the width dependence of the plateau (C-35, C-63, C-74) is consistent with the idea that narrow nets must first build the non-linear p4 feature while wide ones carry it from the start. Not shown: (a) the probe has 20,000 training pairs and the net never sees that many labelled p4 targets, so readability is an upper bound on what is easy to use; (b) readability does not show that the trained net uses it; (c) at width 512 the joint net still needs 500 steps against 150 alone (C-63), so something besides p4 availability remains; (d) the same readability would predict a short plateau for wide nets in the single-bit case too, which is what C-35 found, but I have not run a net where the p4 features are removed or scrambled at width 512 to test the cause. The p4 time dependence on width has therefore a candidate explanation, not a tested one. Idea source: the question 'why does width remove the extra part' from C-74; lazy-training literature (Chizat et al., opened) says wide or large-scale nets stay close to their linearisation, in which case the random features are what is used.

## C-76 (CONFIRMATORY, follows C-75; tests whether the early availability of a_4 XOR b_4 in the random features predicts the bit-4 time when the widths of the three layers differ)
Idea source: C-75 (random width-512 layers already carry p4), the lazy-training lead (random features are used by wide nets). Test of a prediction made from the initial-feature account against the alternative that the width of every layer (capacity) is what counts.
Design. The ordinary joint net of C-59 (lam = 1, Adam 1e-3, batch 256, seeds 0-4, same splits, 12,000-step cap, evaluation every 50 steps), but with layer widths that differ: (512,64,64), (64,512,512), (64,64,512). The untrained best-layer p4 readability of each (c76_probe_configs.py, same method as C-75, output c76/init_probe.csv, medians of 5 seeds, computed before training): (512,64,64) 0.9995 (layer 1 1.000, layer 2 0.712, layer 3 0.673); (64,512,512) 0.955 (layer 1 0.722, layer 2 0.955, layer 3 0.946); (64,64,512) 0.833 (0.722, 0.677, 0.833); uniform 128 0.861 and uniform 512 0.9995 (C-75) with joint bit-4 times 2550 and 500 (C-63, C-59), uniform 64 about 11,000 or longer (C-63).
Predictions (written before training). Under the account 'early availability of p4 in some layer sets the plateau': (1) (512,64,64) median bit-4 time at most 1275 (half of uniform 128's 2550), (2) (64,64,512) median at least 2550 (its best readability, 0.833, is below uniform 128's 0.861), (3) (64,512,512) median below that of (64,64,512). Under the alternative ('every layer needs width' / capacity), (512,64,64) would be slow, in the range of uniform 64 (above 5000). A miss on (1) with a time above 5000 favours the alternative.

RESULT C-76 (ordinary joint Adam, seeds 0-4, 12,000-step cap, no bit-4 censored; folders c76_512_64_64, c76_64_512_512, c76_64_64_512, logs of the same names). Bit-4 times, sorted:
- (512,64,64): 1950 2150 2550 2850 3100, median 2550; largest bit time per seed, median 2550.
- (64,512,512): 650 1000 1350 1400 1500, median 1350; largest-bit median 1350.
- (64,64,512): 3450 3950 4750 6450 7100, median 4750; largest-bit median 5750.
- References from other runs: uniform 512 median 500 (C-63), uniform 128 median 2550 (C-59), uniform 64 about 11,000 or longer, two of five not finished at 12,000 (C-63; different script, same method).
SCORING. (1) (512,64,64) median at most 1275: 2550, MISS. (2) (64,64,512) median at least 2550: 4750, PASS. (3) (64,512,512) below (64,64,512): 1350 against 4750, PASS. The 'alternative' (every layer needs width, (512,64,64) in the range of uniform 64 above 5000) is also not supported: 2550 is far from 11,000.
Reading. The time is not set by the first layer's width alone (512,64,64 = 2550, the same as uniform 128 although its first layer carries p4 perfectly at initialisation) and not by the last layer's width alone ((64,64,512) = 4750, slower than uniform 128); a wide second and third layer on top of a narrow first one (64,512,512) is clearly better than uniform 128 (1350). So the first layer's random p4 readability (C-75) is not sufficient: the narrow later layers (64) of (512,64,64) have p4 readability 0.71 and 0.67 at initialisation, i.e. they discard it. Descriptive rank agreement across the six width patterns between the joint bit-4 time (500, 1350, 2550, 2550, 4750, about 11,000; shorter is better) and the untrained p4 readability: best layer 0.84, the last hidden layer (the one the head reads) 0.81, the first layer 0.55 (Spearman, six patterns, with reference times taken from other runs; not a significance test). So the account 'p4 must be available in the layers that feed on to the head' fits better than 'p4 available in the first layer', but it is not exact (rank 0.84; (64,64,512) vs 128-128-128 are inverted in the last-layer measure) and it is a correlation over six width patterns, not an intervention. Predictions scored 2 of 3.

## C-77 (CONFIRMATORY, follows C-75, C-76; an intervention on which layer's p4 availability counts)
Question. C-76 left two readings: (L) what counts is whether p4 is readable at the layers that feed the head, or (F) it is enough that the random p4 feature exists anywhere upstream (the trainable layers can pass it on). Design: the input goes through a FROZEN random ReLU layer of width 1024 (default init, not trained; p4 readable from it with accuracy about 1.0 by C-75), followed by the trainable 3 x 128 ReLU net of C-59 (default init for those layers, otherwise as C-59: Adam 1e-3, batch 256, seeds 0-4, splits and batch order as before, 12,000-step cap). Joint (lam = 1) and bit-4-alone (lam = 0, gradient of the other eight outputs cut from the trunk, as C-59). Script c77_frozen.py, output c77_joint/ and c77_alone/ (FW=1024).
Untrained p4 readability of the trainable layers on top of the frozen layer (ridge probe as C-75, median of 5 seeds, computed before training): layer 1 0.832, layer 2 0.764, layer 3 (the one the head reads) 0.726. The uniform 128 net has 0.861, 0.802, 0.768; so under (L) no gain is expected, under (F) a large gain.
References: uniform 128 joint 4400 5250 1000 2450 2550 (median 2550); alone 200 200 250 250 300 (median 250).
Predictions (written before the runs). Under (L) (my reading of C-76): (1) joint median bit-4 time at least 1275 (no halving of 2550). (2) joint median at least 4 times the alone median. A joint median at most 1275 would favour (F).

RESULT C-77 (seeds 0-4, 12,000-step cap, none censored; logs c77_joint.log, c77_alone.log, folders c77_joint/, c77_alone/). Bit-4 times: joint 4450 7150 5900 6900 8550 (median 6900); alone 200 200 200 200 150 (median 200). Other joint bit medians: bit 0 100, 1 400, 2 1500, 3 3000, 5 6800, 6 3000, 7 1550, 8 450; the full adder (largest bit time per seed) 6100 7150 6800 8050 10100, median 7150. Reference: uniform 128 joint 2550, alone 250.
SCORING. (1) joint median at least 1275: 6900, PASS (by a wide margin: it is 2.7 times SLOWER than the plain 128 net). (2) joint at least 4 times alone: 34.5 times, PASS.
Reading. Putting a frozen random width-1024 layer in front of the trainable net makes the joint bit 4 slower (6900 against 2550) while bit 4 alone is not changed (200 against 250): so reading (F) 'it is enough that the p4 feature exists upstream' is rejected, and reading (L) is consistent with the prediction but not shown by it: the trainable layers on top of the frozen layer had lower untrained last-layer p4 readability (0.726) than the plain net (0.768), and the joint net is slower. Together with C-76 this says the width benefit is not 'random features exist somewhere in the net'. I do not know why the frozen wide layer hurts: candidates not tested are that the first trainable layer now has 1024 inputs per unit (more parameters per unit and a different gradient scale under Adam), that the frozen features are redundant and give the other eight bits more shared directions to compete over, or that the trainable layers must unlearn the random mixing. Caveats: one frozen width (1024), default init for the frozen layer, 5 seeds; no gradient or dilution measurement was made for this architecture.

## C-78 (EXPLORATORY-WITH-PREDICTION, follows C-77): how does the harm of frozen random features depend on their width?
Design. As the joint arm of C-77 (lam = 1, 5 seeds, 12,000-step cap) with the frozen layer's width FW = 128 and 512 (FW = 1024 is C-77: median 6900; no frozen layer = 2550). Output c78_fw128/, c78_fw512/.
Predictions (written before the runs). (1) the median bit-4 time rises with the frozen width: FW=128 < FW=512 < 6900. (2) FW=128 median at least 2550 (a frozen layer never helps).

RESULT C-78 (joint, 5 seeds, 12,000-step cap; logs c78_fw128.log, c78_fw512.log). Bit-4 times: FW=128: 8300 6700 6900 5600 6850 (median 6850); FW=512: 5600 6250 4500 4300 5800 (median 5600; in seed 1 bit 5 was not finished); FW=1024 (C-77): median 6900; no frozen layer: 2550.
SCORING. (1) FW=128 < FW=512 < 6900: MISS (6850 > 5600; the three frozen widths are within 5600 to 6900 with no trend). (2) FW=128 at least 2550: PASS.
Reading. The slowdown does not depend on the frozen layer's width over 128 to 1024. So the harm of C-77 is not about having many random features (nor protection by them); something about adding a frozen random layer at all (for example the extra depth of the composed net, or the random mixing before the trainable layers) costs a factor of about 2 to 3. This is checked next by training a plain 4-layer 128 net.

## C-79 (CONFIRMATORY, follows C-78): is the harm of the frozen layer a depth effect?
Design.
