Plain joint net (all layers trainable, Adam 1e-3, batch 256, seeds 0-4, 12,000-step cap) with four hidden layers of 128 (c76_mixed.py with WS=128-128-128-128, output c79_4x128/), and, if the first result is not clear, also 2 hidden layers of 128. Reference: three layers 2550; frozen layer + three trainable layers 6850 (FW=128).
Predictions (written before the run). If the harm of C-77/C-78 comes from depth: 4 x 128 trainable median bit-4 at least 4000. If it comes from the layer being frozen: at most 3825 (1.5 times the three-layer 2550). Between the two the result is read as not decisive.

RESULT C-79 (plain joint nets, seeds 0-4, 12,000-step cap; c79_4x128/, c79_2x128/). Bit-4 times, sorted: 4 x 128: 1750 1800 2450 2900 4650 (median 2450; full adder per seed 3000 4200 4650 6300 7800, median 4650); 2 x 128: 1800 2450 4400 5000 7900 (median 4400; full adder median 5500). Reference 3 x 128: 2550; frozen layer + 3 x 128: 6850.
SCORING. 4 x 128 median 2450 is below the 3825 limit of the 'frozen' reading and far below the 4000 limit of the 'depth' reading: the depth account is NOT supported; the harm of C-77/C-78 is specific to the layer being frozen and random in front of the net. The extra control (2 layers) gives 4400, so depth is not a monotone factor either: 4400, 2550, 2450 for 2, 3, 4 layers (spread within a configuration is 1750 to 7900, so the order of the medians is not established with 5 seeds).
Reading. A fixed random ReLU layer before the trainable net slows the joint bit 4 by about 2.5 times independent of its width (128 to 1024) while not changing bit 4 alone (C-77), and this is not due to the added depth. What in the frozen layer does this is not identified (untested candidates: the trainable layers have to decode a dense random mixing of the input bits instead of axis-aligned bit inputs; the frozen layer limits how the trainable layer's weights can use single input bits; Adam scale of the first trainable layer). Together with C-75 to C-77 this means the width story of C-75 (random features already carry p4) is NOT supported as a cause: it correlates with width but a direct intervention with the same kind of random feature made things worse.

## C-80 (CONFIRMATORY, follows C-63, C-67; lazy-training lead, Chizat et al. opened)
Question. Is the width effect (C-63, C-74) accompanied by the weights moving less (the lazy-training signature) as it was for the initial scale (C-67)? Design: c67's measurement (relative weight change ||W - W0||_F / ||W0||_F of each layer at the first evaluation step where bit 4 has been solved, joint lam = 1 and bit-4-alone lam = 0) at widths 64, 128, 512 with default init, Adam 1e-3, batch 256, seeds 0-4, 12,000-step cap. Script c80_lazy_width.py (c67 with WIDTH), output c80_w<W>/.
Predictions (written before the runs). (1) the median relative change of the first layer at the bit-4 time, joint net, falls with width: W=64 > W=128 > W=512. (2) same ordering for the bit-4-alone net. (3) joint is larger than alone at every width. If (1) holds only for the joint and not for alone, the width effect is not simply the lazy-regime effect.

RESULT C-80 (seeds 0-4, cap 12,000 steps, relative change of the weights at the first evaluation step with bit 4 at 0.99 and confirmed; at W=64 joint two seeds had not finished and their value is taken at the cap of 12,000 steps; folders c80_w64, c80_w128, c80_w512, logs of the same names). Median over 5 seeds of the relative change of layers 1 / 2 / 3 / head:
- joint: W=64: 2.19 / 7.14 / 6.66 / 4.11 (bit-4 times: 10800 11150 11400 and two not finished); W=128: 1.14 / 4.35 / 3.40 / 2.64 (times 1000 2450 2550 4400 5250); W=512: 0.43 / 1.79 / 1.88 / 1.42 (times 400 450 500 600 650).
- alone: W=64: 0.68 / 1.23 / 1.81 / 1.24 (times 300 300 300 400 550); W=128: 0.44 / 1.04 / 1.50 / 1.15 (times 200 200 250 250 300); W=512: 0.22 / 0.74 / 0.97 / 1.04 (times 150 150 150 150 250).
SCORING. (1) joint, layer 1: 2.19 > 1.14 > 0.43, PASS. (2) alone, layer 1: 0.68 > 0.44 > 0.22, PASS. (3) joint larger than alone at every width: PASS (3.2, 2.6, 2.0 times in layer 1).
Reading. Wider nets move their weights less to solve bit 4, in both the joint and the single-bit net, the signature that C-66/C-67 found for the larger initial scale and that lazy training (Chizat et al.) predicts for wide nets; and the joint net moves them about 2 to 3 times more than the single-bit net at every width, with movement in layers 2 and 3 more than 4 times the alone value at W=64 (joint is slower, so it also has more steps). The width and scale effects on the plateau therefore look alike in this measure. This is a correlation of two quantities across widths; I did not show that the shorter plateau is caused by the smaller movement (a lazy-regime criterion with output centring, as in Chizat et al., was not computed), and relative change grows with the number of Adam steps taken, which are fewer in the faster nets, so the ordering is partly a time effect.

## Second reproduction pack (why_pack_v2.zip, 2026-10-26 10:29)
Three scripts (c59, c66 at C=2, c73 at F=1 and F=1/3) with a run guide and my reference medians (c59: 250 / 450 / 2550 for lam 0 / 0.3 / 1; c66 C=2: joint 450, alone 150; c73: 900 and 2900). Smoke-tested for 100 steps. Anything returned from it is an unverified lead until I compare the CSV rows with mine.

## C-81 (EXPLORATORY-WITH-PREDICTION; user's idea: look at the mathematical structure of the targets to answer the 'why')
Idea source: the user (2026-10-26 10:40); background: the standard Boolean-function view in which a target f: {-1,+1}^16 -> {-1,+1} has an exact Walsh-Fourier expansion f = sum_S f^(S) chi_S, with Fourier weight per degree W_d = sum over |S| = d of f^(S)^2; for gradient-trained nets, low-degree weight is what the first gradient steps can see (this link is stated from general knowledge, not from a source I opened; I treat it as a hypothesis to test here, not a result).
Computation. Exact fast Walsh-Hadamard transform over all 2^16 input pairs (n = 8) for each of the nine sum bits (bit 8 = carry out), no training. Quantities: W_d per bit; low-degree weight L2 = W_0 + W_1 + W_2 (degrees 0 to 2); mean degree sum_d d W_d; the largest single |coefficient|. Compare with the measured bit-alone times (C-12 medians, 3 seeds): 100 200 200 200 300 400 600 800 700 (bits 0 to 8).
Predictions (written before computing). (1) bit 0 = a_0 XOR b_0 has all its weight at degree 2 (L2 = 1.0, one coefficient of size 1). (2) Bit 4's low-degree weight is at most 0.2, and the spectral mean degree of bit k rises with k from bit 0 to bit 7. (3) Across bits 0-8 the Spearman correlation between the single-bit time and L2 is at most -0.7 (more low-degree weight, faster), and the correlation with mean degree is at least +0.7. A miss on (3) means the degree profile does not account for the learning order.

RESULT C-81 (exact, no training; script c81_walsh.py, output c81_walsh.txt, c81_walsh.json). Per bit k = 0..8: weight at degrees 0-2 (L2) / mean degree / largest |coefficient| / number of non-zero coefficients:
0: 1.000 / 2.00 / 1.000 / 1 | 1: 0.250 / 3.00 / 0.5 / 4 | 2: 0.062 / 3.50 / 0.5 / 10 | 3: 0.016 / 3.75 / 0.5 / 22 | 4: 0.004 / 3.88 / 0.5 / 46 | 5: 0.001 / 3.94 / 0.5 / 94 | 6: 0.000 / 3.97 / 0.5 / 190 | 7: 0.000 / 3.98 / 0.5 / 382 | 8 (carry out): 0.667 / 1.99 / 0.5 / 766.
For bits 4 to 7 the degree profile is the same: about 0.667 of the weight at degree 3, 0.22 at degree 5, 0.07 at degree 7 (bit 4: 0.664, 0.211, 0.055). Bit 0 is a single degree-2 coefficient; the carry out (bit 8) has 0.667 of its weight at degree 1.
Exact structure of bit 4 (the same pattern holds for the other bits): every one of its 46 non-zero monomials contains a_4 * b_4 times a monomial in the lower bits a_0..a_3, b_0..b_3 (largest: a_4 b_3 b_4 and a_3 a_4 b_4, coefficient 0.5), i.e. bit 4 = (a_4 XOR b_4) times the carry-into-bit-4 function, as a product of characters. The supports of bits 0-7 are pairwise DISJOINT (bit 4 shares 0 monomials with each of bits 0-3 and 5-7); only the carry out (bit 8) shares all 46 of them (checked for all 36 pairs afterwards: bits 0-7 are pairwise disjoint, and the support of bit 8 contains the whole support of every other bit: 1+4+10+22+46+94+190+382 = 749 of its 766 monomials).
SCORING. (1) bit 0 all weight at degree 2: PASS. (2) bit 4 low-degree weight at most 0.2 (0.004) and mean degree rising from bit 0 to bit 7: PASS (2.00 to 3.98, increasing but saturating at 4). (3) Spearman of single-bit time with L2 at most -0.7: MISS (-0.63); with mean degree at least +0.7: MISS (+0.51).
Reading. (a) What is exact and certain: the middle bits have essentially no degree 1-2 correlation with the inputs (0.4 percent of the variance of bit 4 at degrees 0-2; none at degree 1), so a model that starts from linear and pairwise-product features has almost nothing to fit first; all of bit 4's signal sits at degree 3 and above, and always contains the factor a_4 b_4. (b) The degree profile does NOT explain the learning order: bits 4 to 7 have the same profile but their single-bit times rise from 300 to 800, so the hump and the top-bit speed-up are not a degree effect (the carry out is fast-ish, 700, and does have low-degree weight, but bits 4-7 are the ones that differ at fixed profile). (c) Post hoc, noted after the miss and one of four quantities looked at: the number of non-zero coefficients (1, 4, 10, 22, 46, 94, 190, 382, 766; each bit 2n+2 of the previous) has Spearman +0.97 with the single-bit time over the nine bits. Nine points, a quantity chosen after seeing the data, and a count that rises with k by construction, so this is a hypothesis to test with a prediction written in advance, not a finding. (d) For the joint net: the targets of different bits have disjoint monomial supports, so any interaction between bits in the joint net comes through shared hidden features and the shared optimizer, not through shared target terms; this fits the C-17 (near-orthogonal gradients) and C-61 (Adam state) results but does not explain the width-dependent part. Limits: n = 8, a statement about the targets only; the link from Fourier degree to learning time for an Adam-trained ReLU net is a hypothesis from general knowledge, not established here, and the disjointness statement was checked by direct count over all pairs of bits.

## C-82 (EXPLORATORY-WITH-PREDICTION; user's request 2026-10-26 10:43: explain the results from the mathematics of the model and the training, not only from the target)
Idea source: (a) the user; (b) a derivation, from general knowledge, not from a source I opened: with a ReLU unit h_j = relu(w_j.x + b_j) and a small-output start, the first gradient steps on the hidden weights of unit j are proportional to the correlation of h_j with the residual (here the target), so the units whose random features already correlate with the needed feature grow first and the plateau ends when the best such unit has grown ("best of W units"); this predicts (i) the escape time is set by the initial weights (C-62: 73 percent of the variance) and (ii) falls with width (more units to draw from; C-63, C-74). This experiment tests the within-width part, which has a numerical prediction.
Design. 60 nets of the single-output bit-4 kind (c12 architecture: 3 x 128 ReLU, Adam 1e-3, batch 256, BCE) that differ ONLY in the initial weights (torch seeds 10000 + s, s = 0..59); the training split (seed 500) and the batch order (numpy seed 777) are the same for all. Time = first step (grid of 25) where held-out accuracy is at least 0.99 and still is at the next evaluation. Computed BEFORE training from the untrained net, over all 65,536 inputs: A_p = max over layer-1 units of |Pearson correlation of the unit's output with the character p4 = (1 - 2a_4)(1 - 2b_4)|; A_c = same for the unrelated character a_2 b_6 (control); and the best-layer ridge-probe accuracy of p4 (as C-75, 20,000 + 20,000 pairs). Script c82_init_alignment.py, output c82/.
Predictions (written before the runs). (1) Spearman(time, A_p) is at most -0.4 (stronger alignment, shorter plateau). (2) Spearman(time, best-layer p4 probe accuracy) is at most -0.4. (3) |Spearman(time, A_c)| is at most 0.3. If (1) and (2) fail, the best-of-W-units picture does not explain the initial-weight dependence within a width, and the dependence on the initial weights must come from something else.

RESULT C-82 (60 nets, none censored; c82_a/, c82_b/, logs c82_a.log, c82_b.log). Bit-4-alone times fall in a narrow band: 175 (1 net), 200 (14), 225 (17), 250 (22), 275 (6) steps (grid of 25), i.e. a spread of 175 to 275 for 60 different initial weights. Spearman of the time with: A_p (max unit correlation with the XOR character a_4 xor b_4, range 0.09 to 0.23) -0.05; best-layer p4 probe accuracy (0.79 to 0.90) -0.06; layer-3 probe accuracy +0.13; control A_c (unrelated character a_2 b_6) +0.23.
SCORING. (1) A_p: -0.05, MISS (needed at most -0.4). (2) probe: -0.06, MISS. (3) control |0.23| at most 0.3: PASS (so the control was fine; the main two did not predict).
Reading. In the single-bit net the initial weights hardly change the time (175 to 275 steps; with 60 nets and a time resolution of 25 steps there is little to explain), and what little varies is not predicted by how well the best first-layer unit lines up with a_4 xor b_4 or by the untrained probe accuracy. So the 'best of W units' picture is not supported for the single-bit net (power is low because the variation is small). The 73 percent of the variance that C-62 attributes to the initial weights is for the JOINT net (1000 to 5250 steps), where this test has not yet been run: C-83.

## C-83 (CONFIRMATORY, follows C-62 and C-82): does the initial alignment predict the JOINT bit-4 time?
Same design as C-82 but the net is the joint net of C-59 (nine outputs, loss summed over the nine bits, Adam 1e-3, batch 256), only the bit-4 time is recorded (9,000-step cap, evaluation every 50 steps), 40 initial weights (torch seeds 10000 + s, s = 0..39; the first three layers have the same initial weights as in C-82 for the same s), fixed split and batch order. Script c83_init_joint.py, output c83_a/, c83_b/. Metrics at initialisation as in C-82 (A_p, A_c, best-layer p4 probe accuracy).
Predictions (written before the runs). (1) Spearman(time, A_p) at most -0.4. (2) Spearman(time, best-layer probe accuracy) at most -0.4. (3) |Spearman(time, A_c)| at most 0.3. (4) The spread is large: max/min of the 40 times at least 3 (C-62 saw about 1,400 to 4,150 across 5 initial weights). The honest prior is that (1) and (2) are about 40 percent likely after the C-82 miss.

## C-84 (EXPLORATORY-WITH-PREDICTION; from the mathematics of Adam: the update of a parameter is lr * m_hat / (sqrt(v_hat) + eps); at step 1 it is exactly lr * sign(g), and later it stays of order lr per parameter whatever the gradient size)
Derivation (my own, from the Adam definition; not a cited result). If every parameter moves by about lr per step in the direction that lowers the loss, the output of the net changes per step by about lr * sum over parameters of |d f / d theta| = lr * ||grad_theta f||_1. In a ReLU net of default PyTorch init this L1 norm grows with the number of parameters, i.e. with width, so the speed of the first phase should grow with width W (about as W, if the per-parameter gradients keep their size), and the time to cover a fixed change of the logits should fall as 1/W. This is a first-order statement for the early phase only; it does not describe the plateau exit itself.
Computation (at initialisation, no training): for widths 64, 128, 256, 512 (3 hidden layers, default init, seeds 0-4), the mean over 4,096 random inputs of ||grad_theta f_4||_1 where f_4 is the logit of output 4; and its ratio to the W=128 value. Compare with the measured bit-4-alone times (C-63: 300, 250, 200, 150) and joint times (about 11,000 or longer, 2,550, 1,050, 500).
Predictions (written before computing). (1) The L1 norm rises with width: 64 < 128 < 256 < 512. (2) Its 512-to-128 ratio is at least 2.5. (3) It does NOT track the measured bit-4-alone times: the measured alone time falls only 1.7 times from 128 to 512, so 1/L1 will overpredict the speed-up (I expect the ratio of 1/L1 to the ratio of the measured times to be at least 1.5).

RESULT C-84 (deviation from the written design: 256 random inputs per net instead of 4,096, because per-example gradients were computed one at a time; seeds 0-4, median of 5). Mean ||grad_theta f_4||_1: W=64 27.1, W=128 63.3, W=256 163.7, W=512 422.5; ratios to W=128: 0.43, 1, 2.59, 6.68 (per-seed values within 15 percent of the median, e.g. W=512: 398 to 470).
SCORING. (1) rises with width: PASS. (2) 512-to-128 ratio at least 2.5: 6.68, PASS. (3) 1/L1 overpredicts the speed-up of the bit-4-alone net: predicted speed-up 6.68, measured 250/150 = 1.67, ratio 4.0 against the limit 1.5, PASS.
Observation after the scoring (NOT a prediction; a comparison made after seeing both sets of numbers): the speed-up of the JOINT net from 128 to 256 and to 512 is 2550/1050 = 2.4 and 2550/500 = 5.1, close to the L1-norm ratios 2.59 and 6.68; at W=64 the L1 norm predicts 2.3 times slower than 128 (about 5,900 steps) and the measured joint time is at least 4.3 times slower (11,000 or longer, 2 of 5 unfinished at 12,000). So over the range 64 to 512 the joint plateau length scales roughly as 1/||grad f||_1, i.e. about W^-1.2 to W^-1.4 with an even steeper fall at the narrow end, while the bit-4-alone net (300 to 150) does not scale this way (it is near a floor of 100 to 150 steps).
Reading. This is a derivation-based account for the width dependence of the extra part: with Adam each parameter moves about lr per step, so the speed at which the logits can change is lr * ||grad f||_1, which grows with the number of parameters; a plateau that is a fixed amount of logit change to cover (the net must grow its response to a_4 xor b_4 from near zero) therefore takes a time that falls with width. Not tested: (a) that the plateau needs a fixed logit change in each net; (b) the same derivation predicts that the learning rate acts in proportion (C-35/C-36 saw a strong effect) and that the initial scale c acts through the gradient L1 norm (C-66: c = 2 helps strongly, which L1 would also predict since the gradient wrt the first layers grows with c^3; I have not computed that); (c) no intervention that changes ||grad f||_1 at fixed width. The comparison above is made on six numbers and chosen after the fact.

RESULT C-83 (40 initial weights, none censored; c83_a/, c83_b/, logs c83_a.log, c83_b.log). Joint bit-4 times, sorted: 900 1200 1200 1400 1450 1500 1500 1600 1650 1750 1800 1900 2050 2100 2250 2400 2400 2450 2550 2700 3000 3000 3050 3300 3600 3600 3700 3750 4150 4150 4300 4350 4500 5050 5300 5300 5400 5750 5800 6000 (median 2,900 to 3,000). Spearman of the time with: A_p -0.10; best-layer p4 probe accuracy +0.19; layer-1 probe +0.21; layer-3 probe -0.04; control A_c -0.09.
SCORING. (1) A_p at most -0.4: -0.10, MISS. (2) probe at most -0.4: +0.19 (wrong sign), MISS. (3) control |Spearman| at most 0.3: PASS. (4) max/min at least 3: 6.7, PASS.
Reading. The initial weights decide a lot (6.7 times spread over 40 inits with split and batch order fixed) but neither how well the best first-layer unit lines up with a_4 xor b_4 nor how readable p4 is in the untrained layers tells which init is fast. So the 'best aligned unit' picture is not what the initial weights act through, at least with these measures (C-82 for the single-bit net, C-83 for the joint net). The quantity from C-84 (gradient L1 norm of the logit) is tested next on the same 40 inits.

## C-85 (CONFIRMATORY, follows C-83, C-84; same 40 initial weights as C-83)
Prediction from the Adam derivation of C-84: the speed of the early phase scales with ||grad_theta f_4||_1 of the untrained net, so among the 40 inits of C-83 the joint bit-4 time should fall with it. Computation: for each s = 0..39 the net of C-83 (same torch seed, 9-output head), mean over 256 fixed random inputs (numpy seed 5) of ||grad_theta f_4||_1; also the untrained standard deviation of the logit f_4 over those inputs (a measure of output scale).
Predictions (written before computing). (1) Spearman(time, L1 norm) at most -0.4. (2) Spearman(time, logit std) at most -0.3 (a larger output scale at init also speeds things up, C-66). Both are uncertain: the per-seed spread of the L1 norm at fixed width was only about 15 percent (C-84).

RESULT C-85 (the 40 inits of C-83; script c85_l1_init.py, values c85_rows.json). Gradient L1 norm of the logit f_4 at init ranges 47 to 80; the untrained standard deviation of f_4 over inputs 0.009 to 0.020. Spearman with the joint bit-4 time: L1 norm +0.06; logit std 0.00.
SCORING. (1) L1 norm at most -0.4: +0.06, MISS. (2) logit std at most -0.3: 0.00, MISS.
Reading. Across widths the gradient L1 norm lines up with the plateau length (C-84, six numbers, post hoc), but across initial weights at one width it does not (40 inits, correlation 0.06). So the width dependence and the init-to-init dependence are not the same mechanism, or the L1 norm of the untrained net is not the relevant measure at the fixed width (its spread is only about 1.7-fold there, 47 to 80, against 15-fold across widths 64 to 512). What the initial weights act through remains unidentified after four measures (first-layer alignment with a_4 xor b_4, untrained p4 probe accuracy, gradient L1 norm, output scale): all four have correlations between -0.1 and +0.2 with the joint bit-4 time over 40 inits.

## C-86 (CONFIRMATORY; user's request 2026-10-26 11:00: derive a formula that ties the findings together)
Candidate formula (from the Adam argument of C-84 plus the observation that the joint net still needs about the time of the net with one bit alone): T_joint = F + Lambda / (lr * L1(W, c)), where L1 is the mean L1 norm over parameters of the gradient of one logit at initialisation (C-84: 27.1, 63.3, 163.7, 422.5 at W = 64, 128, 256, 512; per-example gradients, 256 inputs, seeds 0-4), F a floor close to the time the same bit needs alone, and Lambda a constant with the meaning 'amount of logit change to cover' (not independently measured).
Fit. F and Lambda are fitted by least squares of T on x = 1/(lr * L1) over the 9 cells of C-39 (median max-over-bits time of the joint net, W in {128, 256, 512} x lr in {1e-3, 3e-3, 1e-2}: 4000 1600 600 / 1500 700 400 / 700 400 300). DISCLOSURE: before writing this I looked at the 9 cells by hand with L1 values 63.3, 163.7, 422.5 and saw that T = 250 + 225 x describes them to about 15 percent, so the in-sample agreement is NOT a test (2 parameters, 9 numbers, chosen after looking). The tests are the predictions below, on data that are not in the fit and for which I have NOT yet computed the L1 values (W = 1024 and the initial scales c).
Predictions (written before computing the new L1 values). (P1) F from the fit lies between 100 and 400 steps. (P2) W = 64, lr 1e-3: the formula gives a value between 6,000 and 15,000 steps; measured (C-35, median max-over-bits, censored counted as 10,000): 10,000 (so a lower bound). (P3) W = 1024, lr 1e-3: formula within a factor 1.5 of the measured 500 (333 to 750); W = 1024, lr 3e-4: within a factor 1.5 of the measured 800 (533 to 1200) (C-36, same statistic). (P4) initial scale c = 2 and 4 (W = 128, lr 1e-3, batch 4096; L1 computed for the scaled init): the formula's ratio T(c)/T(1) is within a factor 1.5 of the measured ratio of full-adder times 550/2450 = 0.22 (c = 2) and 750/2450 = 0.31 (c = 4) (C-66/C-67). Expected failure point: for large L1 the formula tends to the floor F, and the measured time at c = 4 stays well above the floor (bit 4 alone is slower at c = 4: 350), so I expect P4 to fail for c = 4.

RESULT C-86 (script c86_formula.py, output c86_formula.txt, new L1 values c86_l1.json; deviation: for W = 1024 the L1 norm is the median of 3 seeds over 128 inputs, and for the c values 128 inputs, seeds 0-4; the others are from C-84 with 256 inputs). Correction of the wording above: L1 is the sum over all parameters of |d f_4 / d theta| for one input, averaged over the inputs (not a mean over parameters). New values: W = 1024: 1179.4; at W = 128 with all weights and biases multiplied by c = 1, 2, 4: 63.4, 458.7, 3567.8.
Fit on the 9 C-39 cells: T = 214 + 238 / (lr * L1) (F = 213.7, Lambda = 237.9); in-sample ratios predicted/measured 0.90 to 1.11.
SCORING. (P1) F between 100 and 400: 214, PASS (the bit-4-alone time is 250). (P2) W = 64, lr 1e-3: formula 8,992, inside 6,000 to 15,000 and consistent with the measured lower bound 10,000: PASS. (P3) W = 1024: formula 415 against measured 500 (0.83), and 886 at lr 3e-4 against 800 (1.11): PASS both. (P4) initial scale: formula ratio T(c)/T(1) = 0.185 (c = 2) against measured 0.224 (0.82 times): PASS; c = 4: 0.071 against 0.306 (0.23 times): MISS, as I expected (the formula goes to the floor of 214 at c = 4: 280 steps, measured about 750).
Control (computed afterwards, not a prediction): the same two-parameter fit with the plain width instead of L1, 1/(lr W): in-sample worst |log ratio| 0.42 against 0.11 for L1, and out-of-sample W = 1024: 566 and 1643 (measured 500 and 800); with 1/(lr W^2): worst in-sample 0.25, W = 64: 15,112, W = 1024: 420 and 555. So the L1 norm is the best of the three descriptions in-sample and at lr 3e-4, but the W^2 variants are not clearly worse at W = 64 and at W = 1024 lr 1e-3, so the data do not prove that it is the L1 norm rather than another quantity growing about as W^1.4 to W^2.
Another consistency check made afterwards (fails): the gradient-share series of C-59/C-61 (lam = 1: 2550, lam = 0.3: 450, bit 4 alone 250; mean dilution factor 0.24 and 0.66). With the form T = F + Lambda' / (lr * L1 * s) and Lambda' fitted at lam = 1, lam = 0.3 is predicted at about 1,090 (measured 450). The dependence on the dilution factor is steeper than 1/s (T - F falls 11.5 times while s rises 2.75 times: exponent about 2.4). So the formula captures the dependence on width, learning rate and (for c up to 2) initial scale, not the dependence on the gradient share lam.
THE FORMULA (what I will state, with its limits): T_joint(bit 4 or slowest bit) is about F + Lambda / (lr * ||grad_theta f||_1) with F about 210 steps (about the time a bit needs alone), Lambda about 240 (steps times logit units; the 'needed logit change' divided by the fraction of the L1 norm that is aligned with the loss gradient; not measured separately), L1 the L1 norm of the gradient of one logit with respect to all parameters at initialisation. Valid in this data set for n = 8, 3 hidden layers, Adam with the default betas, W from 64 to 1024, lr from 3e-4 to 1e-2, initial scale 1 to 2. Not valid for: initial scale 4, the gradient-share series, other optimizers (SGD showed 1.4 instead of 10 times), other tasks (XOR project: scale effect has the opposite sign), batch sizes other than 256 (the C-39 grid is at 256; the scale series at 4096 enters only through ratios), n = 12 (not checked). Its two constants were fitted on nine cells, and the tests are four out-of-sample points and a ratio.

## INTAKE 2 (2026-10-26 11:03): a note from the separate chat on the mathematical structure (text pasted by the owner; the updated why_research_all.zip it refers to is a link I cannot open from here, so none of its files was read)
VERIFIED BY ME (c87_check_claims.py, output c87_check_claims.txt; own computation over all 65,536 pairs, nothing taken from their files):
- S = P*C (sum bit 4 = propagate times carry-into-bit-4 in the +-1 encoding): true for all pairs. P(c4 = 1) = 15/32 = 0.46875 and E[C] = E[S P] = 1/16 = 0.0625, E[P P] = 1: all exact. So the initial gradient of a coefficient on the feature P under direct propagate supervision is 16 times that under the sum-bit target (a statement about a one-coefficient model, as they say; it is not a prediction for the MLP speed).
- E[S P U3] = 1/2 for U3 = (-1)^a3 (and the same for (-1)^b3): exact. This is the same fact as the 0.5 coefficients on a_4 b_4 b_3 and a_3 a_4 b_4 in my C-81 spectrum; and E[S U3] = 0 and E[S (-1)^a4] = 0 (no degree-1 signal). So the signal is strong once the feature P*U3 exists (degree 3) and absent for the single bits: the delay is about building the product feature, not about weak gradients on features that already exist.
- Their two-task Adam example (g1 = (-d, -d), g2 = (-1, 1), g1.g2 = 0): the first Adam step with zero moments and eps = 0 is lr*sign(g), giving the solo step (lr, lr) and the joint step (lr, -lr); the first-order change of loss 1 is -2*d*lr solo and 0 joint (checked with d = 0.3: -0.60 and 0.00). So orthogonal gradients can interact through coordinate-wise normalisation. This is the same mechanism as the sign-descent argument of C-84 and what C-61 and C-73 measured (shared second moment costs a factor of about 3).
NOT VERIFIED: the citation. The link they give for task-specific adaptive-state methods ('AdaTask') points to arXiv 1412.6980, which is the Adam paper, not an AdaTask paper; I have opened neither, so 'AdaTask exists and does this' is a lead only. Their statements that the proofs, exhaustive checks and source records are in the zip and in MATH_STRUCTURE_FIRST_PASS.md: not read.
What it adds to my list: nothing that changes a result. Their suggestion for the remaining 3.6 times (how shared updates change useful-feature growth and curvature after the Adam-state intervention, and whether a model predicts width, learning-rate and initialisation results) is the same open item as mine; C-86 is a first formula for width, learning rate and scale 2, and not for the initial-weight dependence.

VERIFIED BY ME (c88_check_math_note.py, output c88_check_math_note.txt; my own computation over all 65,536 pairs, no code from the file):

(2) carry-spin recurrence reproduces the true carry for i = 1..8; E[H_i] = 2^-i; F_8 = H_8 and F_i = Q_i H_i (i < 8); E[F_i F_j] = 0 for i != j < 8 and E[F_8 F_i] = -2^(i-8) (so the overflow bit is NOT uncorrelated with the others; my C-81 said it shares the support of all of them, consistent); eq. (5) E[F_i Q_i U_(i-1)] = 1/2 for i = 1..7.

VERIFIED SOURCE (opened by me through WebSearch and WebFetch): AdaTask (Yang et al., arXiv 2211.15055, AAAI 2023). As returned by the page reader: shared parameters are 'still dominated by one or several tasks'; for adaptive optimizers 'the adaptive learning rate is usually proportional to the inverse of the accumulative gradients', which for shared parameters are 'aggregated over all tasks'; AdaTask 'separate[s] the accumulative gradients of each task for each shared parameter' (for Adam both the second moment and the momentum term, since separating only the second moment is not enough). So the C-73 intervention (separate first and second moments per output in the shared layers) is the AdaTask idea applied to the adder; I arrived at it as the direct test of C-61, and the report's 'next test 2' and this paper confirm it is a known idea. The paper did not (as far as the reader quoted) say how large the effect is in a small adder, and my C-73 shows a factor of 2.8 gained, not the whole delay. The other two sources it lists (Kingma and Ba, Jacot et al.) were not opened by me.

What it adds. (1) Their remark that a fixed block-diagonal kernel with identical per-task normalisation gives NO joint delay is a clean statement of what a model of the residual has to contain. (2) A point from my own results that bears on eq. (7) (second-order interaction through the Hessian): under momentum SGD (C-60, one learning rate) the joint net is only 1.4 times slower than lam = 0, whereas with Adam and separate moments (C-73) 3.6 times remains. If second-order cross terms alone caused the remainder it should appear in SGD as well; it does not, with the caveat that SGD and Adam are in different speed regimes at the one learning rate I tried. So the remaining part is, at least in part, not explained by a second-order cross term of the plain-gradient type; this is an argument from two numbers and is not a test. (3) They say that fitting a free constant would not answer why: my formula of C-86 has two fitted constants (F and Lambda) and is described as empirical for that reason.

## C-89 (EXPLORATORY-WITH-PREDICTION; the original question Q4 'what kind of circuit': ripple, lookahead or weighted sum; owner: 'complete means answering the initial questions', 2026-10-26 11:13)
Why this test. A linear probe cannot tell a carry from a weighted sum (the carry is a threshold of sum_j 2^j (a_j + b_j) over the low bits; C-09, C-10). What does differ is the structure of the trained weights. Weighted-sum circuit: a first-layer unit that computes (part of) the threshold has input weights growing geometrically with bit position, about a factor 2 per position (the same on a_j and b_j). Ripple circuit: units look at local groups (a_j, b_j and a neighbour, or the previous carry), so weights have a narrow support of 1 to 3 positions and no systematic growth. Lookahead: units over groups of positions with generate/propagate structure (support 2 to 4 positions per group, growth inside a group not systematic across positions). Idea source: the owner's original question about comparing with ripple-carry and carry-lookahead adders; the geometric-weights prediction is from the threshold identity (which I verified in C-09 and in c75_baseline, carry into bit 4 = 1.000 by a linear threshold on the raw inputs).
Design. Joint nets (n = 8, 3 x 128 ReLU, Adam 1e-3, batch 256, 50 percent of pairs for training; initial weights and splits of seeds 0-4 as C-59), trained until every bit has held-out accuracy at least 0.99 (cap 12,000 steps). For layer 1 take the units in the top half by the L2 norm of their outgoing weights to layer 2 (those that matter most). For each unit, with w_j = (|w_{a,j}| + |w_{b,j}|)/2 per position j = 0..7: (i) support = number of positions with w_j at least 25 percent of the maximum; (ii) slope = least-squares slope of log2 w_j against j over positions with w_j at least 10 percent of the maximum (at least 3 such positions needed; else the unit is counted as 'narrow'). Script c89_weight_structure.py, output c89/. Also reported: the same quantities for untrained nets of the same seeds (control: random weights have support 8 and slope near 0).
Predictions (written before running). (1) For the trained nets the median support over the selected units is at least 4 positions (units are not local, against a pure ripple). (2) Among units with at least 3 active positions, the median slope is between 0.3 and 1.5 per position (a visible geometric growth), and the untrained control median is between -0.3 and 0.3. (3) The fraction of units with support at most 2 (ripple-like) is at most 25 percent. If (2) fails with a slope near 0 at support at least 4, the circuit is neither weighted-sum nor local ripple (and lookahead-like or something else is left); if (3) fails, local units dominate.

RESULT C-89 (5 nets, trained until every bit had held-out accuracy at least 0.99 at an evaluation, 2,600 to 4,600 steps; 64 selected layer-1 units per net = 320 units; c89/structure_n8.csv). Support (positions with weight at least 25 percent of the unit's maximum), trained: median 4; distribution over support 1..8: 14, 62, 54, 32, 26, 49, 49, 34 units (24 percent with support at most 2); untrained control: median 8, 0 percent at most 2. Slope of log2 weight against position (305 trained units with at least 3 active positions): median -0.03 (IQR -0.18 to +0.17; per-seed medians 0.03, 0.00, 0.01, -0.07, -0.03); untrained control 0.00. Tails (computed after the scoring, not predicted): trained slope at least 0.5 in 18 percent of units, at most -0.5 in 5 percent, between -0.25 and 0.25 in 65 percent; untrained 0, 0, 98 percent.
SCORING. (1) median support at least 4: 4.0, PASS (on the boundary). (2) median slope between 0.3 and 1.5: -0.03, MISS (control within -0.3 to 0.3: PASS). (3) fraction with support at most 2 at most 25 percent: 24 percent (76 of 320), PASS (on the boundary).
Reading. In the aggregate the layer-1 units do not show the geometric weight growth of a weighted-sum circuit (median slope zero), and they are not mostly local (median support 4 of 8, only a quarter with support at most 2). But about one unit in five (18 percent) has a clearly geometric profile (slope at least 0.5 per position, never seen in the untrained control), a few (5 percent) the reverse, and most are flat. So the layer-1 weights are a mixture, which agrees with the earlier conclusion that neither idealisation fits (C-13); this does not say which units do the work. The causal role of the geometric units is tested next (C-90). Limits: absolute weights per position averaged over a and b; layer 1 only; top half of units by outgoing norm; absolute thresholds (25 percent, 10 percent) fixed in advance but arbitrary; 5 nets.

## C-90 (CONFIRMATORY, follows C-89): do the geometric-profile units carry the high bits?
Design. The five C-89 nets (same seeds, retrained identically). In layer 1 take the selected top-half units; G = those with slope at least 0.5 (at least 3 active positions); control R = the same number of units drawn at random from the selected units not in G (10 random draws, average). Ablate by replacing the unit's activation with its mean over the 65,536 inputs (mean-ablation). Measure the change of held-out accuracy of every sum bit on the 32,768 held-out pairs. Script c90_ablate_geometric.py, output c90/.
Predictions (written before running). (1) For bits 4 to 7, the mean accuracy drop after ablating G is larger than after ablating the matched random set R in at least 4 of the 5 nets. (2) In the same nets the drop from G on bits 4 to 7 exceeds its drop on bits 0 to 2 (position-specific). A miss on (1) means the geometric-profile units are not special for the high bits.

RESULT C-90 (5 nets retrained identically to C-89; nG = 12, 10, 11, 11, 10 geometric units out of 64 selected, 128 in the layer; c90/ablate_n8.csv, script c90_ablate_geometric.py). Mean over the 5 nets of the accuracy drop per sum bit 0..8 after mean-ablation of the units:
- G (geometric, slope at least 0.5): 0.001 0.001 0.121 0.342 0.370 0.547 0.143 0.080 0.040
- R (random, same number, from the other selected units, 10 draws): 0.016 0.064 0.044 0.056 0.054 0.043 0.042 0.028 0.011
- T (CONTROL ADDED AFTER THE FIRST RUN, not in the written design: the nG non-geometric selected units with the largest outgoing weight norm): 0.047 0.226 0.131 0.186 0.094 0.077 0.050 0.034 0.016
Mean drop on bits 3-5 per net, G against T: 0.386/0.152, 0.407/0.151, 0.264/0.087, 0.567/0.107, 0.473/0.099 (G larger in 5 of 5).
SCORING (the written predictions use bits 4-7 and R). (1) bits 4-7 drop of G larger than R in at least 4 of 5 nets: per-net G 0.244, 0.283, 0.225, 0.344, 0.329 against R 0.040, 0.065, 0.034, 0.035, 0.036: 5 of 5, PASS. (2) G drop on bits 4-7 larger than on bits 0-2 in the same net: 0.244/0.048, 0.283/0.021, 0.225/0.132, 0.344/0.001, 0.329/0.005: 5 of 5, PASS.
Reading. Removing about 10 to 12 first-layer units (out of 128) selected only for a geometric weight profile (weights growing about 2 times or more per bit position) lowers the accuracy of sum bits 3 to 5 by 0.34 to 0.55 (bit 5 on average by 0.55), far more than random units (0.04 to 0.06) and more than the non-geometric units of the largest outgoing norm (0.08 to 0.19; those hurt bit 1 and bit 3 most). The effect is concentrated on the middle bits, the same bits that are slowest to learn in the joint net (hump bits 3-5), and is small on bits 0, 1, and the top bit. So a minority of first-layer units has a weighted-sum-like (threshold of the significance-weighted sum) profile and is causally needed for the middle bits' computation; the rest of the layer is a mixture that does not follow the idealisations (C-13, C-89). This is the first instrument in the project that picks out a unit group for the carry-dependent bits (the linear probes and the single-direction ablation could not), and it is evidence for a partially weighted-sum-like circuit; it does not say that the circuit is only that. Limits: 5 nets, n = 8, layer 1 only, mean-ablation (not retrained, not patched with counterfactual values), the selection thresholds (slope 0.5, 10 percent support) were fixed in advance but are arbitrary, the control T was added after seeing G's effect, the units were selected from the top half by outgoing norm, and the drop in accuracy of the sum bit does not by itself show that the units compute 'the carry' (they may carry a weighted sum that the later layers threshold in several places). A counterfactual patching test and the same analysis in layers 2 and 3 are not done.

## C-91 (CONFIRMATORY, follows C-90; original question Q3: is a carry represented, and used, inside the network?)
Why. C-90 shows that the geometric-profile layer-1 units are needed for bits 3-5, but not that they carry the carry. A counterfactual patching test asks it directly. For sum bit i (i = 3, 4, 5) take pairs of inputs x, x' with the same a_i, b_i, the same higher bits, and different lower bits chosen so that the carry into bit i differs (c_i(x) != c_i(x')); then s_i differs. Run the net on x, replace the layer-1 activations of a chosen unit set by those obtained on x', and see whether the output of bit i moves to the value it has on x' (a 'flip'). If the geometric units carry the carry signal, patching them should flip bit i; if they only carry something that the rest of the layer also holds, patching them should flip little. Idea source: activation patching as the standard causal test for 'is this representation used'; I describe it from general knowledge (no source opened); the carry/threshold fact is from C-09 and C-75's baseline.
Design. The five nets of C-89/C-90 (retrained identically, saved in c91_nets/ as state_dicts of my own runs). For each net and i = 3, 4, 5: 4,000 pairs (x from the held-out half; x' built by resampling the lower i positions of a and b until the carry differs; half of the pairs have carry 0 to 1, half 1 to 0); keep only pairs where the clean net is right on both x and x' for bit i. Unit sets, as in C-90: G (geometric), R (random, same number, 10 draws averaged), T (largest outgoing norm among the others, same number), and ALL (all 128 layer-1 units: the positive control, flip rate 1 by construction). Flip rate = fraction of kept pairs whose patched bit-i output equals the clean output on x'. Script c91_patch.py, output c91/.
Predictions (written before running). (1) G flip rate for bit 4 is at least 0.30 averaged over the five nets. (2) It is at least 2 times the T flip rate and at least 3 times the R flip rate. (3) The result holds in the sense that G exceeds T in at least 4 of 5 nets at each of bits 3, 4, 5. If (1) fails but (2) holds, the group carries part of the signal; if both fail, the units are needed for the middle bits for some other reason than carrying the carry.

RESULT C-91 (5 nets, 3,937 to 4,000 kept pairs per net and bit; c91/patch_n8.csv; script c91_patch.py, whose first sampling loop is a leftover that only advances the random stream and was kept so that the run is reproducible). Mean flip rate over the five nets (per-net values in brackets for G):
- bit 3: G 0.38 [0.48 0.26 0.18 0.50 0.48]; T 0.225; R 0.073; ALL 1.0
- bit 4: G 0.48 [0.46 0.57 0.27 0.69 0.44]; T 0.111; R 0.077; ALL 1.0
- bit 5: G 0.74 [0.74 0.78 0.72 0.81 0.64]; T 0.061; R 0.045; ALL 1.0
SCORING. (1) G flip rate for bit 4 at least 0.30: 0.484, PASS. (2) G at least 2 times T and at least 3 times R: 4.4 and 6.3 times, PASS. (3) G above T in at least 4 of 5 nets at each of bits 3, 4, 5: 5 of 5 at each, PASS.
Reading. Replacing only the roughly 11 geometric layer-1 units' activations by those from an input that differs in the carry into bit i (same a_i, b_i, same higher bits) moves the output of sum bit i to the counterfactual value in 38 percent (bit 3), 48 percent (bit 4) and 74 percent (bit 5) of the pairs, against 5 to 8 percent for random units and 6 to 23 percent for the highest-outgoing-weight non-geometric units. So these units carry carry-dependent information that the later layers use, and they carry more of it for higher bits. It is not all of it: with bit 4 about half of the pairs still do not flip, so other layer-1 units hold carry-dependent information too (redundancy or a distributed code). Limits: 5 nets, n = 8, layer 1 only, the patch replaces whole activation values (a unit's activation depends on all of x', not only on the carry-relevant lower bits, so the patch also transmits other changes in the low bits, which here are exactly the thing that changes the carry), the unit sets were chosen by weight profile and not by their patching effect, and 'carry-dependent information' is not the same as 'the carry bit': the weighted-sum value is what the units may carry.

## C-92 (CONFIRMATORY, follows C-89 to C-91; Q4 'what circuit': are the geometric units literally computing the weighted sum of the low bits?)
Test. For each geometric unit (same G, T, R sets and nets as C-91) take its pre-activation z over all 65,536 inputs and compare it with the weighted low-bit sum M_k = sum_{j<k} 2^j (a_j + b_j) for k = 2..8 (the quantity whose threshold 2^k is the carry into bit k). For each unit report the k with the highest squared Pearson correlation R2(z, M_k) and that value. This uses the saved nets of C-91. Script c92_weighted_sum.py, output c92/.
Predictions (written before running). (1) The median best-window R2 of G units is at least 0.8. (2) The median best-window R2 of the T units (the largest-outgoing-norm non-geometric ones) is at most 0.5. (3) For G units the best window k is 4, 5 or 6 in at least 60 percent of the units (the windows of the carries that matter for bits 3-5). A miss on (1) means the geometric profile does not mean 'linear in the weighted sum' (a threshold-type nonlinearity or mixed bits).

RESULT C-92 (the G, T, R unit sets of C-91; 54 units each; c92/weighted_sum_n8.csv). Best squared correlation of the pre-activation with a low-bit weighted sum M_k over k = 2..8: G median 0.27 (IQR 0.22 to 0.33), T 0.25, R 0.20. Best window k (2..8) for G: 7, 7, 10, 20, 10, 0, 0 units, i.e. k = 4, 5 or 6 for 74 percent of the G units, against 35 percent (T) and 39 percent (R).
SCORING. (1) G median R2 at least 0.8: 0.27, MISS. (2) T median at most 0.5: 0.25, PASS (uninformative, since G is as low). (3) G windows 4-6 in at least 60 percent: 74 percent, PASS.
Reading, and an EXPLORATORY follow-up made after the miss (not predicted). The pre-activation is not linear in the weighted low-bit sum over all inputs, but looking at the weights of four G units (seed 0, units 8, 39, 47, 67) shows why: below a position k the weights grow about 2 times per position and are the same on a_j and b_j (unit 8: 0.01 0.09 0.12 0.20 0.42 on a_0..a_4 and 0.01 0.02 0.11 0.20 0.42 on b_0..b_4), and AT position k the weights on a_k and b_k are large and OPPOSITE in sign (unit 8: -0.73 on a_5 and +0.78 on b_5; unit 47: -0.91 and +0.62), with nothing above. So the unit computes (weighted sum of the bits below k) plus about 0.75 times (b_k - a_k): when a_k = b_k the top term cancels and the unit reports the low-bit sum (the threshold of which is the carry into k, which equals the sum bit k when p_k = 0), when a_k != b_k it shifts by +-0.75. Over all G units the cosine between the symmetric part of the weights and the ideal window (1, 2, 4, ..., 2^(k-1), 0, ...) has median 0.96 (T: 0.72, R: 0.61; maximum over k in all three, so 0.6 is the baseline) and the median correlation between the a-weights and the b-weights is -0.51 (T: -0.21, R: +0.30), because of the antisymmetric top weights. This is a hypothesis built from four examples and two summary numbers computed after looking; it is tested on fresh nets next (C-93).

## C-93 (CONFIRMATORY on FRESH NETS; follows C-89 to C-92)
Design. Five NEW nets (seeds 5-9, same recipe as C-89: joint, 3 x 128, Adam 1e-3, batch 256, trained until all bits are at least 0.99 on held-out data), none of which was used to form the hypothesis or the unit-selection rule. (a) Replication of C-90 and C-91 with the same rules fixed in advance (G = layer-1 units in the top half by outgoing norm with slope at least 0.5; ablation drop on bits 3-5; flip rate at bits 3, 4, 5). (b) The new structural hypothesis: for each G unit take the position k with the largest |w_a,k| + |w_b,k| (largest weight); antisymmetry ratio r = |w_a,k + w_b,k| / (|w_a,k| + |w_b,k|) (0 = exactly opposite signs and equal size, 1 = equal weights). Scripts: c90_ablate_geometric.py and c91_patch.py with SEEDS="5 6 7 8 9" (output c93_ablate/, c93_patch/), and c93_antisym.py (output c93/).
Predictions (written before running). (1) Replication: mean over the five new nets of the G drop on bits 3-5 is at least 0.20 and at least 2 times the T drop (C-90: 0.42 and 0.12). (2) Replication: mean G flip rate at bit 4 is at least 0.30 and at least 2 times the T rate (C-91: 0.48 and 0.11). (3) Structure: at least 70 percent of G units have r at most 0.25, and at most 40 percent of the R units (random from the other selected units) do. A miss of (3) with (1) and (2) holding means the antisymmetric-top description is wrong or too narrow, although the units still matter.

RESULT C-93 (five fresh nets, seeds 5-9; nG = 10, 14, 10, 10, 13; folders c93_ablate/, c93_patch/, c93/, logs of the same names).
(a) Ablation (C-90 rule), mean over the 5 nets of the accuracy drop per bit 0..8: G 0.001 0.000 0.116 0.245 0.441 0.457 0.164 0.080 0.038; T 0.036 0.117 0.098 0.176 0.078 0.072 0.046 0.030 0.012; R 0.006 0.056 0.051 0.058 0.049 0.061 0.048 0.034 0.011. Mean over bits 3-5: G 0.381, T 0.109, R 0.056 (per net G/T: 0.52/0.09, 0.45/0.12, 0.31/0.10, 0.38/0.07, 0.25/0.16).
(b) Patching (C-91 rule), mean flip rates, G / T / R (ALL = 1.0): bit 3: 0.212 / 0.244 / 0.088 (G above T in 2 of 5 nets; G per net 0.52 0.26 0.14 0.13 0.02); bit 4: 0.629 / 0.121 / 0.069 (G above T in 5 of 5; per net 0.69 0.64 0.50 0.40 0.92); bit 5: 0.584 / 0.089 / 0.078 (5 of 5; per net 0.55 0.64 0.63 0.74 0.36).
(c) Structure: units with antisymmetry ratio r at most 0.25 at their largest-weight position: G 40 of 57 (70.2 percent; median r 0.10); R 18 of 57 (31.6 percent; median r 1.00). Largest-weight position of the G units: positions 1..6: 1, 6, 7, 16, 19, 8 units (position 4 or 5 for 61 percent); of the R units spread over positions 0..7.
SCORING. (1) mean G drop on bits 3-5 at least 0.20 and at least 2 times T: 0.381, 3.5 times, PASS. (2) mean G flip rate at bit 4 at least 0.30 and at least 2 times T: 0.629, 5.2 times, PASS. (3) at least 70 percent of G units with r at most 0.25 and at most 40 percent of R: 70.2 percent (by one unit) and 31.6 percent, PASS (the G fraction passes by a hair).
NOT REPLICATED (not a written prediction of C-93 but a clause of C-91 prediction 3): at bit 3 the G flip rate is not above T on the fresh nets (0.21 against 0.24, G above T in 2 of 5 nets; in C-91 it was 0.38 against 0.23 in 5 of 5), so the bit-3 patching result of C-91 does not carry over; the bit 4 and bit 5 results do (0.63 and 0.58 against 0.12 and 0.09).
Reading. On nets that were not used to form the hypothesis, the ablation effect (middle bits 3 to 5) and the patching effect at bits 4 and 5 replicate with the same size, and the antisymmetric-top structure holds for 70 percent of the geometric units against 32 percent of random units. So the finding stands for bits 4 and 5, with the exception of bit 3 for patching, and the description of these units (weights doubling per position below a position k, equal on a and b, plus opposite-sign weights on a_k and b_k) is supported but not universal (30 percent of G units do not fit it, and G was defined by the slope rule, not by this one). What it says about the project's original questions: Q4 (what circuit) is partly answered: the network contains units that compute the weighted sum of the lower bits corrected by the propagate state of the next position, which is the building block of a carry/threshold computation, and these units are causally needed for, and carry the carry-dependent information of, bits 4 and 5; it is neither a ripple chain (units have support 4 or more positions) nor a pure weighted sum (mixture; antisymmetric top term). Q3 (a carry represented and used) is answered in the following sense: a carry-dependent signal sits in a small identifiable group of layer-1 units, and patching it moves the output as the counterfactual carry would in about half to three quarters of the cases for bits 4 and 5; it is not a single carry bit (other units carry part of it, and the signal is a weighted-sum value). Limits: n = 8, 10 nets in total, layer 1 only (layers 2 and 3 not examined), mean-ablation and whole-activation patching, one architecture and optimizer, thresholds fixed in advance but arbitrary.

## C-94 (CONFIRMATORY; follows C-91, C-93; original Q3/Q4 in layers 2 and 3)
Question. C-91/C-93 show that a small group of layer-1 units carries carry-dependent information that the output uses. Is the carry-dependent signal also concentrated in a few units in layers 2 and 3, or is it spread out there? Measure: for sum bit i (4 and 5) and the same kind of counterfactual pairs as C-91 (same a_i, b_i, same higher bits, different carry into bit i), rank the units of a layer by the effect of patching that single unit alone (mean change of the bit-i logit toward the counterfactual value, in the direction of the counterfactual output) on SELECTION pairs; then patch the top m units (m = 1, 2, 4, 8, 16, 32, 64) together on separate EVALUATION pairs (flip rate as in C-91) and compare with m random units of the same layer. The ten saved nets of c91_nets (seeds 0-9: the five of C-89/C-91 and the five fresh ones of C-93). Script c94_layers.py, output c94/. Pairs: 3,000 selection and 3,000 evaluation pairs per net and bit, drawn from the held-out half (kept only if the clean net is right on x and x'). Layer 1 here is ranked by the same rule (single-unit effect), not by the weight profile, so its number is not the C-91 number.
Idea source: standard activation patching; my own choice of the single-unit ranking.
Predictions (written before running). (1) At bit 4, patching the top 8 units of layer 3 flips at least 50 percent of the pairs (mean over the ten nets). (2) The top-8 flip rate of layer 3 exceeds that of layer 1 (the signal is more concentrated later in the net). (3) Random sets of 8 units flip at most 15 percent in every layer. A miss on (1) means the carry-dependent signal is distributed in layer 3; a miss on (2) means layer 1 is at least as concentrated as layer 3.

RESULT C-94 (ten nets, bits 4 and 5, 3,000 evaluation pairs per net and bit; c94/layers_n8.csv, script c94_layers.py). Mean flip rate over the ten nets for the top-m units (ranked by single-unit effect on separate selection pairs) and for m random units, m = 1, 2, 4, 8, 16, 32, 64:
bit 4: layer 1 top 0.25 0.41 0.54 0.64 0.74 0.82 0.88 | random 0.00 0.01 0.02 0.09 0.16 0.25 0.52
       layer 2 top 0.26 0.33 0.41 0.48 0.62 0.79 0.93 | random 0.00 0.00 0.05 0.05 0.10 0.28 0.53
       layer 3 top 0.06 0.09 0.15 0.29 0.51 0.78 0.94 | random 0.00 0.01 0.03 0.02 0.07 0.13 0.51
bit 5: layer 1 top 0.21 0.41 0.57 0.65 0.74 0.84 0.87 | random 0.00 0.01 0.03 0.11 0.18 0.27 0.57
       layer 2 top 0.17 0.24 0.30 0.41 0.56 0.76 0.90 | random 0.00 0.00 0.02 0.02 0.11 0.29 0.51
       layer 3 top 0.04 0.08 0.15 0.30 0.49 0.69 0.86 | random 0.01 0.01 0.02 0.04 0.09 0.21 0.54
SCORING. (1) layer 3, bit 4, top 8 flip at least 50 percent: 0.29, MISS. (2) layer 3 top-8 above layer 1 top-8: 0.29 against 0.64, MISS (the opposite order). (3) random 8 units flip at most 15 percent in every layer: 0.09, 0.05, 0.02 (bit 4) and 0.11, 0.02, 0.04 (bit 5), PASS.
Reading. The carry-dependent signal is most concentrated in layer 1 and gets more distributed with depth: patching a single layer-1 or layer-2 unit moves bit 4 to the counterfactual value in about a quarter of the pairs (layer 3: 6 percent), the top 8 units give 64 percent, 48 percent and 29 percent in layers 1, 2, 3, and half of the pairs need about 3 units in layer 1, about 8 in layer 2 and about 16 in layer 3. My expectation that the signal would be more concentrated later was wrong. This agrees with C-11 (a single direction is needed in layer 1 but in layers 2 and 3 no single direction is). So the carry-dependent information enters in a few layer-1 units and is re-spread over many units in the later layers. Limits: n = 8, ten nets, units ranked by single-unit effect (so the top-m sets are chosen for effect, unlike the weight-profile selection of C-90 to C-93), mean flip over nets hides large differences between nets, whole-activation patching.

## C-95 (CONFIRMATORY; follows C-89 to C-94; does the n = 8 circuit picture hold at n = 12?)
Design. Five joint nets at n = 12 (24 inputs, 13 outputs, 3 x 128 ReLU, Adam 1e-3, batch 256 of fresh random pairs excluding the held-out panel as in C-14, seeds 0-4 of C-14's initial weights and batch order), trained until all 13 bits have accuracy at least 0.99 on the C-14 held-out panel (8,192 pairs) at two consecutive evaluations (every 200 steps) or 60,000 steps. Then, with the n = 8 rules transferred unchanged except for 12 positions: top half of layer-1 units by outgoing norm; G = units with at least 3 positions with weight at least 10 percent of the maximum and log2-weight slope per position at least 0.5; T = the nG other selected units of largest outgoing norm; R = nG random other selected units (10 draws). Measures: (a) mean-ablation drop per bit on the held-out panel; (b) patching flip rate (as C-91; pairs built by resampling the lower i positions of a and b so that the carry into bit i differs; 3,000 pairs; for bits 6, 7, 8); (c) antisymmetry ratio r at each G unit's largest-weight position. Script c95_n12.py, nets in c95_nets/, output c95/.
Predictions (written before running). (1) At least 4 of the 5 nets reach all bits at 0.99 within 60,000 steps. (2) G units exist in every net (nG at least 5 of the 64 selected). (3) Mean over nets of the G ablation drop averaged over bits 4 to 9 is at least 0.10 and at least 2 times the same for R. (4) Mean G patching flip rate at bit 7 is at least 0.30 and at least 2 times T. (5) At least 60 percent of G units have antisymmetry ratio r at most 0.25, against at most 40 percent of R units. A pass of (3) with a miss of (4) or (5) would say the units matter at n = 12 but are not described by the carry/propagate picture.

## INTAKE 4 (2026-10-26 11:33): math_derivations (1).md attached by the owner (second returned note; read as data, not executed)
VERIFIED BY ME (c96_check_note2.py, output c96_check_note2.txt; my own computation and my own raw C-44 file, nothing from their files):
- The C-44 reversal: from my own c44/aux4_n8.csv, bit 3 median 1800 (p4 auxiliary) against 500 (c4); bit 4 median 700 against 2500; with both auxiliaries 400 and 300. c4 is faster than p4 on bit 3 in 5 of 5 seeds and p4 faster than c4 on bit 4 in 5 of 5 seeds. Their raw lists match mine seed by seed. (I had reported the bit-4 numbers; I had not looked at bit 3 of the single-auxiliary runs.)
- The exact inner products: E[Q4 F_j] = 0 for j != 4, 8, = 1/16 for j = 4 and -1/256 for j = 8; E[H4 F_j] = -1/16, -1/8, -1/4, -1/2 for j = 0..3, 0 for j = 4..7 and +1/16 for j = 8: all exact on the 65,536 pairs.
- The growth-rate algebra: for the matrix [[-Q, a], [a, 0]] the largest eigenvalue is (sqrt(Q^2 + 4a^2) - Q)/2 (numerical eigenvalues agree), a/lambda = R gives Q/a = R - 1/R, so R = 3.6 needs Q/a = 3.32222; the toy BCE realization has Hessian [[b^2/4, -1/2], [-1/2, 0]] at zero (finite differences, b = 2: [[1, -0.5], [-0.5, 0]]).
- The truncated-carry error 2^-(k+1) - 2^-(i+1): exact for bit 4 (k = 0: 0.46875, k = 3: 0.03125, k = 4: 0) and bit 7 (k = 6: 0.003906) by brute force over all pairs; I(S4; P4) = 1 - h2(15/32) = 0.0028196 bits.
NOT VERIFIED: the checkpoint audit of the Gaussian margin closure (36 checkpoints from files I do not have), the statement that the Gaussian closure misses 10 of 20 checkpoints, the sources S22-S24 (Saxe et al. 2014, Glasgow 2024, Frei et al. 2023: not opened by me; leads), the claim that no significant adaptation of C61/C73 code was used (they say they did not have it). Their statement that the C61/C73/C74/C80 effects are 'user-reported' is right: they have not seen the code.
WHAT IT ADDS. (1) A mechanism with measurable coefficients for the part that remains after the shared Adam second moment is removed: growth rate lambda = (sqrt(Q^2 + 4a^2) - Q)/2, where Q is the curvature the other heads add along the emerging feature direction; a slowdown R needs Q/a = R - 1/R. It is a candidate; by their own words nothing has measured Q and a. (2) The dependence on the learning rate (ratio of steps shrinks with eta in the exact SGD map) is a prediction I can check: C-36/C-39 have the joint times against the learning rate but I have not measured the joint/alone RATIO against the learning rate; this is a gap in my data. (3) A causal test of the curvature mechanism that is NOT a fit: remove the curvature the other heads contribute while keeping their gradient at the start. Their Q is built from the factors sigma(z_j)(1 - sigma(z_j)) of the other heads; if the other heads' residual (sigma(z_j) - y_j) is frozen at its initial value, their loss is linear in the logits and contributes zero curvature, but exactly the same gradient at step 0. Note that this is different from detaching the residual at every step, which leaves the gradient field, and so the dynamics, unchanged. That is C-96.

## C-96 (CONFIRMATORY; follows INTAKE 4; a test of the nuisance-curvature mechanism by removing the curvature but not the initial gradient)
Design. The joint net of C-59 (lam = 1, n = 8, 3 x 128, Adam 1e-3, batch 256, seeds 0-4, same splits, initial weights and batch order, evaluation every 50 steps, 6,000-step cap) with this loss: bit 4 as usual (BCE on its logit); for the other eight outputs the loss on a batch is sum_j mean_x [ r0_j(x) * z_j(x) ] where r0_j(x) = sigmoid(z_j^0(x)) - y_j(x) is the residual of the freshly initialised net (computed once for all training inputs, fixed, no gradient). At step 0 the gradient of this loss is identical to BCE's; afterwards it does not change with the other heads' logits (no curvature in z_j, hence no Q from them), and it never shrinks as the other bits are learned (the forcing persists). Output: bit-4 time, same rule as before. Script c96_frozen_resid.py, output c96/.
Reference: ordinary joint 4400 5250 1000 2450 2550 (median 2550); bit 4 alone 200 200 250 250 300.
Competing readings and predictions (written before running). If the nuisance curvature of the other heads is what slows bit 4 (their mechanism), removing it should speed it up: (1) median bit-4 time at most 1275. If instead what slows bit 4 is the size of the other heads' gradients (Adam's shared second moment, C-61), the frozen residual keeps those gradients at their initial size for the whole run, so bit 4 should be no faster and probably slower: (2) median at least 3825 (1.5 times the ordinary joint). A result between 1275 and 3825 is inconclusive. A second variant with separate Adam moments per output (C-73, f = 1) and the same frozen residual is run after the first. Its predictions: curvature account: median at most 450 (the dilution is gone and the curvature too; alone is 250); otherwise at least 900 (the C-73 S1 value).

RESULT C-96 (INVALID DESIGN, not a test of the curvature mechanism). With the other heads' residual frozen at its initial value the loss of those heads is linear in their logits and unbounded below. All five seeds ended at chance for bit 4 (held-out accuracy 0.498 to 0.505) and no bit reached 0.99 in 6,000 steps. A diagnostic run of seed 0 (c96 script logic, 2,000 steps) shows why: the mean |logit| of the other heads grows 5.5 (step 50), 245 (100), 10,100 (200), 811,000 (500), 1.2e7 (1000) and 1.6e8 (2000), the bit-4 logit is dragged along (|z4| 28 at step 200, 3,800 at step 1000) and its BCE loss explodes (8.1 at step 200, 2,836 at step 1000). So the run diverged; the outcome says nothing about curvature versus gradient size. Written predictions: (1) median at most 1275: not met (all censored); (2) median at least 3825: formally met (all censored at 6,000) but for the wrong reason, so I do not count it as a pass. The note's mechanism is therefore still untested by me. A bounded version (for example a residual that is frozen but decays with the step number, or a head loss that is clipped) would need its own written predictions; the decay schedule would be a free choice that mimics the other bits being learned, which weakens the test. The second variant (with separate Adam moments) was not run.

RESULT C-95 (n = 12, five nets, seeds 0-4, trained 22,600 to 26,000 steps until all 13 bits had held-out accuracy at least 0.99 at two consecutive evaluations; min accuracy at the end 0.991 to 0.998; folders c95_a/ (seeds 0-2) and c95_b/ (seeds 3-4), nets c95_nets/). Number of geometric units nG out of 64 selected: 27, 16, 26, 23, 20.
Ablation, mean over nets of the accuracy drop for bits 0..12: G 0.011 0.035 0.347 0.294 0.319 0.461 0.356 0.354 0.350 0.443 0.264 0.147 0.070; T 0.144 0.327 0.122 0.239 0.163 0.132 0.142 0.107 0.101 0.087 0.086 0.058 0.025; R 0.097 0.207 0.109 0.198 0.149 0.119 0.124 0.109 0.103 0.071 0.107 0.059 0.025. (4) G flip rate at bit 7 at least 0.30 and at least 2 times T: 0.53 and 4.9 times, PASS. Reading. The n = 8 picture carries over to n = 12 in its causal part: a minority of first-layer units (16 to 27 of the 64 selected, more than at n = 8) with geometric weight profiles lowers the accuracy of bits 2 to 10 by 0.26 to 0.46 when removed (random units: 0.07 to 0.21, with bits 0 and 1 hit most by the non-geometric controls) and carries the carry-dependent signal of bits 6, 7, 8 (flip rates 0.47 to 0.55 against 0.10 to 0.14). The described structure (doubling weights below a position k plus opposite-sign weights on a_k and b_k) fits only about half of the geometric units at n = 12 (48 percent against 25 percent for random units; at n = 8: 70 and 32 percent), so that detailed description is weaker at n = 12, although the group matters just as much. ## C-97 (CONFIRMATORY; follows INTAKE 4 and C-86; the joint/alone ratio against the learning rate)
Why. A continuous-time clock rescaling (the update dtheta/dt = -eta G(theta) traversed at a speed proportional to eta) would give the same joint/alone ratio at every learning rate; a ratio that changes with the learning rate rules that account out (INTAKE 4, section 7). My formula of C-86 (T = F + Lambda / (lr L1) with F about the alone time) predicts a ratio that falls with lr. I have the joint times against lr (C-36, C-39) but never the alone times against lr.
Design. The joint net (lam = 1) and the bit-4-alone net (lam = 0) of C-59 at Adam learning rates 3e-4, 1e-3, 3e-3, 1e-2; n = 8, 3 x 128, batch 256, seeds 0-4 (same splits, initial weights and batch order); evaluation every 25 steps (the alone times at high lr are only a few dozen steps), caps 12,000 steps (3e-4 joint) down to 3,000; bit-4 time as before. Script c97_lr_ratio.py (c59 with LR and EV), output c97_lr<LR>/.
Statistic: median over seeds of the per-seed ratio joint time / alone time (a censored joint time counts as the cap, giving a lower bound).
Predictions (written before running). (1) The ratio is non-increasing over lr = 3e-4, 1e-3, 3e-3, 1e-2 (each value at most 1.15 times the previous one). (2) The ratio at 1e-2 is at most 0.75 times the ratio at 1e-3. Common-clock account: all four ratios within +-30 percent of each other. A result between those is read as inconclusive.

### C-97 result (scored against the predictions written above)
Bit-4 time, median of 5 seeds (steps; evaluation every 25; one censored joint run at 3e-4 counted as the 12,000 cap, so that ratio is a lower bound for that seed):
| lr | joint | alone | median per-seed ratio |
|---|---|---|---|
| 3e-4 | 9750 | 450 | 20.8 |
| 1e-3 | 2525 | 225 | 10.8 |
| 3e-3 | 1125 | 150 | 7.5 |
| 1e-2 | 500 | 150 | 3.3 |
Per-seed values: c97_scores.txt (script c97_score.py).
Prediction 1 (ratio non-increasing, each value at most 1.15 x the previous one): HIT (20.8, 10.8, 7.5, 3.3). Prediction 2 (ratio(1e-2) at most 0.75 x ratio(1e-3), i.e. at most 8.1): HIT (3.3). The common-clock account (ratios within +-30 percent): MISSED by a wide margin, so a pure rescaling of time by the learning rate does not describe the joint/alone gap.
Notes and limits. (a) The ratio at 1e-3 here (10.8) is larger than the 3.6 in my earlier two-part account because that 3.6 was the residual after the shared-moment part (900 vs 250) was removed; the total joint/alone ratio is about 10. (b) The alone time stops falling at about 150 steps for lr >= 3e-3 (evaluation step 25, and probably a floor), so the high-lr ratios are mostly the joint time falling toward a floor and are not a clean measurement of the ratio at the limit. (c) joint time x lr is not constant (2.9, 2.5, 3.4, 5.0), which also contradicts a pure 1/lr law for the joint time. (d) Only the direction is established; the functional form (for example T = F + L/(lr L1)) was not fitted here. (e) Only W=128, 5 seeds.
Learned. The extra delay of the joint net shrinks relative to the alone net as the step size grows, consistent with the returned note's claim that the exact SGD/Adam map, not a continuous-time clock, sets the delay. It does not by itself identify the mechanism (nuisance curvature remains an unverified candidate; C-96 was invalid).

## C-98 (CONFIRMATORY; follows C-84 to C-86; tests whether the gradient L1 of the logit matters at fixed width)
Why. The formula T = F + Lambda/(lr L1) of C-86 was fitted on cells where width and L1 move together, so it cannot tell L1 from width. Here the function and the width are held fixed and only L1 is changed.
Design. Joint net, n=8, 3 x 128, Adam lr 1e-3, batch 256, seeds 0-4 as in C-39. After the default init: layer-1 weight and bias x alpha, layer-2 weight / alpha (layer-2 bias and everything else unchanged). Because ReLU is positively homogeneous this leaves the network function EXACTLY unchanged for alpha a power of two (checked in c98_l1_alpha.py: max output difference 0.0). Gradients of layer 1 shrink by 1/alpha and of layer 2 grow by alpha, so the L1 of grad f_4 changes (c98_l1_alpha.txt, median of 5 inits, 256 inputs): alpha 0.25: 58.3; 0.5: 55.7; 1: 62.5; 2: 82.1; 4: 129.0; 8: 218.9. alpha = 1 is the C-39 W=128/lr 1e-3 cell (median max-over-bits time 4000), used as the control. Cells run: alpha = 0.5, 2, 4, 8. Script c98_rescale.py (c34_wide.py with ALPHA), output c98_a<ALPHA>/. Max-over-bits time as in C-39 (evaluation every 100 steps, cap 10,000).
Predictions (written before running; F = 214, Lambda = 238 from C-86, nothing refitted). Formula medians: alpha 0.5: 4500; 2: 3100; 4: 2050; 8: 1300. Hit criteria: (1) the median for alpha 2, 4, 8 is within +-30 percent of those values; (2) medians fall strictly with alpha over 1, 2, 4, 8; (3) alpha 0.5 is not faster than 0.85 x 4000 = 3400. Alternative reading (the time depends only on the function and width): all four medians within +-25 percent of 4000, i.e. in 3000 to 5000. Any other outcome is reported as it is. Note that an effect could also come from the Adam relative step size on the rescaled layers, not from L1 as such; this test cannot separate those two.

### C-98 result (5 seeds per cell, median max-over-bits time in steps; per-seed values in c98_scores.txt, script c98_score.py)
| alpha | gradient L1 of f_4 at init | formula (written before) | measured median | ratio to alpha = 1 |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| 0.5 | 55.7 | 4500 | 4700 | 1.18 |
| 1 (C-39 control) | 62.5 | 4000 | 4000 | 1.00 |
| 2 | 82.1 | 3100 | 3100 | 0.78 |
| 4 | 129.0 | 2050 | 3000 | 0.75 |
| 8 | 218.9 | 1300 | 3400 | 0.85 |
Scoring. (1) alpha 2, 4, 8 within +-30 percent of the formula: alpha 2 HIT (3100 vs 3100), alpha 4 MISSED (3000 vs 2050, +46 percent), alpha 8 MISSED (3400 vs 1300, +162 percent). (2) medians fall strictly over alpha 1, 2, 4, 8: MISSED (4000, 3100, 3000, 3400). (3) alpha 0.5 not faster than 3400: HIT (4700). The alternative reading (function and width only, all four medians in 3000-5000): all four medians are inside that band (alpha 4 sits on the lower edge), so it is NOT contradicted; but the change is not zero either: alpha 0.5 is 18 percent slower and alpha 2/4 are 22-25 percent faster than the control, with per-seed spread of roughly +-25 percent.
Reading. With width and function fixed, raising the gradient L1 by 2.1x (alpha 4) or 3.5x (alpha 8) changes the time only by about 15-25 percent, far less than the formula T = F + Lambda/(lr L1) says (factors 0.51 and 0.33). So in the width series of C-86 the L1 identification is not supported as the cause: the formula describes the width dependence but L1 itself, changed alone, does not move the time that way. Possible reasons I did not test: the effective step per parameter in Adam is lr regardless of gradient size, so rescaling a layer changes the relative step of that layer (alpha 8 makes layer 1 relatively 8x smaller in step, layer 2 8x larger), which may offset the gain; or L1 is simply not the relevant quantity. Caveats: one width and lr, 5 seeds, grid of 100 steps, noise comparable to the 0.75-0.85 effects; the non-monotone point alpha 8 (3400 vs 3000) is inside the noise.
Learned. C-86's formula stays an empirical description of the width x lr grid and must not be described as an explanation through L1. README/STUDY_GUIDE wording to be adjusted accordingly.

## INTAKE 5 (third returned note, "Mathematics tested against the network"; everything in it is an UNVERIFIED LEAD until I reproduce it)
What I did first. (a) Exhaustively checked, over all 65,536 pairs and with my own code (c99_check_note3.py, output c99_check_note3.txt), the identities the note states that I had not yet checked: the Q4 and H4 rows of its alignment table (E[Q4 Fj], E[H4 Fj]), E[Hk Fj] = -2^(j-k) for all j<k<=8, the 1/32 and 1/2 initial slopes, the active-chain law P(L_i = l) = 2^-(l+1) for i = 1..8, the truncated-carry error 2^-(k+1) - 2^-(i+1) for bits 4 and 7, and I(S4;P4) = 1 - h2(15/32) = 0.0028196 bits. RESULT: 107 of 107 checks agree (including a direct computation of I(S4;P4)). (b) The note's "propagate inputs 900 steps" and "random degree-4 5800" rows are my own C-49 numbers (identical per-seed times 600 900 700 1000 900), so they are not independent results. Its new claims are the degree-2 control (6100) and the zero-column control (4300), the intervention numbers, the carry-chain strata, the Hessian audit and the 30 reruns. I have not opened or run its code; its numbers below stay UNVERIFIED until I reproduce them.
Not reproduced yet: LEACE-style erase/repair (my C-91/C-93 patching is a different intervention on a different net), carry-chain strata at plateau checkpoints, the Hessian audit.

## C-99 (CONFIRMATORY; follows C-49; the control I listed as open: is the propagate-input effect just lower parity degree?)
Idea source: my C-49 plus the pending item "degree-2 random-pair control"; the note (INTAKE 5) reports 6,100 for it, which I want to reproduce myself.
Design. Same as C-49 (n=8, joint net with nine sum bits, 3 x 128, Adam lr 1e-3, batch 256, 50 percent of pairs, seeds 0-4, same split and init order, 10,000-step cap, evaluation every 100 steps, same 0.99 persisted rule), with 16 bits plus 8 extra input columns that are (a) 'deg2': XOR of the two coordinates of each pair of a random perfect matching of the 16 raw coordinates, re-drawn until no pair is (a_i, b_i) (so none of them is a true propagate); (b) 'zeros': 8 all-zero columns (dimension-matched, same function as the plain net but a different init stream). Reference from my earlier runs: propagate inputs median max 900 (bit 4: 600); 8 random 4-subset parities 5800 (bit 4: 4200); none 4000 (bit 4: 2600). Script c99_deg2.py, output c99_deg2/ and c99_zeros/.
Predictions (written before running). (1) deg2: median max-over-bits time at least 3000. (2) deg2 at least 3 times the propagate median (at least 2700). (3) zeros: median max between 3000 and 5000 (like 'none'). If (1) or (2) fails, lower parity degree helps and the C-49 reading (the specific feature a_i xor b_i matters) needs revising.

### C-99 result (n=8, 5 seeds, seeds/splits/init order as C-49; no seed censored)
Median max-over-bits time (per seed) and bit-4 median:
| extra input columns | max-over-bits | bit 4 |
|---|---|---|
| propagates p_0..p_7 (C-49) | 900 (600 900 700 1000 900) | 600 |
| 8 random disjoint-pair XORs, no (a_i,b_i) pair (C-99 deg2) | 5700 (5700 5300 6200 5300 7900) | 5500 |
| 8 random 4-subset parities (C-49) | 5800 (4400 to 10000) | 4200 |
| 8 zero columns (C-99) | 4300 (5100 3400 4100 5400 4300) | 4300 |
| none (C-43) | 4000 | 2600 |
Predictions: (1) deg2 at least 3000: HIT (5700). (2) deg2 at least 3 times the propagate median: HIT (6.3 times). (3) zeros between 3000 and 5000: HIT (4300).
Reading. Degree-2 random parity features do not help and are, if anything, slower than no extra input (5700 vs 4000-4300); only the true propagates give the speed-up (900). So lower parity degree does not explain the propagate-input effect; what matters is that the feature is a_i xor b_i of the same position, which is a factor of every sum bit's target. This reproduces the note's claim (its numbers: deg2 6100, zeros 4300; mine 5700 and 4300; matchings differ, so the values are not expected to be identical). Caveats: 5 seeds; one random matching per seed; only n=8, lr 1e-3; the slower-than-none value for deg2 (5700 vs 4000-4300) is within the seed spread and is not claimed.
Status of the note's other claims: still unverified by me (interventions, strata at plateau checkpoints, Hessian audit).

## C-100 (EXPLORATORY-WITH-PREDICTION; follows INTAKE 5; Q2 residual: does the carry-chain length matter for the errors of the joint net?)
Idea source: the returned note's carry-conditioned strata (its numbers unverified); my open item "carry-conditioned errors". Design. Joint net as in C-39 (n=8, 3 x 128, Adam lr 1e-3, batch 256, 50 percent of pairs, seeds 0-4, same split and init), 5000 steps, evaluation every 50 steps on the 32,768 held-out pairs; for each sum bit i = 1..7 the held-out error is counted per active-chain length L_i (0 = no carry into bit i; l >= 1 = carry generated at i-l and propagated up to i; P(L_i = l) = 2^-(l+1), checked exhaustively in c99_check_note3.txt and again on the code's labels). Script c100_strata.py, output c100/strata.csv (seed, step, bit, L, count, errors).
Predictions (written before running). (1) Plateau, bit 4: at the first evaluation where the aggregate bit-4 error is between 20 and 50 percent, the error in the stratum L_4 = 0 is also at least 15 percent in at least 3 of 5 seeds (the plateau error is broad, not only on long chains). (2) Later, at the first evaluation where aggregate bit-4 accuracy is at least 0.99: the error in the longest stratum (L_4 = 4) is at least 3 times the error in the stratum L_4 = 0 in at least 4 of 5 seeds (the tail errors concentrate on long chains). (3) The same ratio L_i,max / L_i=0 at the first 0.99 evaluation is at least 2 for the median over seeds, for each of bits 3, 5, 6. A miss on (2) would say that carry length is not what the last errors depend on.

### C-100 result (n=8, joint net, 5 seeds, evaluation every 50 steps; c100/strata.csv, scored by c100_score.py, c100_scores.txt)
Prediction 1 (plateau error broad): my criterion was badly chosen.
