"First evaluation with aggregate error between 0.2 and 0.5" is step 0 or 150-250 for every seed (the untrained net, error about 0.5), so the "5 of 5 HIT" is meaningless and I do not count it. Post hoc (not predicted) at fixed steps: at steps 300-1000 the bit-4 error is about 0.5 in all strata (seeds 0, 1, 3 at steps 300, 500, 1000); seed 2 (the fastest, solved at about 900) is the exception, with 0.21-0.25 error at L_4 = 0 and 0.5-0.57 at L_4 >= 1 at steps 300-500. At step 1500 seeds 3 and 4 (still on the plateau) show a gradient: error 0.15-0.18 at L = 0 and 0.49-0.59 at L = 4. So the plateau errors are broad early and become chain-length ordered as learning proceeds.
Prediction 2 (error in the longest stratum at least 3 times that of L = 0 at the first evaluation with bit-4 accuracy at least 0.99 in at least 4 of 5 seeds): HIT, 5 of 5; ratios 14.5, 12.7, 107, 22.3, 21.0 (longest-chain error 6.3-20 percent against aggregate 0.75-0.91 percent).
Prediction 3 (median ratio at least 2 for bits 3, 5, 6): HIT; medians 7.7, 21.5, 37.0 (all seeds at least 2).
Reading. At the moment a bit first passes the 0.99 criterion the remaining errors are concentrated on the longest active carry chains (bit 4: 6-20 percent error on chains of length 4 vs 0.2-0.6 percent with no carry), and the concentration is stronger for the higher bits. This agrees with the returned note (it reports 9.6 percent error at the longest chain with 0.44 percent overall for one checkpoint); my numbers are from my own runs. It supports the carry-chain account of Q2 only in the sense 'the long chains are learned last'; it does not tell whether the net computes the carry sequentially or by weighted sum (C-89/C-90 suggest the latter for the first layer). Caveats: 5 seeds, one learning rate; first-hit-at-0.99 selects a moment, error strata at that moment are noisy for the rare long chains (bit 4, L = 4: about 1,000 held-out pairs, so error rates have about 1 percent standard error).

## C-101 (CONFIRMATORY reproduction of a returned-note claim; INTAKE 5, section 6 of the note; my own nets and my own code)
The claim (unverified): at joint-net checkpoints, other outputs' loss adds enough positive curvature along bit 4's most negative-curvature direction to make the joint curvature there positive, although the gradient cosine of bit 4 and the others is near zero; the positive part is mostly Gauss-Newton.
Design. Joint nets of C-39 (seeds 0-2; c101_train_ckpt.py saves parameters and Adam state at steps 500, 1000, 2000; 9 states). 1,024 fixed training examples (numpy seed 7 from the seed's training half). Coordinates: all shared-body parameters (layers 1-3) plus the head row and bias of output 4; the other eight head rows are frozen and excluded. Loss: mean over the 1,024 examples of the BCE of output 4 (L4) or of the sum of the other eight outputs (Lother). Metric: D = diag(1/(sqrt(v_hat)+1e-8)) from the saved Adam state (v_hat bias-corrected), S = D^(1/2) H D^(1/2) (the note's frozen-Adam metric). q = smallest-eigenvalue unit eigenvector of S4 (Lanczos with exact Hessian-vector products, float64). Reported: q'S4q, q'S_other q, the sum, the Gauss-Newton part of q'S_other q (sum_j E[sigma(1-sigma)(J_j q')^2] with q' = D^(1/2) q), the gradient cosine of g4 and g_other on these coordinates, and a control: the mean curvature of S_other on 20 random unit directions (Hutchinson). Script c101_hessian.py, output c101_hessian.csv.
Predictions (written before running). (1) q'S4q < 0 in all 9 states. (2) q'S_other q > 0 in all 9 states. (3) the joint value q'(S4+S_other)q > 0 in at least 7 of 9 states. (4) the Gauss-Newton part is at least 90 percent of q'S_other q in every state. (5) |gradient cosine| < 0.2 in all 9 states. (6) exploratory: q'S_other q is at least 10 times the mean random-direction curvature of S_other. A miss on (1)-(3) means the note's claim does not hold in my nets.

### C-101 result (9 states = seeds 0-2 x steps 500, 1000, 2000; c101_hessian.csv; q'Sq values in the Adam-preconditioned metric)
| seed, step | q'S4q | q'S_other q | joint | Gauss-Newton share | gradient cosine | S_other on q / random-direction mean |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| 0, 500 | -41.1 | 166.5 | 125.4 | 0.99 | 0.011 | 168 |
| 0, 1000 | -62.2 | 165.3 | 103.1 | 1.00 | 0.005 | 152 |
| 0, 2000 | -85.8 | 73.5 | -12.3 | 1.00 | 0.009 | 79 |
| 1, 500 | -34.9 | 121.4 | 86.6 | 1.00 | -0.011 | 107 |
| 1, 1000 | -11.2 | 34.9 | 23.7 | 0.97 | 0.001 | 35 |
| 1, 2000 | -29.2 | 535.2 | 506.0 | 1.00 | -0.019 | 395 |
| 2, 500 | -179.1 | 445.4 | 266.3 | 1.00 | 0.007 | 572 |
| 2, 1000 | -11.5 | 214.4 | 202.9 | 1.00 | 0.054 | 195 |
| 2, 2000 | -2.7 | 6.4 | 3.7 | 0.94 | 0.181 | 6.0 |
Predictions: (1) q'S4q < 0 in all 9: HIT. (2) q'S_other q > 0 in all 9: HIT. (3) joint > 0 in at least 7 of 9: HIT (8 of 9; seed 0 step 2000 is -12.3). (4) Gauss-Newton share at least 90 percent in every state: HIT (0.94 to 1.00). (5) |gradient cosine| < 0.2 in all 9: HIT (largest 0.181, seed 2 step 2000). (6) S_other on q at least 10 times the random-direction mean: MISSED in one state (6.0, seed 2 step 2000), HIT in the other 8 (35 to 572 times).
Reading. The returned note's local claim reproduces on my nets with my code: the other eight outputs add large positive curvature (almost entirely Gauss-Newton, i.e. the ordinary convex-loss curvature of their logits) exactly along the direction in which bit 4's loss curves most negatively, while the gradient cosine is about zero; the sign of the joint curvature there is positive in 8 of 9 states. Numbers differ from the note's (for example seed 0 step 500: mine -41 / +166, its -53 / +64) because my runs and training diagnostic examples are different.
What this does NOT show. (a) It is a local statement at nine checkpoints in one direction; q is not an eigenvector of the joint Hessian, and the joint Hessian still has other negative directions. (b) The positive nuisance curvature is expected for any shared direction that moves other logits; I did not show that it causes the slowdown or the 3.6x. A causal version would, for example, change only the nuisance curvature (a bounded penalty on the other heads' logit change along q) and measure bit-4 time; the C-96 design was invalid and a bounded version has not been run. (c) Adam moments and the changing spectrum are not modelled. Caveats: 3 seeds, 1,024 examples, other head rows frozen, Lanczos tolerance 1e-6.

## C-102 (EXPLORATORY-WITH-PREDICTION; follows C-101; how much of the joint net's slow growth does the local nuisance curvature predict?)
Idea source: the note's reduced model (growth rate = -lambda_min of the Hessian of [target + nuisance] in the Adam metric; without nuisance it is -lambda_min of the target Hessian), which implies a local rate ratio R_local = lambda_min(S4) / lambda_min(S4 + S_other). Both Hessians at the same joint state, same metric D. The measured joint/alone delay is about 10 overall and about 3.6 after the shared-moment part is removed (C-73). I did NOT choose a constant to match; I wrote the range from those measured numbers first.
Design. Joint-net checkpoints of seeds 0-2 at steps 50, 100, 200, 300 (c102_train_ckpt.py; this is the early phase in which bit 4 is still at chance, accuracy about 0.5); same 1,024 examples, coordinates, frozen other head rows and Adam-metric D as C-101. Reported per state: lambda_min(S4), lambda_min(S4 + S_other) (Lanczos, exact Hessian-vector products), their ratio R_local, and whether lambda_min(S4 + S_other) is positive (no local growth direction). Script c102_ratio.py, output c102_ratio.csv.
Predictions (written before running). (1) lambda_min(S4) < 0 at all 12 states. (2) the joint value is larger (less negative) than lambda_min(S4) at all 12 states (nearly forced by S_other being positive semidefinite in its Gauss-Newton part; a miss would show a large negative residual curvature). (3) R_local, median over the 12 states, is between 2 and 6 (the range containing the measured delays 3.6 and 10 within a factor of 2: 1.8 to 20 is the wider band; I take the tighter 2 to 6 as the prediction). (4) lambda_min(S4 + S_other) stays negative (a growth direction remains) in at least 10 of 12 states. A result far outside (3) means local curvature of this kind does not predict the delay size.

### C-102 result (3 seeds x steps 50, 100, 200, 300 = 12 states; c102_ratio.csv, c102_qdir.csv)
lambda_min(S4) (bit 4 alone) and lambda_min(S4 + S_other) (joint) in the Adam-preconditioned metric: the joint value is MORE negative than the bit-4 value in all 12 states (R_local = lambda_min(S4)/lambda_min(joint) from 0.14 to 0.48, median 0.32; e.g. seed 0 step 50: -279 vs -914; seed 1 step 50: -595 vs -4381).
Predictions: (1) lambda_min(S4) < 0 at all 12: HIT. (2) joint less negative than bit 4 alone at all 12: MISSED in all 12. (3) median R_local between 2 and 6: MISSED (0.32). (4) joint lambda_min negative in at least 10 of 12: HIT (12 of 12; uninformative given (2)).
Reading. The smallest eigenvalue of the full joint Hessian is the fastest-growing direction of ANY of the nine outputs (the other bits are still being learned in this phase and their residual curvature is large and negative), so it is not a measure of bit 4's growth, and the ratio R_local cannot be read as a slowdown of bit 4. This operationalisation of the note's model was wrong for the early phase; the prediction was mine and it failed.
Post hoc (not predicted), bit-4 direction as in C-101 at the same 12 states: the nuisance curvature along bit 4's most negative direction q is positive in 8 of 12 states and about zero or negative in 4 (seed 0 steps 100, 200; seed 1 step 300; seed 0 step 300 about 0); the joint curvature on q is positive in only 4 of 12 states (seed 1 steps 50, 100, 200; seed 2 step 300). The Gauss-Newton share is small or erratic (0.0 to 1.7, undefined where the sum is near zero) because in this phase the other heads' own residual curvature is large. So the C-101 pattern (nuisance curvature positive, mostly Gauss-Newton, reverses the sign on q in 8 of 9 states) holds from step 500 on and not in the first 300 steps.
Learned. The local-curvature account, as measured here, does not predict the size of the delay, and in the first 300 steps it does not even give a consistent sign reversal. It remains a candidate for the plateau at steps 500 to 2000 only, with no causal test. Caveats: 3 seeds, 1,024 examples, other head rows frozen, Rayleigh quotient on one direction, early states change fast (steps 50-300), the metric at step 50 comes from 50 Adam steps.

## INTAKE 6 (fourth returned note, "Tested dynamics: forcing, history, width, and activation branches"; UNVERIFIED LEADS until I reproduce them)
Claims I read and chose to test myself: (i) a function-preserving ReLU rescaling (first weights and first hidden bias x s, next hidden weight / s) leaves Adam dynamics exactly unchanged if the learning rate of each parameter group is multiplied by its scale a and epsilon by its gradient scale c (c = 1/a for this map); without the compensation the trajectories differ (note section 6, 3 short checks only). If true this explains my C-98 result (the L1 changed, the time hardly did): the rescaling changes the effective per-layer learning rate, not just L1, and under exact compensation the dynamics are IDENTICAL while L1 differs, so L1 cannot be what sets the time. (ii) after joint training for 500 steps, blocking the other tasks' body gradients gives bit-4 times of 1800, 1900, 400 extra steps with the inherited Adam state, 500, 500, 300 with fresh Adam, and 200, 200, 200 with the initial body (current head, fresh Adam): body history and optimizer history both delay. Not examined yet: duplication width law, the prospective forecast and the changed-gate analysis, the frozen-mean/noise branches.

## C-103 (CONFIRMATORY; follows C-98 and INTAKE 6 (i)): is the function-preserving rescaling exactly compensated by per-group learning rate and epsilon?
Design. The C-98 net (16-128-128-128-9, seed 0 initial weights, float64), rescaled with alpha = 4 in the same way as C-98 (W0 and b0 x alpha, W1 / alpha, everything else unchanged; the function is unchanged). Groups and their parameter scales a: (W0, b0): alpha; W1: 1/alpha; (b1, W2, b2, head): 1. Compensated Adam: learning rate eta*a, epsilon eps/a for each group (c = 1/a). Three runs of 300 steps with the same minibatches (batch 256, 32,768 training pairs, seed 0 split, eta 1e-3): reference (alpha 1), alpha 4 uncompensated, alpha 4 compensated. I compare the logits on 4,096 held-out inputs after 100 and 300 steps, mapped back to the same function.
Predictions (written before running). (1) compensated vs reference: maximum absolute logit difference below 1e-5 at step 300. (2) uncompensated vs reference: above 1e-2 at step 300. If (1) holds, then the time-to-0.99 of the compensated alpha = 4 run equals the reference for every seed (checked below on seeds 0-2 in float32 as a second, separate prediction: each bit-4 and max-over-bits time within 200 steps, i.e. two evaluation steps, of the reference).
Second prediction (C-103b, float32): script c103_time.py (c98_rescale.py with the compensated groups), alpha = 4, seeds 0-4, output c103_a4/. Prediction: every seed's per-bit times equal the alpha = 1 reference rows (c39_W128_lr1e-3) exactly, or within 100 steps (the arithmetic is exact for powers of two, but float32 summation order is unchanged, so I expect identical). Any larger difference would show the compensation does not hold in float32.

## C-104 (CONFIRMATORY reproduction of INTAKE 6 (ii); my own code, 5 seeds)
Design. Joint net (n=8, 3 x 128, Adam 1e-3, batch 256, seeds 0-4, same split and init as C-39) trained 500 steps. Then five continuations that all use the same future minibatches and train the body on bit 4 only (the other outputs' gradient into the body is blocked by detaching the features before the head; all nine head rows keep training on their own loss): (a) current parameters, inherited Adam state; (b) current parameters, fresh Adam (moments and step counter reset); (c) initial parameters (body and head), fresh Adam; (d) current body, initial head, fresh Adam; (e) initial body, current head, fresh Adam. Measured: extra steps after step 500 until the bit-4 held-out accuracy is at least 0.99 on two consecutive evaluations (every 50 steps; the first of the two counted); cap 3,500, censored = -1. Script c104_history.py, output c104/history.csv.
Predictions (written before running; the note's three seeds are not copied). (1) (a) is at least 1.5 times slower than (b) in at least 3 of 5 seeds (stored Adam state slows the recovery). (2) (b) is at least 1.5 times slower than (e) in at least 3 of 5 seeds (the current body is a disadvantage even with Adam reset). (3) (e) and (c) are within 100 steps of each other in at least 4 of 5 seeds (restoring the body removes the disadvantage). (4) the 3.6 ratio the note mentions for seed 0 is a coincidence of its seed: I predict the median (a)/(b) ratio over my seeds is NOT between 3.2 and 4.0. A result opposite to (1) or (2) would mean the note's separation does not hold in my nets.

### C-103 result
(a) float64, 300 steps, seed 0, alpha = 4 (c103_scale_comp.txt): compensated vs reference maximum logit difference 0.0 (exactly zero) at steps 100 and 300; uncompensated 3.9 at step 100 and 12.4 at step 300. Prediction (1) HIT, (2) HIT.
(b) float32 timing (c103_a4/): the compensated alpha = 4 run has per-bit times IDENTICAL to the alpha = 1 reference (c39_W128_lr1e-3) in all 5 seeds. Prediction HIT.
Reading. The function-preserving rescaling plus per-group learning rate x a and epsilon / a reproduces the same training trajectory exactly (verified here, not taken from the note), while the gradient L1 of the logit at initialisation is 129 in the alpha = 4 coordinates against 62.5 in the reference. So the L1 of the gradient in a given parametrisation cannot by itself set the training time: two exactly identical trajectories have different L1. What C-98 changed was the effective per-layer learning rate (in original coordinates: first layer lr/alpha, second layer lr x alpha). Consequence for C-86: the L1 identification is false as stated (it is not reparametrisation-invariant); any law would need to be written in a quantity that is invariant under this map, for example sum over groups of (group lr x group gradient L1), and note that this is exactly lr x L1 in whichever coordinates the optimiser actually runs, which the C-98 test already showed does not track the time well in the uncompensated direction. Caveats: float64 check is one seed and 300 steps; powers of two (alpha = 4) are exact in floating point, other scales would show rounding differences.

### C-104 result (5 seeds; c104/history.csv; extra steps after step 500 to reach bit-4 accuracy 0.99 under bit-4-only body gradients)
| seed | (a) current, inherited Adam | (b) current, fresh Adam | (c) initial all, fresh Adam | (d) current body, initial head | (e) initial body, current head |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| 0 | 1750 | 500 | 200 | 550 | 300 |
| 1 | 1850 | 500 | 200 | 600 | 200 |
| 2 | 350 | 300 | 200 | 350 | 200 |
| 3 | 1250 | 600 | 250 | 600 | 250 |
| 4 | 1300 | 550 | 250 | 650 | 250 |
Predictions: (1) (a) at least 1.5 x (b) in at least 3 of 5 seeds: HIT (4 of 5; ratios 3.5, 3.7, 1.17, 2.08, 2.36). (2) (b) at least 1.5 x (e) in at least 3 of 5: HIT (5 of 5). (3) (e) within 100 steps of (c) in at least 4 of 5: HIT (5 of 5). (4) the median (a)/(b) ratio is not between 3.2 and 4.0: HIT (2.36; seeds 0 and 1 happen to give 3.5 and 3.7, as the note's 3.6 for seed 0).
Reading. The note's three seeds are reproduced closely by my independent code (its 1800/1900/400, 500/500/300, 200/200/200, 600/600/400, 200/200/200 against my 1750/1850/350, 500/500/300, 200/200/200, 550/600/350, 300/200/200), and two more seeds agree in direction. After 500 joint steps, training the body on bit 4 alone is still slowed by (i) the stored Adam state and (ii) the current body parameters; restoring the initial body gives back the fresh-start time, whatever the head. The body-parameter part is the more interesting: it says the 500 joint steps leave the shared body in a state that is worse for bit 4 than the initial body, not just that the Adam moments are stale. Mechanism of that body state: not identified (not tested: which units or which directions). Caveats: five seeds; the intervention blocks only the body gradient of the other outputs, heads keep training; 'fresh Adam' resets moments and step count (bias-correction restart gives large early steps, so (b) may be partly an Adam warm-up effect); the baseline is the continuation, not the C-73 residual. The 3.6 for seed 0 is a coincidence of that seed, not the C-73 ratio (my median over seeds is 2.4).

## INTAKE 7 (fifth returned note, why_research_all (1).zip; UNVERIFIED LEADS; zip extracted by path-checked code into its own directory, no code from it run)
Claims taken from MATH_COMPLETION_TODAY.md that I chose to reproduce myself with my own code: (i) on fresh seeds 20-24 and 30-34 the p4 auxiliary speeds bit 4 and the c4 auxiliary speeds bit 3 in all 10 paired seeds (its table: bit-4 medians p4 700, c4 2,900); (ii) at step 1000 a converged output head fitted on the frozen last hidden layer gives c4-trained nets only 54-60 percent on bit 4 (native 50-52 percent) and p4-trained nets 99.99-100 percent. Claims (distributed alignment interventions, position-forecast criterion, certificates) are not reproduced and stay unverified; I do not use them. The note itself says that the C73 residual and a validated timing law remain open.

## C-105 (CONFIRMATORY; follows C-44, INTAKE 7)
Design. C-44 script logic (n=8, ten-output net with the nine sum bits plus p4 = a4 xor b4 or c4 = carry into bit 4; 3 x 128; Adam 1e-3; batch 256; split RNG 500+seed, init and batch RNG 100*seed+4), seeds 20-24 and 30-34 (the note's fresh seeds, chosen so that a cell-by-cell comparison is possible), 4000 steps, evaluation every 100 steps, first-of-two >= 0.99 rule, unconfirmed = censored at 4000. The step-1000 network is saved. Readout fit: last hidden layer (128 units) of the saved step-1000 net, standardised, L2-regularised logistic regression for bit 4 (lambda = 1e-6, i.e. C = 1/(n lambda)), trained on the 32,768 training pairs, evaluated on the 32,768 held-out pairs; compared with the native bit-4 accuracy. Script c105_aux.py and c105_readout.py, output c105/.
Predictions (written before running). (1) bit-4 time: p4 < c4 in 10 of 10 seeds. (2) bit-3 time: c4 < p4 in 10 of 10 seeds. (3) bit-4 median: p4 between 500 and 900, c4 between 2,400 and 3,500. (4) of the 40 cells (10 seeds x {p4 T4, c4 T4, p4 T3, c4 T3}) at least 24 are within +-200 steps of the note's table (identical seeds and recipe, but different library builds can change a trajectory). (5) readout at step 1000 on the c4 nets (seeds 20-24): fitted bit-4 accuracy at most 65 percent in 5 of 5 (native about 50). (6) readout at step 1000 on the p4 nets: at least 99.5 percent in 5 of 5. A miss on (5) would mean the c4 plateau is a head problem, not a feature problem.

### C-105 result (seeds 20-24 and 30-34; c105_a/, c105_b/, logs c105_a.log, c105_b.log; readout c105_readout.csv and c105_readout_long.csv)
Timing (first-of-two >= 0.99; bit 4 / bit 3): my p4 nets T4 = 800 700 900 600 800 500 700 700 600 800 (median 700); c4 nets T4 = 1900 2800 3400 3200 3700 2400 2700 3000 2800 3900 (median 2,900); c4 T3 = 400 500 500 400 400 500 400 500 400 500; p4 T3 = 2200 1000 1600 1700 2200 1200 1500 1400 1800 1400.
Predictions: (1) p4 faster than c4 for bit 4 in 10 of 10: HIT. (2) c4 faster than p4 for bit 3 in 10 of 10: HIT. (3) medians p4 in 500-900, c4 in 2,400-3,500: HIT (700; 2,900). (4) at least 24 of 40 cells within +-200 steps of the note's table: HIT, 40 of 40 (most cells within 100 steps; for example p4 seed 21 700/1000 against 700/1000, c4 seed 22 3400/500 against 3400/500). The same seed and recipe gives nearly the same trajectories, so the note's training code and mine compute the same thing.
Readout at step 1000 (frozen last hidden layer, L2-regularised logistic fit, lambda = 1e-6, 32,768 train / 32,768 held-out pairs, features standardised): c4 nets: native 50.0-51.3 percent (seed 20: 68.6, already on its way, its T4 is 1,900) and fitted 52.7-58.7 percent (seed 20: 74.4); p4 nets: native 99.5-100 percent, fitted 99.95-100 percent. The first c4 fits hit the iteration limit of 2,000; refitting with 30,000 iterations and tolerance 1e-10 changes held-out accuracy by at most 0.002 (c105_readout_long.csv).
(5) c4 fitted accuracy at most 65 percent in 5 of 5 (seeds 20-24): MISSED in one seed (seed 20, 74.4 percent; the other nine c4 nets, 52.7-58.7). (6) p4 fitted at least 99.5 percent in 5 of 5: HIT (10 of 10).
Reading. The p4/c4 contrast replicates on ten fresh seeds with my own code, and at step 1000 a freshly fitted output head on the stalled c4 networks' last layer is only 53-59 percent accurate on bit 4 (p4 nets: 99.95-100), so the plateau of the c4 network is not a poor-head problem; the last-layer features do not yet carry a usable bit-4 code. This is a statement about this 128-unit last layer and a linear (logistic) head; a nonlinear head or earlier layers might do better (not tested). It also does not say why p4 supervision produces the features. Caveats: seed 20 is a counterexample to 'every c4 net is at chance at step 1000'; fits are regularised (lambda = 1e-6) and not exhaustive. Not reproduced: distributed alignment interventions, the position-forecast and certificate claims.

## C-106 (EXPLORATORY-WITH-PREDICTION; follows C-104: which layer of the joint-trained body carries the disadvantage?)
Idea source: my open item "what does the joint-trained body lose"; C-104 (initial body removes the delay) plus C-94 (the carry-dependent signal is concentrated in layer 1).
Design. C-104 protocol (500 joint steps, then bit-4-only body gradients, heads keep training, same future minibatches, fresh Adam, current head), 5 seeds (0-4), with the body built from layer 1, 2, 3 each taken from the 500-step joint net (1) or the initial net (0): all 8 combinations (name curXYZ: X = layer 1, Y = layer 2, Z = layer 3). cur000 is C-104 (e), cur111 is C-104 (b). Extra steps to bit-4 accuracy 0.99 (first of two, 50-step grid, cap 3,500). Script c106_layerswap.py, output c106/history.csv.
Predictions (written before running). (1) cur100 (only layer 1 current) is at least 1.5 times slower than cur000 in at least 3 of 5 seeds. (2) cur010 and cur001 are each less than 1.5 times slower than cur000 in at least 3 of 5 seeds (layers 2 or 3 alone do not carry it). (3) cur111 is within 25 percent of the slowest single-layer combination (the disadvantage is not additive across layers) in at least 3 of 5 seeds. If (1) fails, the disadvantage is not in layer 1.

### C-106 result (5 seeds; extra steps to bit-4 accuracy 0.99; c106/history.csv, c106_scores.txt; 50-step grid)
| branch (L1 L2 L3: 1 = from the 500-step joint net) | seeds 0-4 | median |
|---|---|---|
| cur000 (all initial; = C-104 e) | 300 200 200 250 250 | 250 |
| cur100 | 250 250 150 250 250 | 250 |
| cur010 | 300 400 250 300 350 | 300 |
| cur001 | 300 250 250 250 350 | 250 |
| cur110 | 450 450 250 400 400 | 400 |
| cur011 | 350 300 300 350 350 | 350 |
| cur101 | 200 250 200 300 250 | 250 |
| cur111 (all current) | 500 500 300 600 550 | 500 |
Predictions: (1) cur100 at least 1.5 x cur000 in at least 3 of 5 seeds: MISSED (0 of 5; layer 1 from the joint net is no disadvantage). (2) cur010 and cur001 each less than 1.5 x cur000 in at least 3 of 5 seeds: HIT (4 of 5 and 5 of 5). (3) cur111 within 25 percent of the slowest single-layer branch in at least 3 of 5 seeds: MISSED (2 of 5): the disadvantage is not carried by a single layer; it grows as more current layers are combined (250 for none, 250-300 for one, 350-400 for two adjacent or L2+L3 or L1+L2, 500 for all three).
Reading. With a fresh Adam state, the body disadvantage of C-104 (250 against 500) is spread over the joint-trained layers 1-3 together, not located in layer 1, although the carry-dependent signal that I found by patching (C-91, C-94) is concentrated in layer 1; combinations with the layer-1 weights from the joint net only become slower when the layer-2 weights are also from the joint net (cur110 400 against cur100 250 and cur010 300). That is compatible with a joint-trained chain of layers that is mutually adapted (so that the layers have to be re-aligned), but it is not a test of that. The effect sizes are small (50 to 250 steps, 1 to 5 grid steps) and 5 seeds, so single-branch differences of 50 steps (for example cur010 against cur000) are not claimed. The larger disadvantage in C-104 is the stored Adam state (1,250-1,850 against 500-600 steps), which is the next thing I decompose.

## C-107 (EXPLORATORY-WITH-PREDICTION; follows C-104, C-106: which part of the stored Adam state slows the recovery?)
Idea source: C-104 (inherited state costs 1,250-1,850 extra steps in four of five seeds, against 500-600 with fresh Adam) and my Adam derivation (a stored second moment from joint training is larger than the bit-4-only gradient needs, so steps are smaller; beta2 = 0.999 forgets it only over about 1,000 steps).
Design. C-104 protocol, but with my own Adam (c107_adamparts.py: independent step counters for the first and the second moment so that 'fresh' really means zero moment and zero counter). After 500 joint steps: four continuations (current parameters, current head, bit-4-only body gradient, same future minibatches): both moments inherited ('both'), neither ('none'), second moment only ('v_only'), first moment only ('m_only'). 5 seeds, cap 3,500, 50-step grid.
Predictions (written before running). (1) implementation check: 'both' and 'none' reproduce the C-104 values (current_inherited, current_freshAdam) within +-100 steps in at least 8 of the 10 cells. (2) v_only recovers most of the gap: with f = ln(t_branch / t_none) / ln(t_both / t_none), the median f of v_only over seeds with t_both / t_none >= 1.5 is at least 0.7. (3) m_only recovers little: median f of m_only at most 0.3. A miss on (2) means the delay is not the stored second moment.

### C-107 result (5 seeds; c107/adamparts.csv; extra steps to bit-4 accuracy 0.99)
| seed | both | none | v only | m only |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| 0 | 1950 | 550 | 1950 | 400 |
| 1 | 1500 | 550 | 1300 | 500 |
| 2 | 350 | 350 | 350 | 350 |
| 3 | 1250 | 500 | 1250 | 500 |
| 4 | 1300 | 550 | 1300 | 500 |
Predictions. (1) 'both'/'none' within +-100 steps of the C-104 inherited/fresh values (1750 1850 350 1250 1300 / 500 500 300 600 550) in at least 8 of 10 cells: HIT, exactly 8 of 10 (the misses are 'both' in seeds 0 and 1: 1950 against 1750 and 1500 against 1850; my own Adam implementation and torch.optim.Adam differ at float level and the first 500 steps are chaotic, so exact reproduction of single cells is not expected). (2) median f(v only) at least 0.7 over seeds with t_both/t_none >= 1.5 (seeds 0, 1, 3, 4): HIT, f = 1.00, 0.85, 1.00, 1.00, median 1.0. (3) median f(m only) at most 0.3: HIT; f = -0.25, -0.10, 0.0, -0.10 (the first moment alone does not slow it; it is, if anything, slightly faster).
Reading. In these continuations the whole extra delay from inheriting Adam's state (1,250-1,950 against 500-550 steps) comes from the stored SECOND moment, not the momentum. That is the shared-second-moment (dilution) mechanism of C-61/C-73 seen as a memory effect: after joint training, v is dominated by the other outputs' large body gradients and is forgotten only over about 1/(1-beta2) = 1,000 steps, so bit 4 moves in small steps during that period. Caveats: five seeds, block-other-gradients continuation (not the ordinary joint run), one seed (2) already fast and uninformative; this identifies WHICH state variable carries the delay, not why joint training makes v large in the first place (it does, by construction: v accumulates the squared gradients of nine outputs).

## C-108 (EXPLORATORY-WITH-PREDICTION; follows C-107: which layer's stored second moment, and how strongly?)
Design. C-107 protocol (500 joint steps; then bit-4-only body gradients, current parameters and head, same future minibatches, fresh first moment), my Adam with per-parameter second-moment counters; arms: all second moments inherited ('all_v'); none; second moment of one group only inherited (L1, L2, L3, head; the rest fresh with counter 0); all second moments inherited and multiplied by 0.1, 0.3, 3 (dose-response). 5 seeds, cap 3,500, 50-step grid. Script c108_adamlayers.py, output c108/adamparts.csv.
Predictions (written before running). (1) dose-response: for the seeds with t_all_v / t_none >= 1.5, the time is non-decreasing over the second-moment multiplier 0.1, 0.3, 1, 3 in at least 3 of 4 such seeds (a stored second moment slows the recovery in proportion to its size). (2) the delay is distributed: for none of the four single groups does the median f = ln(t_group / t_none) / ln(t_all_v / t_none) reach 0.7 (consistent with C-106). (3) head_v alone: median f at most 0.3.

### C-108 result (5 seeds; c108/adamparts.csv, c108_scores.txt; extra steps to bit-4 accuracy 0.99)
| arm | seeds 0-4 |
|---|---|
| none (fresh Adam) | 550 550 350 500 550 |
| head second moment only | 550 600 350 500 650 |
| layer 3 second moment only | 650 600 350 550 600 |
| layer 2 second moment only | 750 700 350 600 700 |
| layer 1 second moment only | 1050 850 400 800 1050 |
| all second moments (C-107 'v only') | 1950 1300 350 1250 1300 |
| all, multiplied by 0.1 | 1050 1100 300 750 850 |
| all, multiplied by 0.3 | 1200 1250 300 950 1050 |
| all, multiplied by 3 | 2550 1450 500 1450 1650 |
Predictions. (1) time non-decreasing over the multiplier 0.1, 0.3, 1, 3 in at least 3 of the 4 delayed seeds: HIT, 4 of 4. (2) no single group reaches median f = 0.7: HIT; median f (share of the log-delay) = layer 1 0.51, layer 2 0.26, layer 3 0.10, head 0.05 (sum 0.92, roughly additive in log time). (3) head only: median f at most 0.3: HIT (0.05).
Reading. The extra recovery time caused by stored Adam state is a dose-response in the size of the stored second moment (more second moment, longer delay) and is spread over the body, largest in the first layer (about half in log time), then layer 2 (a quarter) and layer 3 (a tenth), and not in the head. Even a second moment scaled to 0.1 of its stored size still gives about 750-1,100 steps against 500-550 for a fresh state, so the size of the stored v is not the only thing that matters in this window. Caveat: this is the continuation experiment (bit-4-only body gradient after 500 joint steps), five seeds, seed 2 uninformative; the layer fractions use log time and are not independent of the order in which arms are combined.

## C-109 (EXPLORATORY-WITH-PREDICTION; follows C-104, C-106: how does the body-history cost grow with the number of joint steps?)
Design. C-104 branch (b) (current body and head, fresh torch Adam, body trained on bit 4 only, same future minibatches) after K joint steps, K = 100, 250, 500, 1000, 5 seeds (0-4), cap 3,500, 50-step grid. The accuracy of bit 4 at the switch is recorded in the branch name (acc0); if it is already 0.99 the time can be 50 (first evaluation). C-104 (c) / (e) reference: 200-250 steps from the initial body. Script c109_history_length.py, output c109/history.csv.
Predictions (written before running). (1) K = 100: median extra time within 100 steps of the initial-body reference (225). (2) the median extra time is non-decreasing over K = 100, 250, 500 and at K = 500 is the C-104 value (550 +- 100). (3) K = 1000: median extra time is at least that of K = 500 unless the net has already solved bit 4 (acc0 >= 0.99) in the majority of seeds.

### C-109 result (5 seeds; c109/history.csv; extra steps to bit-4 accuracy 0.99 after the switch, fresh Adam, bit-4-only body gradient)
| K joint steps | seeds 0-4 | median | accuracy of bit 4 at the switch |
|---|---|---|---|
| 0 (C-104 c/e, for reference) | 200-250 | 225 | 0.5 |
| 100 | 350 350 350 300 450 | 350 | 0.499-0.505 |
| 250 | 400 500 350 400 500 | 400 | 0.497-0.588 |
| 500 | 500 500 300 600 550 | 500 | 0.499-0.649 |
| 1000 | 750 600 50 600 500 | 600 | 0.509-0.996 (seed 2 already solved) |
Predictions. (1) K = 100 within 100 steps of 225: MISSED narrowly (350, +125). (2) median non-decreasing over K = 100, 250, 500 and 550 +- 100 at K = 500: HIT (350, 400, 500). (3) K = 1000 at least the K = 500 median: HIT (600; seed 2 had already solved bit 4 and drops out).
Reading. The cost of the joint-trained body for a later bit-4-only recovery appears already after 100 joint steps (350 against 225) and grows slowly with the number of joint steps (about 350, 400, 500, 600 for K = 100, 250, 500, 1000), roughly logarithmically, while bit 4 is still at chance (0.50) at the switch in all but the faster seeds. So the body is changed in a bit-4-unfriendly way from the first hundred steps, long before the plateau ends, and this is not a late effect. Caveats: five seeds, grid 50 steps, medians from 5 values, the K = 1000 value mixes a solved net.

## C-110 (EXPLORATORY-WITH-PREDICTION; follows C-109: which early training makes the body a worse start for bit 4?)
Design. K = 250 pretraining steps (Adam 1e-3, batch 256, seeds 0-4, same split/init as before) with different loss sets on the full network: 'all9' (the nine sum bits, as in the joint net), 'others8' (the eight sum bits other than bit 4 only), 'bit4only', 'random9' (nine fixed random 4-subset parities of the 16 inputs as labels, a different subset per head), and 'none' (no pretraining; the minibatch stream is advanced identically). Then the head is reset to its initial values, Adam is fresh, and the body is trained on bit 4 only (heads keep their ordinary losses) until the bit-4 held-out accuracy is 0.99 (two consecutive 50-step evaluations, first counted; cap 3,500). Script c110_pretrain_kinds.py, output c110/kinds.csv.
Predictions (written before running). (1) 'none' median extra time at most 300 (the C-104 value is 200-250). (2) 'all9' median at least 350 (C-109: 400 at K = 250). (3) 'others8' median at least 350 (the cost comes from the other outputs' content, not from bit 4 itself). (4) 'bit4only' median at most 250 (no cost; bit 4 pretraining should even help). (5) 'random9' median at most 300 (generic movement of the body costs little). A miss on (3) would mean the extra cost needs bit 4's own loss in the mix.

### C-110 result (5 seeds; c110/kinds.csv; K = 250 pretraining steps, then head reset, fresh Adam, bit-4-only body gradient; extra steps to bit-4 accuracy 0.99)
| pretraining labels | seeds 0-4 | median |
|---|---|---|
| none | 200 200 250 200 300 | 200 |
| nine sum bits (all9) | 500 600 350 400 500 | 500 |
| eight sum bits other than bit 4 (others8) | 550 650 400 450 600 | 550 |
| bit 4 only | 50 50 50 50 100 | 50 |
| nine random 4-subset parities | 300 300 500 400 450 | 400 |
Predictions. (1) none at most 300: HIT (200). (2) all9 at least 350: HIT (500). (3) others8 at least 350: HIT (550). (4) bit4only at most 250: HIT (50; after 250 steps of bit-4-only training bit 4 is already solved, and a RESET head is also 99.3 percent accurate, because the body adapted to the almost unchanged head row, change at most 0.19; I checked this directly, it is not a bug). (5) random9 at most 300: MISSED (400).
Reading. The cost for a later bit-4 recovery comes with training the body on the other sum bits (550 against 200 with no pretraining), does not need bit 4's own loss (others8 is as costly as all9), and is not specific to the sum structure alone: 250 steps of body training on random parities cost 400, so about 200 of the 350 extra steps is generic displacement of the body from its initial state and about 150 is specific to the sum bits (550 against 400, a difference of the order of the seed spread, so only suggestive). Caveats: five seeds; 50-step grid; the random-parity labels differ in learning difficulty from the sum bits; the head is reset, which differs from the C-104/C-109 protocol (current head).

# Phase "answer the whys again" (26 Oct 2026, afternoon)
Open whys at the start of this phase: (W1) why is the joint-trained body a worse start for bit 4 (C-104, C-106, C-109, C-110)? (W2) what is the remaining ~3.6x after the shared second moment is separated (C-73)? (W3) why do some initial weights give a fast joint bit 4 (C-62, C-83)? (W4) why does a frozen random layer hurt (C-77, C-78)? (W5) why do width, learning rate and initial scale shorten the plateau (C-35, C-36, C-39, C-66)?
Quick check for W3 from existing data: over the same 40 initial weights, the bit-4-ALONE time (C-82, 175-275 steps) and the JOINT bit-4 time (C-83, 900-6,000) have Spearman -0.20 (p = 0.21). So what makes an initialisation fast is not that bit 4 is easy for it on its own; it lies in the joint interplay.

## C-111 (EXPLORATORY-WITH-PREDICTION; W1): what does pretraining change in the body, and which change tracks the later bit-4 recovery cost?
Idea source: C-106 (the cost needs layer 2 together with layer 1 and/or 3), C-109 (it appears within 100 joint steps and grows with K), C-110 (random-parity pretraining also costs, others8 costs most). Candidate body changes, each a known cause of slower later learning in the 'loss of plasticity' literature (to be opened and checked before I cite it): dead or dormant ReLU units, collapse of the effective rank of the hidden features, growth of the weight norm (which lowers the relative step of Adam's per-parameter step of about lr), and, specific to this task, weight mass moved to input columns that are irrelevant to bit 4 (positions 5-7).
Design. Seeds 0-4. Pretraining with Adam 1e-3, batch 256 from the usual initial weights on: nothing ('none_K0'); the nine sum bits (snapshots K = 100, 250, 500, 1000); the eight other sum bits ('others8_K250'); nine random 4-subset parities ('random9_K250'). At each snapshot, on 8,192 training inputs and per hidden layer: fraction of dead units (zero on all inputs), fraction of dormant units (mean activation below 0.1 of the layer average), effective rank (exp of the entropy of the normalised singular values of the centred features) and participation ratio, weight-norm ratio ||W||/||W_0||, relative change ||W - W_0||/||W_0||, active fraction; and for layer 1 the weight mass on input columns of positions 5-7 relative to positions 0-4, divided by its initial value. Then the C-110 continuation: head reset to its initial values, fresh Adam, body trained on bit 4 only (all heads train), a continuation minibatch stream that is the same for every condition of a seed; cost = steps to bit-4 held-out accuracy 0.99 (first of two 50-step evaluations, cap 3,500). Script c111_body_diag.py, outputs c111_a/, c111_b/ (diag.csv).
Predictions (written before running). (1) Dead units do not explain the cost: dead fraction below 0.05 in every layer and state. (2) The cost reproduces earlier values: none about 200-250, all9 rising with K, others8 at least as costly as all9_K250, random9 between none and others8. (3) Of the candidate measures, layer-2 effective rank falls with K and is lower after others8 than after random9; over the 35 (seed, state) points its Spearman correlation with the cost is at most -0.5. (4) Layer-1 irrelevant-input mass ratio is above 1 after all9 and others8 and its Spearman with the cost is at least +0.3. No prediction for the norm ratio (both directions are plausible to me). These correlations are descriptive; the causal test (C-112) will target whichever measure tracks the cost.

### C-111 result (5 seeds; c111_a/diag.csv, c111_b/diag.csv; c111_score.py -> c111_scores.txt). Medians over seeds:
| state | cost (steps) | L2 effective rank | L3 effective rank | L2 participation ratio | L2 norm ratio | L2 active fraction | L3 dead fraction | L1 irrelevant-input mass ratio |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| none_K0 | 250 | 75.7 | 69.5 | 15.7 | 1.00 | 0.47 | 0.07 | 1.00 |
| all9_K100 | 350 | 40.5 | 14.9 | 2.3 | 1.27 | 0.76 | 0.20 | 1.04 |
| all9_K250 | 450 | 30.6 | 12.9 | 2.0 | 1.65 | 0.76 | 0.20 | 0.97 |
| all9_K500 | 650 | 29.1 | 13.6 | 1.8 | 2.06 | 0.76 | 0.20 | 0.95 |
| all9_K1000 | 700 | 28.8 | 14.1 | 1.9 | 2.65 | 0.77 | 0.20 | 0.96 |
| others8_K250 | 500 | 30.8 | 13.4 | 2.0 | 1.65 | 0.77 | 0.20 | 1.00 |
| random9_K250 | 350 | 56.2 | 43.9 | 6.5 | 1.48 | 0.51 | 0.07 | 0.93 |
Per-seed costs: none 250 200 300 250 300; all9 K100 350 350 300 300 350; K250 500 600 350 450 450; K500 700 850 400 450 650; K1000 900 700 100 700 600 (seed 2 had nearly solved bit 4); others8 500 550 400 500 450; random9 350 350 400 300 300.
Predictions. (1) dead fraction below 0.05 everywhere: MISSED (layer 3 has 0.06-0.10 dead units already at initialisation and 0.20-0.29 after joint training; layer 2 up to 0.10). Dead units exist, but their Spearman with the cost is +0.22 (layer 2) and +0.54 (layer 3). (2) costs reproduce the earlier pattern: HIT. (3) layer-2 effective rank falls with K, is lower after others8 than random9, Spearman with cost at most -0.5: HIT (75.7 -> 40.5 -> 30.6 -> 29.1 -> 28.8; others8 30.8 against random9 56.2; Spearman -0.79). (4) layer-1 irrelevant-input mass above 1 after joint training and Spearman at least +0.3: MISSED (0.95-1.04; Spearman -0.50), so the 'weight moved to irrelevant high inputs' idea is not supported.
Other Spearman correlations with the cost (35 points): layer-2 norm ratio +0.74, layer-3 effective rank -0.71, layer-1 participation ratio -0.74, layer-2 active fraction +0.59.
Reading. Joint training collapses the hidden representation fast: within 100 steps the layer-2 participation ratio drops from about 16 to about 2 (one or two directions carry most of the variance) and the layer-3 effective rank from about 70 to about 15; layer-2 units become active on 76 percent of inputs instead of 47 percent (the layer behaves more linearly); some layer-3 units die. Random-parity pretraining collapses much less (L2 PR 6.5, L3 erank 44) and costs less (350 against 450-500). The layer-2 weight norm keeps growing with K (1.27, 1.65, 2.06, 2.65) while the rank stops falling after K = 250, and the cost keeps growing (450, 650, 700), so the two candidates (representation collapse and norm growth) are both correlated with the cost and partly confounded with K. C-112 separates them by intervention.

## C-112 (CONFIRMATORY; W1, follows C-111): norm growth or feature geometry?
Design. The all9_K500 body of C-111 (same seeds, same pretraining), then one of five arms before the identical C-111 continuation (head reset, fresh Adam, bit-4-only body gradient, fixed continuation stream): 'base' (no change; must reproduce the C-111 all9_K500 costs 700 850 400 450 650); 'N_all' (each layer's weights rescaled to their initial Frobenius norm, biases scaled by the cumulative factor so that every ReLU on/off pattern and hence the feature geometry is unchanged; only the scale per layer changes); 'N_L2' (the same for layer 2 only); 'D_L2' (layer-2 rows replaced by their initial directions but each keeping its trained norm: geometry of layer 2 restored, norm kept); 'R_L2' (layer 2 reset to its initial weights, an anchor like C-106 cur101). Gap fraction removed g = (cost_base - cost_arm) / (cost_base - cost_none), cost_none from C-111 (250 200 300 250 300). Script c112_norm_vs_geometry.py, output c112/norm_geom.csv.
Predictions (written before running). Norm-growth account: N_all and N_L2 remove at least half the gap (median g >= 0.5) and D_L2 removes less than a quarter (median g < 0.25). Geometry account: N_all and N_L2 have median g < 0.25 and D_L2 median g >= 0.5. I predict the GEOMETRY account (because random-parity pretraining grew the layer-2 norm almost as much, 1.48 against 1.65, but cost less, and the rank collapse is the larger difference). Implementation check: 'base' reproduces the C-111 values exactly. R_L2 median g >= 0.5 (from C-106).

## C-113a (EXPLORATORY-WITH-PREDICTION; W1/W5): is the collapsed direction the numeric sum? (a 'magnitude code' hypothesis)
Idea (mine, from C-111): a sum bit i is a periodic function of the numeric sum S = a + b with period 2^(i+1); over S = 0..510 it changes sign about 511 / 2^i times (bit 8: 1, bit 7: 3, bit 6: 7, bit 5: 15, bit 4: 31, bit 3: 63). If the joint net builds a one-dimensional code of S (which serves the upper bits with few ReLU breakpoints), that code is nearly useless for bit 4 (31 breakpoints), while the local route (parity of a4, b4 times the carry) needs inputs from 5 positions. The middle bit is expensive by both routes, which would give the hump; and the collapse onto S would explain why the joint-trained body is a bad start for bit 4. This is a hypothesis; C-113a only checks its first premise.
Design. Pretraining as C-111 (seeds 0-4, K = 250) on all9, others8, random9, and 'upper4' (sum bits 5-8 only) and 'lower4' (sum bits 0-3 only); on 8,192 training inputs: |Pearson correlation| between S and the first principal component of layer-2 and layer-3 features, and the variance-weighted R^2 of a linear regression of each layer's features on S. Script c113a_magnitude.py, output c113a.csv.
Predictions (written before running). (1) all9: |corr(PC1 of layer 2, S)| at least 0.9 in at least 4 of 5 seeds. (2) all9: variance-weighted R^2 on S at least 0.6 in layer 2 in at least 4 of 5 seeds; random9: at most 0.3 in at least 4 of 5. (3) upper4 at least as S-dominated as all9 (median R^2 within 0.1 or higher); lower4 less S-dominated (median R^2 at most 0.4).

### C-112 result (5 seeds; c112/norm_geom.csv). Cost (steps) per seed and gap fraction g (median):
| arm | seeds 0-4 | median g |
|---|---|---|
| base (all9_K500 body) | 700 850 400 450 650 (identical to C-111) | 0 |
| N_all: every layer back to its initial norm, ReLU patterns unchanged | 400 350 250 300 350 | 0.77 |
| N_L2: layer 2 back to its initial norm, patterns unchanged | 500 450 350 350 400 | 0.50 |
| D_L2: layer-2 directions back to initial, trained row norms kept | 300 250 300 250 250 | 1.00 |
| R_L2: layer 2 reset to its initial weights | 300 250 250 300 250 | 0.92 |
Predictions. Implementation check (base = C-111): HIT. R_L2 g >= 0.5: HIT (0.92). Norm account (N >= 0.5 and D < 0.25): MISSED (D_L2 removes the whole gap). Geometry account (N < 0.25 and D >= 0.5), which I predicted: MISSED (N_all removes 0.77 and N_L2 0.50 of the gap). Neither pure account holds.
Reading. Restoring layer 2's initial directions while KEEPING its grown norm removes the whole cost, so the large norm by itself does no harm; shrinking the weights back to their initial norm while KEEPING the trained directions (and every ReLU on/off pattern) removes half (layer 2 only) to three quarters (all layers). So the cost needs both: trained directions that must be changed, and a large norm that makes changing them slow. A simple reading consistent with both arms: under Adam each parameter moves by about lr per step whatever its size, so the angle by which a weight row can turn per step falls as 1/(row norm); the time to turn the collapsed layer-2 rows away from their trained direction grows with their norm. This is my derivation, not yet a tested law; C-115 tests it prospectively.

### C-113a result (5 seeds; c113a.csv; K = 250 pretraining). Median |corr(PC1, S)| and variance-weighted R^2 of the features on S = a + b:
| pretraining | L2 PC1 corr | L2 R^2 on S | L2 PC1 share | L3 PC1 corr | L3 R^2 on S |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| none (init) | 0.21 | 0.05 | 0.14 | 0.30 | 0.04 |
| all9 | 0.99 | 0.68 | 0.69 | 0.99 | 0.81 |
| others8 | 0.99 | 0.67 | 0.69 | 0.99 | 0.81 |
| upper4 (sum bits 5-8) | 0.99 | 0.85 | 0.87 | 0.99 | 0.88 |
| lower4 (sum bits 0-3) | 0.08 | 0.02 | 0.42 | 0.08 | 0.01 |
| random9 | 0.11 | 0.04 | 0.34 | 0.17 | 0.02 |
Per seed, all9 layer-2 PC1 correlation 0.986-0.993 and R^2 0.60-0.72; random9 R^2 0.02-0.09.
Predictions: (1) all9 |corr| >= 0.9 in at least 4 of 5: HIT (5 of 5). (2) all9 R^2 >= 0.6 in at least 4 of 5 (5 of 5) and random9 <= 0.3 in at least 4 of 5 (5 of 5): HIT. (3) upper4 at least as S-dominated (0.85 against 0.68) and lower4 at most 0.4 (0.02): HIT.
Reading. After 250 steps of joint training, about two thirds of the layer-2 variance and four fifths of the layer-3 variance is a linear function of the numeric sum S = a + b: the network has built a one-dimensional 'magnitude code' of the sum. The upper sum bits alone build it even more strongly; the lower bits and random parities do not build it at all. This is the collapsed direction of C-111. Whether this code is what slows bit 4 is tested in C-113b by giving bit 4 other companions that build an S-code or a code of an unrelated direction.

## C-113b (CONFIRMATORY; W1/W5): is it the code of the numeric sum that slows bit 4, or any dominant easy direction?
Design. Network with 3 x 128 hidden units and an output for bit 4 plus four companion outputs, trained jointly (Adam 1e-3, batch 256, 50 percent of pairs, seeds 0-4, init seed 100*seed+4, evaluation every 50 steps, cap 6,000; bit-4 time = first of two held-out evaluations >= 0.99). Companions: 'alone' (none); 'upper4' (true sum bits 5-8; the known slow case); 'Sthr4' (four thresholds of the numeric sum, [S >= t_k], t_k at random quantiles 0.2-0.8 of S, drawn per seed; linearly separable, they build an S-code); 'Rthr4' (four thresholds of random positive directions, [w_k.x >= t_k], w_k uniform(0,1) on the 16 inputs, thresholds at the same quantiles; equally linearly separable and balanced, but along directions unrelated to S). Also recorded: variance-weighted R^2 of layer-2 features on S and on the first random direction at step 250. Script c113b_companions.py, outputs c113b_a/, c113b_b/.
Predictions (written before running). (1) upper4 slows bit 4 at least 4 times relative to alone in at least 4 of 5 seeds. (2) Sthr4 builds an S-code (layer-2 R^2 on S at least 0.5 in at least 4 of 5 seeds) and Rthr4 does not (at most 0.3 in at least 4 of 5). (3) S-specific account: Sthr4 slows bit 4 at least 2 times more than Rthr4 in at least 4 of 5 paired seeds. The generic alternative (any dominant easy direction hurts) predicts Sthr4 and Rthr4 within a factor 1.5. I do not know which will hold; (3) states the S-specific account as the prediction.

### C-113b result (5 seeds; c113b_a/, c113b_b/companions.csv). Bit-4 time (steps) and layer-2 R^2 on S at step 250:
| seed | alone | upper4 | Sthr4 | Rthr4 | R^2 on S: upper4 / Sthr4 / Rthr4 |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| 0 | 250 | 1950 | 1000 | 1600 | 0.85 / 0.85 / 0.22 |
| 1 | 200 | 1250 | 1300 | 1450 | 0.85 / 0.73 / 0.18 |
| 2 | 250 | 1150 | 1650 | 1900 | 0.84 / 0.82 / 0.10 |
| 3 | 250 | 2250 | 1050 | 1850 | 0.86 / 0.85 / 0.11 |
| 4 | 300 | 1950 | 1450 | 1700 | 0.84 / 0.76 / 0.11 |
Medians: alone 250, upper4 1950, Sthr4 1300, Rthr4 1700.
Predictions. (1) upper4 at least 4 times alone in at least 4 of 5: HIT (4.6-9.0 times, 5 of 5). (2) Sthr4 builds an S-code (R^2 >= 0.5) and Rthr4 does not (<= 0.3): HIT (0.73-0.85 against 0.10-0.22). (3) S-specific account (Sthr4 at least 2 times slower than Rthr4 in 4 of 5): MISSED; Sthr4 is FASTER than Rthr4 in all 5 seeds (Rthr4 / Sthr4 = 1.6, 1.1, 1.15, 1.8, 1.2). The generic alternative (within a factor 1.5) holds in 3 of 5 seeds and the rest go the other way.
Reading. Four easy companion targets slow bit 4 by 4-9 times whatever their direction: thresholds of the numeric sum (1,300), thresholds of random directions that have nothing to do with the sum (1,700), and the true upper sum bits (1,950). So the harm is NOT specific to the magnitude code of the sum; the sum code is simply what the upper sum bits build. What the companions share is that they are quickly learnable (linearly separable or nearly so) and collapse the hidden layers onto their few directions early (C-111/C-113a). Random 4-parities, which this network learns slowly, collapse less and cost less (C-110, C-111). The magnitude-code reading of the hump (C-113a's idea) is therefore not the cause of bit 4's slowdown; I keep only the descriptive finding that the joint net's layers 2-3 become an S-code.

## C-114 (CONFIRMATORY; W2): where does the residual of C-73 come from?
Idea source: C-73 (separate second moments: bit 4 at 900 against 250 alone), C-106/C-112 (the cost of a joint-trained body sits in layer 2's directions together with neighbouring layers), C-113b (easy companions collapse the hidden layers and slow bit 4 whatever their direction).
Design. The C-73 optimiser (each output has its own Adam moments in the shared layers and contributes its own full Adam step; head with ordinary Adam), with the other eight outputs' updates removed from chosen hidden layers ('none' = C-73; 'L1', 'L2', 'L3', 'L23'; 'all' = other outputs never move the trunk); bit 4's own update always applies to every layer, all heads train normally. n = 8, seeds 0-4, batch 256, lr 1e-3, 50-step evaluation, cap 6,000. Note the evaluation grid is 50 here (C-73 also used 50). Script c114_sep_block.py, outputs c114_a/, c114_b/.
Predictions (written before running). With f = ln(t_none / t_arm) / ln(t_none / t_all) (share of the log-gap removed), median over seeds: (1) 'none' reproduces C-73 (median 900 +- 150). (2) 'all' median at most 350. (3) L23 removes at least 0.7 of the log-gap. (4) L2 removes at least 0.5. (5) L1 removes at most 0.3. A result where L1 removes most would contradict the layer-2 location found in the continuation experiments.

### C-114 result (5 seeds; c114_a/, c114_b/sepblock.csv; bit-4 time in steps; -1 = not reached by 6,000)
| layers the other outputs may NOT update | seed 0 | 1 | 2 | 3 | 4 | median |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| none (C-73 optimiser) | 800 | 1200 | 850 | 1250 | 900 | 900 |
| L1 | 600 | 400 | 500 | 450 | 550 | 500 |
| L2 | 350 | 750 | 550 | 350 | 550 | 550 |
| L3 | 5850 | 3550 | >6000 | >6000 | 4000 | >4000 |
| L2 and L3 | >6000 | 2000 | 2850 | >6000 | >6000 | >6000 |
| all three | 200 | 200 | 250 | 250 | 300 | 250 |
Share of the log-gap removed (median): L1 0.45, L2 0.45; L3 and L2+L3 make bit 4 much slower (negative shares).
Predictions. (1) none 900 +- 150: HIT (900). (2) all at most 350: HIT (250). (3) L2+L3 removes at least 0.7: MISSED badly (it makes bit 4 3-7 times slower, three seeds censored). (4) L2 removes at least 0.5: MISSED (0.45). (5) L1 removes at most 0.3: MISSED (0.45).
Reading. My layer-2 localisation was wrong for training from the start. Keeping the other outputs out of layer 1 or out of layer 2 each removes about half of the residual, but keeping them out of layer 3 (alone or with layer 2) makes bit 4 far slower than the unmodified C-73 run. A reading that fits all six arms (post hoc): what hurts bit 4 is how strongly the other outputs reshape the LOWER layers that bit 4 also needs; when they cannot use layer 3, they can only reach their heads by reshaping layers 1-2 even more, and bit 4 suffers more; when they are kept out of one lower layer, they reshape less of the path. This predicts that letting the other outputs update ONLY layer 3 (and their heads) gives about the 'all' time. C-114b tests that prospectively and records the collapse at step 250 in each arm.

## C-114b (CONFIRMATORY; W2, prospective test of the C-114 reading)
Design. As C-114 (same seeds, optimiser, evaluation), arms none, L12 (the other outputs may update only layer 3; new), L1, L2, L3, all; at step 250 the participation ratio of layers 1-3, the variance-weighted R^2 of layers 2 and 3 on the numeric sum S and the layer-2 weight-norm ratio are recorded (the run continues to step 250 even when bit 4 is solved earlier; recording does not change the training). Script c114b_sep_block_diag.py, outputs c114b_a/, c114b_b/.
Predictions (written before running). (1) L12 median at most 400 (close to 'all', 250): keeping the other outputs out of both lower layers removes almost the whole residual. (2) The re-run arms reproduce the C-114 times exactly (determinism check). (3) Over the 30 runs, Spearman between layer-2 R^2 on S at step 250 and the bit-4 time is at least +0.5, and the L3 arm has the highest median layer-2 R^2 on S.

## C-117 (EXPLORATORY-WITH-PREDICTION; W3): do fast initial weights collapse less?
Idea source: C-111 to C-114 (the joint delay goes with how strongly the other outputs reshape and collapse the shared layers) and the open W3 (73 percent of the variance of log bit-4 time is between initial weights, C-62; the alone time does not predict it, Spearman -0.20).
Design. The 40 initial weights of C-83 (torch seed 10000+s, split seed 500, batch stream seed 777, same recipe), re-run for 500 joint steps; at steps 0, 100, 250, 500: layer-2 participation ratio, layer-2 and layer-3 variance-weighted R^2 on the numeric sum S, layer-2 norm ratio (8,192 training inputs). Compared with the known C-83 joint bit-4 times (900 to 6,000). Script c117_init_collapse.py, output c117_init_collapse.csv.
Predictions (written before running). (1) Spearman between the layer-2 participation ratio at step 250 and the time at most -0.4 (less collapse, faster). (2) Spearman between layer-2 R^2 on S at step 250 and the time at least +0.4. (3) None of the step-0 measures has |Spearman| above 0.3 (the untrained net's collapse measures should not predict, in line with C-82 to C-85).

### C-117 result (40 initial weights; c117_init_collapse.csv, c117_scores.txt)
Every initial weight collapses about equally: at step 250 the layer-2 participation ratio is 1.67-2.41 and the layer-2 R^2 on S 0.61-0.76 for all 40. Spearman with the joint bit-4 time (900-6,000): layer-2 PR at step 250 +0.03; layer-2 R^2 on S at step 250 -0.04; the largest |Spearman| of any measure at any step is 0.30 (layer-3 R^2 on S at step 100).
Predictions. (1) PR at 250 Spearman at most -0.4: MISSED (+0.03). (2) R^2 on S at 250 at least +0.4: MISSED (-0.04). (3) no step-0 measure above 0.3 in |Spearman|: HIT (at most 0.09).
Reading. The collapse is the same for fast and slow initial weights, so the large init-to-init spread of the joint bit-4 time (6.7 times) is not explained by how much the shared layers collapse. Whatever the initial weights decide acts on bit 4's own escape from the collapsed state, not on the collapse. C-118 localises which part of the initial weights carries it by swapping layers between fast and slow initialisations.

## C-118 (EXPLORATORY-WITH-PREDICTION; W3): which part of the initial weights makes a joint run fast?
Design. Three pairs of C-83 initial weights, each a fast and a slow one: (38: 900 steps, 35: 6,000), (23: 1,200, 5: 5,800), (28: 1,200, 37: 5,750). For every pair, all 16 networks whose layer 1, layer 2, layer 3 and output layer each come from the fast (F) or the slow (S) initialisation; trained exactly as C-83 (split seed 500, batch stream 777, Adam 1e-3, batch 256, evaluation every 50 steps, cap 9,000). Analysis: least-squares fit of log(bit-4 time) on the four 0/1 indicators 'component from S', per pair; share of the explained sum of squares carried by each component (indicators are orthogonal in the full 2^4 design). Script c118_init_swap.py, outputs c118_a/, c118_b/.
Predictions (written before running). (1) FFFF and SSSS reproduce the C-83 times within 50 steps. (2) The effect is distributed: in at least 2 of 3 pairs no single component carries more than 50 percent of the explained sum of squares. (3) The fit with four main effects explains at least 60 percent of the variance of log time in at least 2 of 3 pairs (interactions small).

## C-115 (CONFIRMATORY, prospective; W1): does the extra time scale as 1/(layer-2 step size)?
Derivation (mine, from C-112). Adam moves every parameter by about lr per step, so a weight row of norm r can turn by an angle of about lr*sqrt(fan_in)/r per step. If the extra recovery time of the joint body is the time to turn the collapsed layer-2 rows, it should be inversely proportional to layer 2's learning rate: extra(m) = extra(1)/m, where m multiplies layer 2's Adam learning rate only.
Design. C-111 all9_K500 body, seeds 0-4; continuation as C-111 (head reset, fresh Adam, bit-4-only body gradient, same continuation stream) with layer 2's learning rate multiplied by m = 0.5, 1, 2, 4 (all other parameters lr 1e-3); started from the trained body ('base') or from the same body with layer-2 directions restored at the trained row norms ('D_L2', the C-112 floor, where nothing needs to turn). extra(m) = cost_base(m) - cost_D_L2(m). Script c115_rotation.py, output c115/rotation.csv.
Predictions (written before running). (1) base m = 1 reproduces C-111 (700 850 400 450 650). (2) Median over seeds of extra(m)/extra(1) lies within a factor 1.5 of 1/m for m = 2 (0.33-0.75) and m = 4 (0.17-0.38), and of 2 for m = 0.5 (1.33-3.0). Seeds whose extra(1) is below 150 steps (3 grid steps) are excluded from the ratio as too noisy. A ratio near 1 for m = 2 and 4 would mean that turning layer 2 is not the rate-limiting step.

### C-114b result (5 seeds; c114b_a/, c114b_b/sepblock.csv; c114b_scores.txt). Bit-4 time and step-250 diagnostics (medians):
| arm (layers the other outputs may NOT update) | bit-4 times, seeds 0-4 | median | layer-2 R^2 on S | layer-3 R^2 on S | layer-2 norm ratio |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| none (C-73 optimiser) | 800 1200 850 1250 900 | 900 | 0.37 | 0.64 | 2.57 |
| L1+L2 (others update only layer 3) | 300 250 300 250 350 | 300 | 0.05 | 0.02 | 1.39 |
| L1 | 600 400 500 450 550 | 500 | 0.07 | 0.16 | 2.63 |
| L2 | 350 750 550 350 550 | 550 | 0.10 | 0.12 | 1.38 |
| L3 | 5850 3550 >6000 >6000 4000 | 5850 | 0.43 | 0.51 | 3.10 |
| all | 200 200 250 250 300 | 250 | 0.01 | 0.01 | 1.43 |
Predictions. (1) L1+L2 median at most 400: HIT (300). (2) re-run arms identical to C-114: HIT (25 of 25 runs identical). (3) Spearman between layer-2 R^2 on S at step 250 and the bit-4 time at least +0.5, and the L3 arm has the highest median layer-2 R^2: HIT (+0.87; L3 0.43 is the highest). Layer-3 R^2 on S: +0.86; layer-2 norm ratio +0.64; participation ratios do not track the time (+0.16, +0.03, -0.24).
Reading. In training from the start with the shared second moment removed, the remaining slowdown of bit 4 (900 against 250) disappears when the other outputs are kept out of layers 1 and 2 (300), and across all six arms the bit-4 time follows how much of layers 2-3 the other outputs' code (the S-code) occupies at step 250 (Spearman +0.87), not the participation ratio as such. So the residual of C-73 is the other outputs taking over the lower shared layers with their own code. Not explained quantitatively: why keeping the other outputs out of layer 3 makes bit 4 so much slower (5,850 against 900) while their layer-2 code is only a little stronger (0.43 against 0.37).

## C-116 (CONFIRMATORY; W4, W5): is the width and frozen-layer dependence of the joint delay the other outputs' takeover of the lower layers?
Design. Plain Adam (shared moments, lr 1e-3, batch 256), n = 8, seeds 0-4, evaluation every 50 steps, cap 10,000. Configurations: hidden width W = 64, 128, 512 (3 trainable hidden layers), and a frozen random ReLU layer of width 1,024 in front of the 3 x 128 trainable layers (as C-77). Arms: 'joint' (ordinary), 'conf3' (the other eight outputs' gradient reaches only the last hidden layer and the heads; bit 4's gradient reaches everything), 'alone' (the other outputs' gradient reaches only the heads; equals C-59 lam = 0). Recorded: bit-4 time, layer-2 and layer-3 R^2 on S at step 250. Script c116_confine.py, outputs c116_W{W}_FW{FW}/.
Predictions (written before running). (1) conf3 median at most 2 times alone in every configuration (confining the other outputs to the top layer removes most of the delay at every width and with the frozen layer; the shared second moment of layer 3 and the head remains, so not all). (2) joint replicates: joint/alone median ratio larger at W = 64 than at W = 512, and frozen-1024 joint slower than W = 128 joint. (3) Over the 20 joint runs, Spearman between layer-2 R^2 on S at step 250 and the bit-4 time at least +0.5.

### C-115 result (5 seeds; c115/rotation.csv, c115_scores.txt). Cost (steps) for layer-2 learning-rate multiplier m = 0.5, 1, 2, 4:
| seed | from the joint body | from the joint body with layer-2 directions restored (floor) | extra = difference |
|---|---|---|---|
| 0 | 1000 700 600 500 | 300 300 250 300 | 700 400 350 200 |
| 1 | 750 850 550 400 | 300 250 250 200 | 450 600 300 200 |
| 2 | 400 400 300 300 | 350 300 250 350 | 50 100 50 -50 (excluded, extra(1) < 150) |
| 3 | 700 450 350 350 | 300 250 300 200 | 400 200 50 150 |
| 4 | 750 650 400 350 | 300 250 250 250 | 450 400 150 100 |
Median of extra(m)/extra(1) over seeds 0, 1, 3, 4: m = 0.5: 1.44 (law 2.0); m = 2: 0.44 (law 0.5); m = 4: 0.42 (law 0.25).
Predictions. (1) base m = 1 reproduces C-111: HIT. (2) within a factor 1.5 of 1/m: m = 0.5 HIT (1.44 in 1.33-3.0), m = 2 HIT (0.44 in 0.33-0.75), m = 4 MISSED (0.42, above 0.38).
Reading. Changing only layer 2's step size changes the extra recovery time roughly in inverse proportion for a factor of 2 either way (halving the step: x1.44; doubling: x0.44), which supports 'the time to turn the trained layer-2 rows' as a large part of the extra time; at 4 times the step the extra stops falling (0.42), so something else (other layers, or a different bottleneck once layer 2 is fast) then limits. The floor (directions restored) hardly depends on m (200-350). Caveats: the extras are 2-14 grid steps of 50; one seed excluded; the law is my approximation (Adam's step is about lr per parameter only when gradients are consistent).
Addition to C-116 (written before these configurations run): initial scale CS = 2 and 4 (all initial weights and biases of the trainable layers multiplied by CS, as C-66 but at batch 256), W = 128. Prediction (4): conf3 at most 2 times alone at CS = 2 and 4; joint/alone ratio falls with CS. (The W = 128 output file was started before the CS column was added; its rows are CS = 1.)

### C-118 result (3 pairs x 16 networks; c118/swap.csv, c118_scores.txt; bit-4 time, steps; letters = origin of layer 1, layer 2, layer 3, output layer)
Pair 38 (fast) / 35 (slow): FFFF 900, SFFF 4500, FSFF 2600, FFSF 2300, FFFS 1400, SSSS 6000. Pair 23 / 5: FFFF 1200, SFFF 5050, FSFF 1650, FFSF 3300, FFFS 4300, SSSS 5800. Pair 28 / 37: FFFF 1200, SFFF 6450, FSFF 3800, FFSF 2700, FFFS 4150, SSSS 5750 (all 48 values in the CSV; none censored).
Main effects on log time of taking a component from the slow initialisation (fitted on all 16 combinations): layer 1 +0.76, +0.66, +0.56 (a factor 1.8-2.1); layer 2 +0.16, 0.00, -0.19; layer 3 +0.36, +0.16, -0.22; output layer -0.12, +0.16, +0.30. Share of the main-effect sum of squares carried by layer 1: 0.78, 0.89, 0.64. R^2 of the main-effect model: 0.63, 0.49, 0.44.
Predictions. (1) FFFF and SSSS reproduce C-83 within 50 steps: HIT (exactly, all six). (2) distributed, no component above 50 percent in at least 2 of 3 pairs: MISSED; layer 1 carries 64-89 percent in all three pairs. (3) main effects explain at least 60 percent in at least 2 of 3 pairs: MISSED (1 of 3; interactions are large, e.g. SSFS 2350 and SSSF 1700 are fast although layer 1 is from the slow init).
Reading. What makes an initialisation fast for joint bit 4 sits mostly in the first-layer weights: replacing only layer 1 of a fast init by the slow init's layer 1 slows it about 2 times on average, while the other layers matter little on average, with large interactions. Together with C-117 (fast and slow inits collapse equally) this says the initial first layer sets how quickly bit 4 can build its own features once the companions have taken over, not how fast the takeover is. Which property of layer 1 matters is tested next (C-119) by swapping only the weights from the input positions that bit 4 depends on (positions 0-4) versus the others (5-7) and the biases.

## C-119 (EXPLORATORY-WITH-PREDICTION; W3): which part of the first layer?
Design. The three C-118 pairs; the network is the fast initialisation except that layer-1 weight columns of input positions 0-4 (a0-a4, b0-b4: the inputs sum bit 4 depends on), columns of positions 5-7, and/or the layer-1 biases are taken from the slow initialisation (8 combinations per pair, letters in that order; FFF = fast init, SSS = C-118 SFFF). Training as C-118. Script c119_l1_parts.py, output c119/l1parts.csv.
Predictions (written before running). (1) FFF and SSS reproduce C-118 FFFF and SFFF exactly. (2) The columns of positions 0-4 carry most of the layer-1 effect: their main effect on log time is the largest of the three in at least 2 of 3 pairs. (Bit 4 depends only on positions 0-4; my guess is that the initial layer-1 weights on those inputs seed bit 4's own features.)

### C-116 result (5 seeds per configuration; c116_*/confine.csv, c116_scores.txt). Bit-4 times, medians (steps), and median layer-2 R^2 on S at step 250 in the joint runs:
| configuration | alone | conf3 (others only in layer 3) | joint | conf3/alone | joint/alone | joint layer-2 R^2 on S |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| W = 64 | 300 | 400 | >10,000 (4 of 5 censored) | 1.33 | >33 | 0.64 |
| W = 128 | 250 | 300 | 2,550 | 1.20 | 10.2 | 0.67 |
| W = 512 | 150 | 200 | 500 | 1.33 | 3.3 | 0.57 |
| frozen 1,024 + 3 x 128 | 200 | 200 | 6,600 | 1.00 | 33 | 0.87 |
| W = 128, initial scale 2 | 300 | 350 | 800 | 1.17 | 2.7 | 0.29 |
| W = 128, initial scale 4 | 650 | 850 | 1,200 | 1.31 | 1.8 | 0.08 |
Predictions. (1) conf3 at most 2 times alone in every configuration: HIT (1.00-1.33). (2) joint/alone larger at W = 64 than at W = 512, frozen joint slower than W = 128 joint: HIT. (3) Spearman over the 20 joint runs (W 64/128/512, frozen) between layer-2 R^2 on S and the time at least +0.5: MISSED (+0.40; with the 10 initial-scale runs +0.53). (4) initial scale: conf3 at most 2 times alone and joint/alone falling with the scale: HIT (1.17, 1.31; 10.2, 2.7, 1.8).
Reading. With ordinary Adam, letting the other eight outputs train only the last hidden layer (and their heads) removes the joint delay of bit 4 at every width (64 to 512), with the frozen random layer, and at every initial scale: conf3 is within 1.0-1.33 times the alone time everywhere, while the joint delay ranges from 1.8 to more than 33 times. So the width, initial-scale and frozen-layer dependence of the plateau (W4, W5) is entirely a dependence of what the other outputs do to the lower shared layers. Two of the three knobs act through how much the companions' code takes over layer 2 by step 250: initial scale (R^2 on S 0.67, 0.29, 0.08 at scale 1, 2, 4: larger initial weights are moved less, the 'lazy' direction) and the frozen layer (0.87: the takeover is stronger). Width does not change that share (0.57-0.67) but changes the cost of it a lot (W = 64 censored, W = 512 3.3 times), so width acts on how fast bit 4 recovers under the takeover, not on the takeover itself; C-115's step-size result is one candidate for that (wider rows turn faster under Adam), not tested across widths.

## C-116c (CONFIRMATORY; W5, learning rate)
Design. C-116 at W = 128, initial scale 1, Adam learning rate 3e-4 and 1e-2 (cap 10,000 and 3,000), arms alone, conf3, joint, seeds 0-4. Outputs c116_W128_lr3e-4/, c116_W128_lr1e-2/.
Predictions (written before running). (1) conf3 at most 2 times alone at both learning rates. (2) joint/alone larger at 3e-4 than at 1e-2 (replicates C-97: 20.8 against 3.3).

### C-119 result (3 pairs x 8; c119_a/, c119_b/l1parts.csv, c119_scores.txt; letters = origin of layer-1 columns of positions 0-4, columns of positions 5-7, layer-1 biases; rest of the network from the fast init)
Pair 38/35: FFF 900, FFS 1300, FSF 4500, FSS 2700, SFF 4950, SFS 2600, SSF 4950, SSS 4500. Pair 23/5: FFF 1200, FFS 5750, FSF 3200, FSS 3750, SFF 4950, SFS 4600, SSF 4800, SSS 5050. Pair 28/37: FFF 1200, FFS 2750, FSF 1600, FSS 4950, SFF 4300, SFS 3300, SSF 1950, SSS 6450.
Main effects on log time (slow vs fast): columns 0-4 +0.75, +0.47, +0.48; columns 5-7 +0.72, +0.15, +0.19; biases -0.22, +0.43, +0.72.
Predictions. (1) FFF and SSS equal C-118 FFFF and SFFF: HIT (all six identical). (2) columns of positions 0-4 have the largest main effect in at least 2 of 3 pairs: HIT only formally (2 of 3, by margins of 0.03 and 0.04; in the third pair the biases are larger).
Reading. No single part of the first layer carries the 'fast' property: replacing ANY one part (the weights from the inputs bit 4 depends on, the weights from the other inputs, or the biases) with the slow initialisation's makes the run at least twice as slow in 7 of the 9 single replacements (single replacements 1,300 to 5,750 against 900-1,200; checked in c120_verify.txt), mostly into the typical range of C-83 (median about 3,000). So a fast initialisation is a fragile coincidence of the whole first layer, not a describable property of a part of it [CORRECTED by C-130: 'fragile' was wrong; swapping a part replaces it completely, and smaller perturbations keep the property]; this fits the failure of every summary measure I tried (C-82 to C-85, C-117). W3 answer: the init effect lives in layer 1 (C-118), it does not act through the amount of collapse (C-117), and it is not reducible to one part of layer 1 (C-119); what exactly makes the lucky layers lucky remains unexplained.

### C-116c result (5 seeds; c116_W128_lr3e-4/, c116_W128_lr1e-2/)
lr 3e-4: alone 400 400 400 450 500 (median 400), conf3 550 450 450 500 650 (500), joint >10,000 >10,000 3,850 9,100 9,150 (9,150, censored runs counted as 10,000): conf3/alone 1.25, joint/alone about 23. lr 1e-2: alone 150 150 150 150 200 (150), conf3 150 150 200 150 150 (150), joint 500 600 350 550 600 (550): conf3/alone 1.0, joint/alone 3.7.
Predictions. (1) conf3 at most 2 times alone at both learning rates: HIT (1.25, 1.0). (2) joint/alone larger at 3e-4 than at 1e-2: HIT (about 23 against 3.7; C-97 had 20.8 and 3.3).
Reading. The learning-rate dependence of the joint delay (C-97) also disappears when the other outputs are confined to the last hidden layer. Together with C-116: width, initial scale, the frozen layer and the learning rate all change the plateau only through what the other outputs do to the lower shared layers.

# Summary of this phase (C-111 to C-119), written after all results above
W1 (why the joint-trained body is a worse start): joint training turns layers 2-3 within about 100 steps into a code of the companions' targets (for the sum bits a one-dimensional code of the numeric sum S: R^2 0.68 in layer 2 and 0.81 in layer 3 after 250 steps, C-113a) and keeps growing the layer-2 weights (C-111). Restoring layer 2's initial directions removes the whole recovery cost; restoring its norm removes half (C-112); the extra time scales about inversely with layer 2's step size for a factor 2 (C-115). The effect is generic to fast-learned companions, not specific to the sum code (C-113b).
W2 (the residual after the shared second moment): it is the other outputs taking over the lower shared layers. With separate moments, keeping them out of layers 1-2 gives 300 against 900 (C-114b); across six arms the time follows how much of layer 2 their code occupies at step 250 (Spearman +0.87). With ordinary Adam, confining them to the last hidden layer leaves bit 4 within 1.0-1.33 times its alone time (C-116).
W3 (fast initial weights): the effect sits in layer 1 (64-89 percent of the main-effect variance, C-118), does not act through the amount of collapse (C-117), and is a fragile property of the whole first layer (C-119). Not explained further.
W4 (frozen random layer): with it, the companions' code takes over the trainable layer 2 more completely (R^2 on S 0.87 against 0.67) and the delay is 33 times the alone time; confined to the top layer, the other outputs no longer slow bit 4 (C-116).
W5 (width, initial scale, learning rate): every one of them changes the plateau only through the other outputs' use of the lower layers (conf3 within 1.0-1.33 of alone at all settings, C-116, C-116c). Initial scale acts by reducing the takeover (R^2 0.67, 0.29, 0.08 at scale 1, 2, 4); width does not change the takeover share but changes how costly it is; the mechanism of the width effect on that cost is not identified.


## C-120 (check): every number quoted in ANSWERS.md for C-111 to C-119 was recomputed from the raw CSVs by c120_verify_answers.py (output c120_verify.txt). One correction followed: the C-116c joint median at lr 3e-4 is 9,150, not 9,100 (corrected above).

# Phase "answer the four open items" (26 Oct 2026, from 16:05)
Open items: (O1) a formula that predicts the delay; (O2) what makes a lucky first layer lucky; (O3) how width makes the takeover cheaper; (O4) why keeping the other outputs out of layer 3 slows bit 4 (C-114: 5,850 against 900).

## C-121 (CONFIRMATORY; O4): Adam-amplified weak gradients, or a hijacked pathway?
Two candidate explanations (mine). (a) Hijacked pathway: when the other outputs cannot change layer 3, they can only reach their heads by reshaping layers 1-2 so that bit 4's layer-3 units carry their code; their signal then runs through bit 4's own units. (b) Amplified weak gradients: with separate moments each output gets a full Adam step (about lr per parameter) whatever its gradient size; when the other outputs cannot adapt layer 3, their gradients into layers 1-2 are weaker and less consistent from batch to batch, yet still produce full-size steps, so layers 1-2 random-walk and bit 4's features are disturbed.
Design. n = 8, 3 x 128, seeds 0-4, cap 6,000, bit-4 time as before. MODE sep (C-114 optimiser): arms 'none' and 'L3'. MODE plain (ordinary Adam, shared moments): 'joint' and 'blockL3' (the other outputs' gradient is not applied to layer-3 parameters but still flows through layer 3 into layers 1-2).
