| others' minibatch noise around their frozen mean (centered noise) | 2,600 2,250 350 2,150 1,650 |
| others' frozen mean gradient (frozen mean) | >3,500 in all five |
| others' gradient at the frozen parameters (frozen other) | >3,500 in all five |
| others' live gradient (joint) | >3,500, 3,450, 500, 1,950, 2,050 |
Predictions. (1) block within 100 steps of C-104 in at least 4 of 5: HIT (5 of 5 identical). (2) frozen mean slower than centered noise in at least 4 of 5, and censored or at least 2 times in at least 3 of 5: HIT (5 of 5 censored). (3) centered noise within 1.5 times of block in at least 3 of 5: HIT (4 of 5).
Reading. The returned note's claim reproduces in my own code, on five seeds: the other outputs' coherent MEAN push on the shared layers, held fixed at its step-500 value, keeps bit 4 from being learned at all within 3,500 steps, while their minibatch noise alone barely matters. This is the takeover of C-111 to C-116 seen as a force: it is a steady, coherent pull toward the other outputs' code, not noise. The frozen mean is even worse than the live gradient, presumably because the live push weakens as the other outputs are learned and their gradients shrink (not tested).

### C-154 result (5 seeds per alpha; c154/traj_*.npz, c154_scores.txt)
| alpha (share of independent init, by amplitude) | T median | onset median | growth median |
|---|---|---|---|
| 0 (pure duplicate, C-131) | 1,250 | 475 | 625 |
| 0.1 | 650 | 150 | 500 |
| 0.3 | 550 | 75 | 475 |
| 0.5 | 600 | 100 | 500 |
| 0.7 | 550 | 75 | 500 |
| independent width 512 (C-131, another draw) | 500 | 100 | 400 |
Predictions. (1) onset non-increasing in alpha: MISSED by one grid step (75 at 0.3, 100 at 0.5; the curve is flat from 0.3 on). (2) onset falls by a larger factor than growth between alpha 0 and 0.7: HIT (6.3 against 1.25).
Reading. A small admixture of independent weights (alpha = 0.1, i.e. 1 percent of the variance) already brings the onset from 475 to 150 and halves the time, and by alpha = 0.3 the duplicated net behaves like an independent width-512 net. Under exact duplication the clones receive identical gradients and stay identical forever, so the duplicated net IS a width-128 net (with a larger effective learning rate, C-122); a little noise lets the 512 units differentiate. So the 'new units' half of the width effect is the number of DISTINCT units, and it acts on how soon bit 4's signal appears, not on how fast it then grows.

### C-152 result (1,000 new initialisations; c152_deepsets.py -> c152_scores.txt)
Labelled initialisations: training 593 (217 escaped), validation 81, test 114. The DeepSets model's best validation AUC was 0.534 (at epoch 3, i.e. nothing generalisable was learned; training AUC 0.653). Test AUC 0.523; Spearman(score, T) on s = 0-59: -0.12.
Predictions. (1) test AUC at least 0.65: MISSED. (2) Spearman at most -0.3: MISSED.
Reading. With 1,000 initialisations and a flexible order-blind model of the first layer (each unit's weights, bias, activity and outgoing-weight norm, with mean and max pooling), there is still no usable predictor of which initialisations escape early. Together with C-139, C-142, C-144 to C-147 this is strong evidence that the escape is not decided by a simple or moderately complex static property of the first layer's units taken one by one; it is decided by the early training dynamics of the whole network (C-123b to C-123c, C-130, C-133).

## C-155 (CONFIRMATORY; R1 and R2 from the dynamics side): does bit 4 escape only when the other outputs' coherent push has weakened?
Idea source: C-153 (the other outputs' frozen mean push keeps bit 4 stuck indefinitely; their live push lets it through, presumably because it weakens as they are learned). If so, the timing of the escape should follow the decay of that push: P(t) = norm of the other outputs' mean gradient on the shared layers (over 8,192 fixed training pairs).
Design. (a) The 40 C-83 initialisations (C-83 recipe), and (b) seeds 0-4 of C-116 settings lr 3e-4, lr 1e-2, width 64, width 512 (C-116 recipe); every 25 steps P(t) and the bit-4 logit-target correlation; run until bit 4 is confirmed (cap 10,000). t_half = first step after the peak of P at which P is below half its peak; t_on as in C-125. Script c155_push.py, outputs c155_inits/, c155_settings/.
Predictions (written before running). (1) Over the 40 initialisations, Spearman(t_half, t_on) at least +0.5. (2) In at least 70 percent of all runs, t_on >= t_half - 100 (bit 4's signal appears only after the push has roughly halved). (3) Across the four settings plus width 128 (from (a)), the setting medians of t_half and t_on have Spearman at least +0.7.

### C-155 result (40 initialisations + 4 settings x 5 seeds; c155_inits/, c155_settings/, c155_scores.txt)
All 40 initialisations reproduce their C-83 times. The other outputs' push P(t) peaks early (median at step 450) and falls below half its peak very soon after (median t_half 475, range 425-5,300). Spearman(t_half, t_on) over the 40 initialisations +0.18; Spearman(t_half, T) +0.29. Runs with t_on >= t_half - 100: 25 of 60 (42 percent). Setting medians (t_half / t_on / T): lr 3e-4 3,800 / 2,425 / 9,150; lr 1e-2 175 / 200 / 550; width 64 6,925 / 1,650 / 10,000; width 512 325 / 100 / 500; width 128 lr 1e-3 475 / 600 / 2,850.
Predictions. (1) Spearman(t_half, t_on) over initialisations at least +0.5: MISSED (+0.18). (2) at least 70 percent of runs with the onset after the push has halved: MISSED (42 percent). (3) Spearman of setting medians at least +0.7: HIT (+0.80), but this mostly reflects that everything is faster or slower together in a setting.
Reading. The decay of the other outputs' push on the shared layers does not time bit 4's escape within a setting: the push (its norm) peaks and halves within the first few hundred steps in most runs, while bit 4's onset varies from 50 to 1,750 independently of it. The frozen-mean result of C-153 shows that a coherent push CAN hold bit 4 back indefinitely, but the timing of the escape in ordinary training is not set by when that push weakens (measured by its norm). R1 and R2 are not advanced by this measure.

## C-156 (CONFIRMATORY; is the takeover account specific to Adam?)
Idea source: C-60 (with momentum SGD the joint/alone ratio is only about 1.4, against about 10 for Adam), C-116 (with Adam the delay is the takeover of the lower layers, made costly by Adam's per-parameter steps), C-73 (shared second moment).
Design. C-116 recipe (n = 8, 3 x 128, batch 256, seeds 0-4, cap 10,000) with momentum SGD (lr 0.01, momentum 0.9, as C-60); arms alone, conf3, joint; layer-2 R^2 on S at step 250 recorded. c116_confine.py with OPT=sgd, output c156_sgd/.
Predictions (written before running). (1) joint/alone median ratio at most 2 (replicates C-60). (2) The takeover still happens under SGD: joint layer-2 R^2 on S at step 250 at least 0.5. If (2) holds while (1) holds, the takeover is not costly by itself; the cost comes from how Adam turns the other outputs' coherent push into steps.

### C-156 result (momentum SGD, 5 seeds; c156_sgd/confine.csv)
alone 1,800 2,100 1,700 2,400 2,250 (median 2,100); conf3 1,850 2,000 1,750 2,400 2,200 (2,000); joint 2,850 2,850 2,850 3,500 3,500 (2,850). Joint layer-2 R^2 on S at step 250: 0.65-0.70 [corrected by C-159 from 0.66-0.70] (layer 3: 0.89-0.90).
Predictions. (1) joint/alone at most 2: HIT (1.36). (2) takeover still happens under SGD (R^2 on S at least 0.5): HIT (0.65-0.70, as strong as under Adam, 0.67).
Reading. Under momentum SGD the other outputs take over the lower layers just as much as under Adam, yet bit 4 is delayed only 1.36 times. So the takeover is costly only together with Adam: Adam's per-parameter normalisation divides bit 4's small gradient by the other outputs' large second moment (C-73, C-107/C-108) and lets their coherent push set the update direction, while plain SGD adds bit 4's gradient on top of theirs. This ties the two halves of the mechanism together: the takeover provides the coherent push, Adam converts it into a delay. (Under SGD bit 4 is slow even alone, 2,100 steps, at this one learning rate.)

## C-157 (CONFIRMATORY; Q2 leftover: is the slope left after C-08 intrinsic, or an artefact of stratifying by the pair's longest run?)
Idea source: my own open item in C-08 ("whether that remaining slope is intrinsic difficulty or an artefact of how I stratified, by the pair's longest run M, not by each bit's own dependency"). Design information computed before any outcome was read (c157_counts.py, seed 30's training sets rebuilt with the same random calls as adder_learn4.py): in the C-08 stratified set each bit's OWN dependency-length counts are U-shaped, not flat. Example, bit 8: d_8 = 0..8 counts 1,570, 613, 234, 131, 112, 115, 153, 177, 171; bit 6: 1,212, 496, 265, 233, 273, 304, 493. So the middle lengths are the rarest in the stratified sets, and the longest length is not.
Part A (observational, the existing C-08 networks c08/final_*_stratified.npz, held-out errors per bit and own d). For bits 5-8 in the stratified arm, count held-out errors per (bit, own d) pooled over seeds 30-34, and the training count of each cell (rebuilt per seed).
Predictions A (written before reading the per-cell errors). (A1) If the leftover slope were only frequency, error would be highest at the rarest (middle) lengths. I predict instead that for each of bits 6, 7, 8 the error at the longest length (d = i) is higher than the error at the rarest-count length, pooled over seeds; hit if true for at least 2 of the 3 bits. (A2) In a per-seed logistic regression of error on own d with bit effects (bits 5-8) and the log training count of the (bit, d) cell as an extra covariate, the d coefficient stays positive in at least 4 of 5 seeds.
Part B (causal). New arm "own6": training set of the same size (3,276), drawn from the same pool and with the same test set as C-08, but with equal quotas per bit 6's own dependency length d_6 = 0..6 (468 each; pool has at least 697 of each), everything else as adder_learn4.py (seeds 30-34, 10,000 steps, same init and batch seeds). Script c157_own6.py, output c157/.
Predictions B (written before running). (B1) Bit 6 held-out error still rises with d_6 in the own6 arm: the pooled (5 seeds) logistic coefficient of d_6 on bit-6 error is positive with a 95 percent interval (bootstrap over seeds and examples) excluding 0. (B2) That coefficient is at least half of the M-stratified arm's bit-6 coefficient (pooled). If both hold, the leftover slope is intrinsic to the long dependencies (at equal exposure), not an artefact of the M stratification; if the own6 coefficient is near 0, it was frequency. Note (from R-5): "intrinsic" here cannot separate a sequential chain from a weighted-sum threshold that fails near the carry boundary; long chains put the sum close to the boundary.

### C-157 part A result (existing C-08 networks; c157_partA.py, c157/partA.txt)
Regression check: my refit reproduces the C-08 coefficients exactly (random 0.87, 0.82, 0.74, 0.67, 0.86; stratified 0.30, 0.36, 0.37, 0.21, 0.21).
Stratified arm, pooled over seeds 30-34, held-out error rate by own dependency length (training count summed over seeds in brackets):
- bit 5, d = 0..5: 0.0042 (5,611), 0.0063 (2,480), 0.0206 (1,607), 0.0314 (1,679), 0.0152 (1,868), 0.0000 (3,135).
- bit 6, d = 0..6: 0.0054 (6,038), 0.0103 (2,387), 0.0225 (1,347), 0.0307 (1,191), 0.0299 (1,371), 0.0171 (1,568), 0.0000 (2,478).
- bit 7, d = 0..7: 0.0009, 0.0004, 0.0036, 0.0051 (892), 0.0041 (877, rarest), 0.0032, 0.0000, 0.0000 (1,734).
- bit 8: at most 10 errors per cell (too few to read), zero at d = 7 and 8.
Predictions. (A1) error at the longest length above the error at the rarest-count length for at least 2 of bits 6-8: MISSED (0 of 3; the longest length has zero held-out errors for every bit, 1,615 held-out examples for bit 6). (A2) d coefficient still positive with the log training count of the cell added, in at least 4 of 5 seeds: MISSED (3 of 5: 0.110, 0.181, 0.178, -0.144, -0.043; the log-count coefficient is -0.79 to -1.02 in every seed).
Reading. In the C-08 stratified networks the errors are hump-shaped in each bit's own dependency length and peak where that bit's own training count is lowest (the middle lengths), not at the longest lengths, which are plentiful in the stratified set and have no errors at all. Adding the cell's training count removes most of the d effect (coefficient from 0.21-0.37 to -0.14 to 0.18). This is observational (count and d are not set independently), so Part B tests it by training with equal counts per own length.

## C-158 (CONFIRMATORY; robustness of C-156: is the small SGD cost of the takeover specific to lr 0.01?)
Idea source: C-60 and C-156 used one SGD learning rate (0.01); the README lists this as a limit.
Design. c116_confine.py with OPT=sgd (momentum 0.9) at lr 0.03 and lr 0.003; arms alone and joint; seeds 0-4; cap 10,000 at lr 0.03 and 20,000 at lr 0.003; outputs c158_lr0.03/, c158_lr0.003/. Layer-2 R^2 on S at step 250 recorded as in C-156.
Predictions (written before running). (1) joint/alone median ratio at most 2 at both learning rates (C-156: 1.36 at 0.01; Adam: about 10 at 1e-3). (2) joint layer-2 R^2 on S at step 250 at least 0.5 at lr 0.03 (the takeover happens; at lr 0.003 it may not have happened by step 250, so no prediction there).

### C-157 part B result (own6 arm, seeds 30-34; c157/summary.csv, c157/final_*_own6.npz; c157_partB.py -> c157/partB.txt)
The own6 training sets were rebuilt in the scoring script and match the trained ones (saved M of the training pairs identical; exactly 468 pairs per d_6 = 0..6 per seed). Mean accuracy over all bits 0.9916-0.9954 (C-08: random 0.9925-0.9983, stratified 0.9958-0.9987).
Bit-6 held-out error rate by d_6 = 0..6, pooled over 5 seeds (training count summed over seeds):
- random: 0.0009 (8,159), 0.0026 (4,189), 0.0062 (2,030), 0.0157 (978), 0.0411 (522), 0.0784 (249), 0.0941 (253); coefficient of d_6 +0.79 (95 percent bootstrap +0.76 to +0.82).
- M-stratified (C-08): 0.0054 (6,038), 0.0103, 0.0225, 0.0307 (1,191), 0.0299, 0.0171, 0.0000 (2,478); coefficient +0.29 (+0.26 to +0.31).
- own6 (equal counts, 2,340 per length): 0.0327, 0.0118, 0.0054, 0.0014, 0, 0, 0; coefficient -1.01 (-1.09 to -0.93).
Predictions. (B1) own6 coefficient positive with interval excluding 0: MISSED (it is strongly negative). (B2) at least half of the stratified coefficient: MISSED (ratio -3.5).
Reading. With the same number of training pairs at every length of bit 6's own dependency, the long dependencies are not harder: they have NO held-out errors at d_6 >= 4, and the errors now sit on the short ones (3.3 percent at d_6 = 0). In all three training schemes the bit-6 error follows how many training pairs the length has (random: few long ones, errors rise with d; stratified: fewest in the middle, errors peak there; own6: equal counts, errors fall with d), not the length itself. So the slope left after C-08 was an artefact of stratifying by the pair's longest run, and the C-07 slope is a training-frequency effect. Why do equal counts favour long lengths? A cell of length d contains a fraction 2^-(d+1) of all pairs (2^-6 for the longest), so 468 pairs cover a long-dependency cell far more densely than the d_6 = 0 cell (about 46 percent of the 1,024 longest-chain pairs against about 1.4 percent of the 32,768 pairs with d_6 = 0). In this problem a long chain and a small input region are the same thing, so 'per example' and 'per region' difficulty cannot be separated by construction, except at d_6 = 5 against 6 (equal cell sizes): in the random arm these have equal training counts (249, 253) and similar error (0.078 against 0.094, a difference of about 1.6 standard errors). My prediction (an intrinsic slope) was wrong. Q2 answer updated: what makes a carry case hard for these networks is how sparsely the training data cover it, not how long the dependency is. Caveats: one bit (6) balanced; 5 seeds; fraction 0.05; the R-5 point (error versus distance from the carry threshold) is not addressed by this arm.

### C-158 result (momentum SGD, 5 seeds per learning rate; c158_lr0.03/, c158_lr0.003/confine.csv, c158_scores.txt)
| lr | alone, seeds 0-4 (median) | joint (median) | joint/alone | joint layer-2 R^2 on S at step 250 |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| 0.03 | 850 800 800 1,050 1,050 (850) | 1,450 1,300 1,350 1,300 1,700 (1,350) | 1.59 | 0.66-0.86 |
| 0.01 (C-156) | (2,100) | (2,850) | 1.36 | 0.65-0.70 |
| 0.003 | 5,700 6,300 5,350 7,300 7,150 (6,300) | 9,000 9,100 7,450 8,800 10,400 (9,000) | 1.43 | 0.07-0.26 |
Predictions. (1) joint/alone at most 2 at both learning rates: HIT (1.59 and 1.43). (2) layer-2 R^2 on S at least 0.5 at lr 0.03: HIT (0.66-0.86).
Reading. Over a tenfold range of SGD learning rates the joint delay of bit 4 stays small (1.4-1.6 times), against about 10 times with Adam at its default rate. At lr 0.03 the takeover of layer 2 by the sum code is as strong as with Adam (R^2 0.66-0.86 at step 250), so under SGD the takeover is cheap at every rate tried. At lr 0.003 the takeover is slower (R^2 0.07-0.26 at step 250, not yet complete), which does not change the conclusion. The README limit 'one SGD learning rate' is removed; still momentum 0.9 only and n = 8.

## C-161 (EXPLORATORY-WITH-PREDICTION, saved data only; R2): when does an initialisation's luck become visible?
Idea source: R2 is open because nothing static predicts escape (C-139, C-142, C-147, C-152); the head start J predicts strong cases at step 125 (C-123c, C-134). A predictability horizon describes when the outcome is decided, even without a static description.
Data: the 1,000 C-139/C-152 initialisations (c139/init_s*.npz), held-out bit-4 logit-target correlation J(t) every 25 steps to step 500. Label as C-152: escaped if J(500) >= 0.2, not escaped if J(500) < 0.05, others left out. For each t = 25..475, AUC of J(t) for the label (all labelled inits); and on the 60 inits with known time T, Spearman(J(t), T). Script c161_horizon.py, output c161_horizon.txt.
Predictions (written before computing). (1) AUC at t = 50 at most 0.65 (luck is not yet visible). (2) AUC at t = 250 at least 0.85. (3) The first t with AUC at least 0.8 lies between 100 and 300.

### C-161 result (c161_horizon.py -> c161_horizon.txt; 1,000 initialisations, 840 labelled, 306 escaped; 60 with known T)
AUC of J(t) for escape by step 500: t = 25: 0.59; 50: 0.69; 75: 0.79; 100: 0.85; 125: 0.91; 150: 0.94; 200: 0.98; 250: 0.99. Spearman(J(t), T) on the 60 initialisations with known bit-4 time: t = 25: +0.01; 50: -0.33; 75: -0.41; 100: -0.48; 125: -0.60; 200: -0.69; 300: -0.76; 375 onward about -0.8.
Predictions. (1) AUC at t = 50 at most 0.65: MISSED (0.69; the outcome is already partly visible at step 50). (2) AUC at 250 at least 0.85: HIT (0.99). (3) first t with AUC at least 0.8 between 100 and 300: HIT (100).
Caveat: the escape label is defined from J(500), so the AUC necessarily approaches 1 as t approaches 500; the Spearman with the actual learning time T is the cleaner measure (independent outcome, but only 60 initialisations).
Reading (R2). An initialisation's luck is invisible in its static weights (AUC 0.52 with a learned model, C-152; first-step alignment, C-147) and at step 25 (AUC 0.59, Spearman with T +0.01), but it becomes visible within the first 50-125 Adam steps (AUC 0.69-0.91; Spearman with T -0.33 to -0.60) and is mostly settled by step 250 (Spearman about -0.7, against a typical learning time of 3,000 steps). So the outcome is decided by a short early phase of training that amplifies small differences, consistent with the smoothness of C-130 and the failure of every static description. This does not supply the compact description R2 asked for; it says where in time to look for it.

## C-165 (CONFIRMATORY; generality of the takeover mechanism across depth)

Design. c165_depth.py (c116_confine.py with DEPTH hidden layers of width 128; 'conf3' = the other outputs' gradient reaches only the last hidden layer and the heads); Adam 1e-3, batch 256, n = 8, seeds 0-4, cap 10,000; DEPTH = 2 and 4; arms alone, conf3, joint. Reproduction check: DEPTH = 3, seed 0 must give the C-116 times (alone 200, conf3 300, joint 4,600). The CSV columns L2_R2S250 / L3_R2S250 hold the second-to-last / last hidden layer. ### C-162 result (200 full runs, s = 100-299; c162_a/, c162_b/, c162_score.py -> c162_scores.txt)
Reproduction: J(t) for t <= 500 equals the saved C-139 values in all 200 runs (maximum difference 0). No run censored; T median 2,625 (1,100-7,000).
Spearman(J(t), T): t = 25: -0.16; 50: -0.22; 75: -0.45; 100: -0.60; 125: -0.64; 150: -0.67; 200: -0.78; 250: -0.81; 300: -0.82; 400: -0.85; 500: -0.89.
Onset law, frozen since C-126 (T_hat = 1,283 + 2.53 t_on), all 200 runs: median |ln(T_hat/T)| 0.11 (maximum 0.57), against 0.32 for the constant 2,900; Spearman(T_hat, T) 0.90.
Predictions. (1) |Spearman| at t = 25 at most 0.15: MISSED narrowly (-0.16; a weak signal is already present at step 25). (2) at t = 125 at most -0.4: HIT (-0.64). (3) at t = 250 at most -0.55: HIT (-0.81). (4) onset law median |ln error| at most 0.25: HIT (0.11). (5) Spearman at least 0.8: HIT (0.90).
Reading. On 200 new initialisations the picture of C-161 holds and is sharper: the head start carries a weak signal about the final learning time at step 25 (-0.16), a moderate one by step 75-100 (-0.45 to -0.60), and a strong one by step 200-250 (-0.78 to -0.81), against a median learning time of 2,625 steps. The onset law, fitted on 40 runs in C-126 and never refitted, predicts the learning time of 200 new runs with a median error of about 11 percent. So, per run: the time is set by when bit 4's escape starts (its onset), and the onset is decided in the first few hundred steps; neither is readable from the initial weights.

### C-164 result (bit 5, training fraction 0.10, seeds 30-34; c164/, c164_score.py -> c164_scores.txt)
Training sets rebuilt in the scoring script match the trained ones (checked for seed 30, both arms). own5 has 1,092 pairs per length plus one random pair (6,553 = 6 x 1,092 + 1).
Bit-5 held-out error by d_5 = 0..5 (pooled): random 0.0005, 0.0007, 0.0007, 0.0082, 0.0260, 0.0151 (training counts 16,535 ... 1,018, 991); own5 0.0013, 0.0006, 0, 0, 0, 0. Coefficients: random +0.81 (+0.75 to +0.88), own5 -1.19 (-1.45 to -0.95).
Predictions. (1) own5 coefficient negative: HIT. (2) random coefficient positive: HIT. (3) random, d_5 = 4 against 5 within 2 standard errors: MISSED (0.0260 against 0.0151, -3.0 standard errors: the longest length is EASIER at equal count and region size).
Reading, and a correction to C-160 and C-163. The C-157/C-160 result replicates at twice the training data: with equal counts per own length, bit 5 makes errors only at lengths 0-1. But the (3) miss made me re-examine the 'equal region size' contrast of C-160/C-163, and it is not what I said it was. For bit i, length i-1 means positions 1..i-1 propagate and position 0 is a generate or kill, so the carry equals a_0 AND b_0; length i means all positions 0..i-1 propagate, so the carry is always 0. Both cases depend on all i lower positions; they differ in whether the chain ends in a carry source, not in the number of steps. So the contrast does NOT test 'one more step of dependency', and the C-160/C-163 sentences claiming that are withdrawn (marked below). What stands: at equal training counts per length, longer chains have fewer or no errors (C-157, C-160, C-164); in this problem a longer chain is also a smaller input region, so 'per example' and 'per region' difficulty cannot be separated, and there is no clean within-problem test of one extra step.

### C-165 result (5 seeds per depth; c165_d2/, c165_d4/, c165_d3check/, c165_scores.txt)
Reproduction: DEPTH = 3, seed 0 gives exactly the C-116 values (alone 200, conf3 300, joint 4,600; same R^2).
| depth | alone (median) | others only in the last hidden layer (median) | joint (median) | conf/alone | joint/alone | joint R^2 on S at 250 (second-to-last / last hidden layer) |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| 2 | 300 300 350 400 500 (350) | 600 600 500 650 650 (600) | 7,900 2,450 1,800 4,400 5,000 (4,400) | 1.71 | 12.6 | 0.16-0.18 / 0.50-0.56 |
| 3 (C-116) | (250) | (300) | (2,550) | 1.2 | 10.2 | 0.60-0.72 / 0.79-0.83 |
| 4 | 300 150 150 200 250 (200) | 200 200 150 250 250 (200) | 4,650 1,750 1,800 2,450 2,900 (2,450) | 1.00 | 12.3 | 0.84-0.87 / 0.79-0.83 |
Predictions. (1) conf at most 1.5 times alone: HIT at depth 4 (1.00), MISSED at depth 2 (1.71). (2) joint at least 3 times alone: HIT at both (12.6, 12.3).
Reading. The joint delay of bit 4 is about the same size at depths 2, 3 and 4 (10-13 times its alone time), and keeping the other outputs out of all hidden layers but the last removes it completely at depth 4 and almost completely at depth 2 (4,400 down to 600 against 350 alone; about 80 percent of the log delay). At depth 2 the last hidden layer is the only one left to share, it is itself strongly taken over in joint training (R^2 on S 0.50-0.56), and bit 4 must read its features from that layer, so a residual is expected. The takeover mechanism therefore holds across depth, with the size of the residual depending on how much of the network the others still share.

## C-166 (EXPLORATORY-WITH-PREDICTION; does the joint delay depend on Adam's second-moment memory beta2?)
Idea source: C-107/C-108 (the extra recovery time after joint training is carried by Adam's stored second moment and grows with its size) and C-73 (shared second moment). beta2 sets how long that moment remembers (time constant about 1/(1 - beta2): 1,000 steps at 0.999, 100 at 0.99, 10 at 0.9).
Design. c165_depth.py, depth 3, n = 8, Adam lr 1e-3 with beta2 = 0.99 and 0.9 (beta1 0.9), arms alone and joint, seeds 0-4, cap 10,000; outputs c166_b0.99/, c166_b0.9/. Reference beta2 = 0.999: C-116 (alone 250, joint 2,550, ratio 10.2).
Predictions (written before running). (1) joint/alone ratio at beta2 = 0.9 at most 0.7 times the ratio at 0.999 (i.e. at most 7.1): a shorter memory of the other outputs' large gradients removes part of the delay. (2) It stays at least 2 (the instantaneous dilution remains). No prediction for 0.99 (reported).

### C-166 result (5 seeds per beta2; c166_b0.9/, c166_b0.99/)
| beta2 | alone (median) | joint (median) | joint/alone |
|---|---|---|---|
| 0.9 | 300 300 300 350 350 (300) | 4,050 5,000 1,800 3,450 3,450 (3,450) | 11.5 |
| 0.99 | 200 200 250 250 350 (250) | 4,450 3,850 1,300 2,850 3,100 (3,100) | 12.4 |
| 0.999 (C-116) | (250) | (2,550) | 10.2 |
Joint R^2 on S at step 250: layer 2 0.56-0.67, layer 3 0.79-0.83 (beta2 0.9); 0.60-0.71 / 0.785-0.83 (0.99).
Predictions. (1) ratio at beta2 = 0.9 at most 7.1: MISSED (11.5). (2) at least 2: HIT.
Reading. The joint delay does not depend on how long Adam remembers past gradient sizes: shortening the second-moment memory from about 1,000 to about 10 steps leaves the ratio at 10-12. During joint training the other outputs' large gradients are present at every step, so the second moment reflects them whatever its memory; what matters is the instantaneous normalisation by their size (C-73, C-156), not stored history. This is consistent with C-107 (where stored history mattered only after the other outputs were switched off) and corrects the expectation I wrote down. Caveat: beta1 fixed at 0.9; lr 1e-3 only.

## C-167 (CONFIRMATORY; is the joint delay specific to Adam, or to per-parameter normalisation in general?)
Idea source: C-60/C-156/C-158 (momentum SGD: 1.4-1.6 times), C-73 and C-166 (the instantaneous per-parameter normalisation by the other outputs' gradient size is what matters). If that is right, other optimisers that divide each parameter's step by a running gradient size should also show a large delay.
Design. c165_depth.py, depth 3, n = 8, arms alone and joint, seeds 0-4, cap 10,000: RMSprop (lr 1e-3, PyTorch defaults: alpha 0.99, no momentum) and Adagrad (lr 1e-2, PyTorch defaults; its normaliser is the sum of all past squared gradients). Outputs c167_rmsprop/, c167_adagrad/.
Predictions (written before running). (1) RMSprop joint/alone median ratio at least 5. (2) Adagrad joint/alone at least 5. If either is near the SGD value (below 2), the delay is tied to Adam's particular combination rather than to normalisation as such.

### C-167 result (5 seeds per optimiser; c167_rmsprop/, c167_adagrad/)
| optimiser | alone (median) | joint (median) | joint/alone |
|---|---|---|---|
| RMSprop lr 1e-3 | 250 250 400 200 500 (250) | 2,200 3,400 1,850 3,350 4,600 (3,350) | 13.4 |
| Adagrad lr 1e-2 | 200 200 250 200 300 (200) | >10,000; >10,000; 4,150; 4,850; >10,000 (>10,000) | at least 50 |
| Adam lr 1e-3 (C-116) | (250) | (2,550) | 10.2 |
| momentum SGD (C-156, C-158) | | | 1.4-1.6 |
Joint layer-2 / layer-3 R^2 on S at step 250: RMSprop 0.59-0.64 / 0.64-0.68; Adagrad 0.60-0.64 / 0.656-0.695.
Predictions. (1) RMSprop ratio at least 5: HIT (13.4). (2) Adagrad ratio at least 5: HIT (at least 50; three of five runs not learned by 10,000).
Reading. The large joint delay is a property of per-parameter normalised optimisers in general, not of Adam's particular recipe: RMSprop (no momentum) gives 13 times and Adagrad, whose normaliser accumulates every past squared gradient (so the other outputs' large early gradients are never forgotten), at least 50 times, against 1.4-1.6 times for momentum SGD at three learning rates. With C-166 (Adam's memory length does not matter) this fits one account: the per-parameter step of a shared weight is divided by the size of everyone's gradient on it, which the other outputs dominate. Adagrad's extreme case also shows that a normaliser that never forgets does hurt (unlike C-166, where even Adam's 1,000-step memory is short compared with the run). Caveat: one learning rate per optimiser (PyTorch-typical values), not tuned.

## C-168 (check): numbers for C-162 to C-167 recomputed from the raw outputs (c168_verify.py -> c168_verify.txt)
c164_score.py reproduces its score file; c162_score.py reproduces its file except for the reproduction line I appended by hand (the J comparison with C-139). All per-seed times, medians, ratios and R^2 ranges quoted for C-165 to C-167 match the recomputed values. No corrections.

## C-169 (CONFIRMATORY; does a normalisation layer remove the joint delay?)
Why: a question an outsider would ask ("would LayerNorm fix it?"). The account so far puts the cause in the optimiser's per-parameter normalisation together with the other outputs' takeover of the shared layers; a LayerNorm in the network changes the scale of the hidden features but not the optimiser.
Design. c165_depth.py with LN=1 (Linear -> LayerNorm -> ReLU in each of the 3 hidden layers), Adam 1e-3, n = 8, seeds 0-4, arms alone, conf3, joint, cap 10,000; output c169_ln/.
Predictions (written before running). (1) joint/alone at least 5 (the delay persists). (2) conf3 at most 1.5 times alone (the confinement still removes it).

### C-169 result (LayerNorm before each ReLU, 5 seeds; c169_ln/confine.csv)
alone 250 200 250 250 300 (median 250); conf3 300 200 300 250 300 (300); joint 350 450 350 400 450 (400). joint/alone 1.6; conf3/alone 1.2. Joint R^2 on S at step 250: layer 2 0.13-0.18, layer 3 0.08-0.11 (without LayerNorm: 0.60-0.72 and 0.79-0.83).
Predictions. (1) joint/alone at least 5: MISSED (1.6). (2) conf3 at most 1.5 times alone: HIT (1.2).
Reading. A LayerNorm in each hidden layer almost removes the joint delay (400 against 250 steps alone; 2,550 without LayerNorm), and the takeover of the shared layers by the numeric-sum code largely disappears (R^2 on S 0.13-0.18 instead of 0.6-0.7). So with LayerNorm the other outputs no longer turn the shared layers into a one-dimensional code of the sum S (C-113a), and without that takeover there is little for Adam to turn into a delay. Why LayerNorm prevents the takeover is not tested here. Two candidate routes: (a) if part of the S-code is carried by how large each example's hidden vector is (a larger S giving a larger activation norm), LayerNorm removes that part directly; (b) LayerNorm makes each layer insensitive to a common rescaling of its weights, which changes the norm-growth part of the cost found in C-112. My prediction (that the delay lives only in the optimiser and would survive a normalisation layer) was wrong. Not yet tested: which part of LayerNorm matters (centring, scaling, the learned gain), where it must sit, and whether it holds at n = 12 (C-170).

## C-170 (CONFIRMATORY, follows C-169): which part of LayerNorm removes the delay, does the hidden-vector norm code the sum, and does it hold at n = 12?
Disclosure: before writing these predictions I ran a 300-step smoke test of the new metric on seed 0 without normalisation: at step 250 the correlation between the norm of each example's layer-2 vector and S = a + b was 0.75.
Design. (a) n = 8, depth 3, Adam 1e-3, seeds 0-4, arms alone and joint, cap 10,000: NORM = rms (divide each hidden vector by its root mean square; no centring, no gain) and NORM = center (subtract its mean only); outputs c170_rms/, c170_center/. (b) The same metric (correlation of the layer-2 vector norm with S at step 250) for joint runs without normalisation and with LayerNorm (NORM = none / ln, joint arm only; c170_none/, c170_ln/). (c) n = 12 (C-149 data recipe, 20,000-step cap) with LayerNorm, arms alone and joint, seeds 0-4; c149_n12_confine.py with NORM=ln, output c170_n12ln/ (without LayerNorm, C-149: alone 400, joint 3,000 to more than 20,000).
Predictions (written before running). (1) rms: joint/alone at most 2.5 (rescaling each example's hidden vector is the part that matters). (2) center: joint/alone at least 5. (3) without normalisation, the norm-S correlation at step 250 is at least 0.5 in all 5 seeds; with LayerNorm at most 0.3 in all 5. (4) n = 12 with LayerNorm: joint/alone median at most 3.

### C-170 result, parts (a) and (b) (n = 8, 5 seeds; c170_rms/, c170_center/, c170_none/, c170_ln/)
| hidden-layer normalisation | alone (median) | joint (median) | joint/alone | joint R^2 on S at 250 (layer 2 / 3) | corr(norm of layer-2 vector, S) at 250, joint |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| none (C-116; metric runs c170_none) | (250) | (2,550) | 10.2 | 0.60-0.72 / 0.79-0.83 | 0.60-0.75 |
| LayerNorm (C-169; metric runs c170_ln) | (250) | (400) | 1.6 | 0.13-0.18 / 0.08-0.11 | -0.03 to 0.26 |
| RMS scaling only | 250 200 300 300 300 (300) | 400 350 400 550 550 (400) | 1.33 | 0.08-0.18 / 0.07-0.10 | -0.24 to -0.01 |
| centring only | 200 200 250 250 350 (250) | 3,000 2,950 1,500 3,950 2,450 (2,950) | 11.8 | 0.68-0.80 / 0.83-0.87 | 0.64-0.69 |
Predictions. (1) rms joint/alone at most 2.5: HIT (1.33). (2) center at least 5: HIT (11.8). (3) norm-S correlation at least 0.5 without normalisation and at most 0.3 with LayerNorm, all seeds: HIT (0.60-0.75; -0.03 to 0.26).
Reading. The part of LayerNorm that removes the delay is the per-example rescaling: dividing each hidden vector by its root mean square removes both the takeover (R^2 on S 0.08-0.18) and the delay (1.33 times), while subtracting the mean only leaves both (R^2 0.68-0.80, 11.8 times). In ordinary joint training the size of each example's layer-2 vector itself tracks the sum S (correlation 0.60-0.75 at step 250), i.e. part of the sum code is carried by activation magnitude, and rescaling per example removes that channel. This supports route (a) of C-169; it does not exclude route (b) (rescaling also makes each layer insensitive to a common rescaling of its weights), which this design cannot separate.

### C-170 result, part (c) (n = 12 with LayerNorm, 5 seeds; c170_n12ln/n12confine.csv)
alone 400 500 400 600 600 (median 500); joint 800 1,400 700 800 900 (median 800); joint/alone 1.6. Without LayerNorm (C-149): alone 400, joint 7,300; >20,000; 3,000; 17,900; >20,000.
Prediction (4) joint/alone at most 3: HIT (1.6).
Reading (C-169, C-170 together). Normalising each example's hidden vector (LayerNorm, or just dividing by its root mean square) removes the joint delay of the middle sum bit at n = 8 (10 times down to 1.3-1.6) and at n = 12 (from 7 to more than 50 times down to 1.6), and with it the takeover of the shared layers by the code of the sum. Without normalisation, part of that code is carried by how large each example's hidden vector is (correlation with S 0.60-0.75 by step 250). So the full account of the delay now has three parts, each tested causally: the fast upper bits build a code of S in the shared layers, partly in the activation magnitude (C-113a, C-170); a per-parameter normalised optimiser turns their dominance of the shared gradient into slow progress for bit 4 (C-73, C-156, C-158, C-166, C-167); and per-example normalisation of the hidden layers removes the magnitude channel and the delay (C-169, C-170).

## C-171 (CONFIRMATORY; separates the two routes of C-169/C-170)
Why: per-example rescaling (C-170) both removes the activation-magnitude channel of the sum code (route a) and makes each layer insensitive to a common rescaling of its weights (route b; C-112 found that grown weight norms slow the turning of layer-2 rows under Adam). This design keeps route (a) open and closes route (b): every hidden weight row keeps its initial norm for the whole run (W = c v / ||v||, v trained, c fixed), so no weight norm can grow, while each example's activations remain free to vary in size.
Design. c165_depth.py with FIXNORM=1, depth 3, n = 8, Adam 1e-3, seeds 0-4, arms alone and joint, cap 10,000; output c171_fixnorm/. Check: the default code path is unchanged (seed 0 joint metrics at step 250 identical to C-116: 0.6981 / 0.8094).
Predictions (written before running). Route (a), which I expect: (1) joint/alone at least 5; (2) joint norm-S correlation at step 250 at least 0.5 in all seeds. If instead joint/alone is at most 2.5, the delay needs weight-norm growth (route b).

### C-171 result (fixed-norm weight rows, 5 seeds; c171_fixnorm/confine.csv)
alone 300 250 300 250 350 (median 300); joint not learned by 10,000 in 4 of 5 seeds, 6,150 in one (median at least 10,000); joint/alone at least 33. Joint at step 250: R^2 on S 0.705-0.761 (layer 2) / 0.817-0.848 (layer 3); norm-S correlation 0.61-0.75.
Predictions. (1) joint/alone at least 5: HIT (at least 33). (2) norm-S correlation at least 0.5 in all seeds: HIT (0.61-0.75).
Reading. Stopping every hidden weight row from growing does not remove the delay; it makes it much longer. So the delay does not need weight-norm growth (route b of C-169 is rejected as the reason LayerNorm helps), and the sum code still forms, including its activation-magnitude part (correlation 0.61-0.75). Together with C-170 this points to route (a): per-example rescaling removes the delay because it removes the magnitude channel of the sum code, not because it makes layers insensitive to weight scale. Why fixed norms make it worse is not tested (one guess: with rows unable to grow, Adam's steps turn the rows faster, so the other outputs' push changes the shared directions even more strongly).

## C-172 (CONFIRMATORY; back to Q1: does LayerNorm flatten the 'middle bits last' hump?)
Why: Q1 found that in the joint adder the middle sum bits are learned last (a hump over bit position), and the bit-4 delay was traced to the takeover (C-111 to C-171). If LayerNorm removes the takeover, the hump should flatten for all middle bits, not only bit 4.
Design. c172_bits.py: joint net, C-116 recipe, all nine bit times (two consecutive held-out evaluations at least 0.99, every 50 steps), NORM = none and ln, seeds 0-4, cap 10,000; outputs c172_none/, c172_ln/. Hump ratio = (largest time among bits 2-6) / (largest time among bits 0, 1, 8).
Predictions (written before running). (1) Without normalisation the hump ratio is at least 3 in at least 4 of 5 seeds. (2) With LayerNorm it is at most 2 in at least 4 of 5 seeds. (3) With LayerNorm the time of the slowest bit is at most half of the slowest bit's time without it, in every seed.

### C-172 result (5 seeds per arm; c172_none/, c172_ln/, c172_scores.txt)
Median time per sum bit 0..8 (steps): without normalisation 100, 450, 800, 2,500, 2,550, 2,850, 1,650, 950, 550; with LayerNorm 100, 200, 250, 350, 400, 400, 400, 400, 200. Slowest bit per seed without / with LayerNorm: 4,600 / 400; 3,950 / 450; 2,500 / 450; 2,850 / 500; 4,450 / 500. Hump ratio (largest of bits 2-6 over largest of bits 0, 1, 8): without 4.2-8.4; with 1.67-2.5.
Predictions. (1) hump ratio at least 3 without normalisation in at least 4 of 5: HIT (5 of 5). (2) at most 2 with LayerNorm in at least 4 of 5: MISSED (3 of 5; 1.67-2.5). (3) slowest bit with LayerNorm at most half of the slowest without, every seed: HIT (6 to 11 times faster).
Reading. LayerNorm does not only remove bit 4's delay: every middle bit is learned 6-11 times sooner, and the whole adder is learned within 400-500 steps. The order over bit position is still there in a mild form (low bits first, then the middle and bit 7 together at about 400, top bit at 200; ratio about 2), so the 'middle bits last' shape of Q1 is mostly the takeover cost and partly something that survives normalisation. The Q1 answer gains a condition: the strong hump belongs to un-normalised MLPs trained with per-parameter normalised optimisers.

## C-173 (CONFIRMATORY; does LayerNorm also rescue the worst optimiser case, Adagrad?)
Why: Adagrad gave the largest joint delay (at least 50 times, C-167). If the delay needs the sum-code takeover and LayerNorm prevents the takeover (C-169, C-170), LayerNorm should rescue Adagrad too.
Design. c165_depth.py with OPT=adagrad LR=1e-2 NORM=ln, depth 3, n = 8, seeds 0-4, arms alone and joint, cap 10,000; output c173_adagrad_ln/.
Prediction (written before running). joint/alone median at most 3.

### C-173 result (Adagrad + LayerNorm, 5 seeds; c173_adagrad_ln/confine.csv)
alone 300 300 200 300 300 (median 300); joint 700 650 500 700 900 (median 700); joint/alone 2.3 (Adagrad without LayerNorm: at least 50). Joint R^2 on S at 250: 0.193-0.245 / 0.103-0.129.
Prediction (at most 3): HIT.
Reading. LayerNorm also rescues the optimiser with the worst delay: from at least 50 times to 2.3 times. A normaliser that never forgets the other outputs' early gradients does little harm once those outputs cannot take over the shared layers.

## C-174 (check): numbers for C-169 to C-173 recomputed from the raw outputs (c174_verify.py -> c174_verify.txt)
All per-seed times, medians, ratios, R^2 ranges and norm-S correlations quoted for C-169 to C-173 match (values quoted to two decimals were checked against four-decimal recomputations; three borderline roundings had already been replaced by the exact values in C-166, C-167, C-171, C-173). No corrections.

## C-175 (CONFIRMATORY; C-172 at n = 12)
Why: the hump of Q1 was also found at n = 12; C-172 tested the LayerNorm condition at n = 8 only.
Design. c175_bits12.py: C-149 data recipe (n = 12, 300,000 training pairs, 20,000 held-out), joint net 3 x 128, Adam 1e-3, batch 256, all 13 bit times (two consecutive held-out evaluations at least 0.99, every 100 steps), cap 20,000; NORM = none and ln, seeds 0-4; outputs c175_none/, c175_ln/. Hump ratio = (largest time among bits 2-10) / (largest among bits 0, 1, 12), censored runs counted at 20,000.
Predictions (written before running). (1) none: hump ratio at least 3 in at least 4 of 5. (2) ln: slowest bit at most half of the none slowest bit in every seed. (3) ln: hump ratio at most 3 in at least 4 of 5.

### C-175 result (n = 12, 5 seeds per arm; c175_none/, c175_ln/, c175_scores.txt)
Median time per sum bit 0..12 (censored at 20,000): without normalisation 200, 600, 2,200, >20,000, 17,900, 15,000, 18,300, 15,600, 10,400, 7,600, 5,200, 2,500, 1,000 (10 bit-runs not learned by 20,000; every seed has at least one); with LayerNorm 100, 300, 400, 600, 800, 800, 1,100, 1,300, 1,200, 1,200, 1,100, 800, 500 (all learned; slowest bit 1,200-3,000). Hump ratio: without 15-29 (lower bounds); with 2.8-5.0.
Predictions. (1) hump ratio at least 3 without normalisation: HIT (5 of 5). (2) slowest bit with LayerNorm at most half of the slowest without, every seed: HIT (1,200-3,000 against more than 20,000). (3) hump ratio at most 3 with LayerNorm in at least 4 of 5: MISSED (2 of 5; 2.8-5.0).
Reading. At n = 12 LayerNorm turns an adder that does not finish in 20,000 steps into one that learns all 13 bits in 1,200-3,000 steps, at least 7-17 times faster. The hump keeps its shape in a much weaker form (middle bits 6-10 slowest at about 1,100-1,300 steps against 500 for the top bit), as at n = 8 (C-172). So the strong hump of Q1 is mostly the takeover cost; a mild middle-bits-last order remains with normalisation and is not explained here.

## C-176 (CONFIRMATORY; where must the normalisation sit?)
Why: confinement showed that the other outputs hurt bit 4 through the two lower hidden layers (C-116, C-121); the magnitude channel of the sum code is measured at layer 2 (C-170). If LayerNorm works by removing that channel where the takeover happens, it should be needed in the lower layers, not in the last one.
Design. c165_depth.py, depth 3, n = 8, Adam 1e-3, seeds 0-4, arms alone and joint, NORM = ln in hidden layers 1-2 only (NORM_LAYERS=1,2) and in layer 3 only (NORM_LAYERS=3); outputs c176_ln12/, c176_ln3/.
Predictions (written before running). (1) LayerNorm in layers 1-2 only: joint/alone at most 2.5. (2) LayerNorm in layer 3 only: joint/alone at least 5.

### C-176 result (5 seeds per arm; c176_ln12/, c176_ln3/)
| LayerNorm in | alone (median) | joint (median) | joint/alone | joint at 250: R^2 on S layer 2 / layer 3; corr(norm of layer-2 vector, S) |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| layers 1-2 | 200 200 250 250 250 (250) | 450 550 450 650 600 (550) | 2.2 | 0.22-0.23 / 0.41-0.43; 0.08-0.45 [corrected by C-183 from '-0.08 to 0.45'] |
| layer 3 only | 300 200 200 300 350 (300) | 400 450 300 500 500 (450) | 1.5 | 0.14-0.23 / 0.08-0.11; 0.31-0.59 |
| all three (C-169) | (250) | (400) | 1.6 | 0.13-0.18 / 0.08-0.11 |
| none (C-116) | (250) | (2,550) | 10.2 | 0.60-0.72 / 0.79-0.83 |
Predictions. (1) layers 1-2 only, at most 2.5: HIT (2.2). (2) layer 3 only, at least 5: MISSED (1.5).
Reading. Normalising only the LAST hidden layer removes the delay as well as normalising all three, and the lower layers then no longer turn into a code of S either (layer-2 R^2 0.14-0.23), although nothing in them is normalised. So the takeover is not a local event in the lower layers that a local normalisation must block: the upper bits build the S-code in the lower layers because a code carried partly by activation magnitude is useful to them at the output, and removing the magnitude channel anywhere on the path to the heads removes the reason to build it. My localisation argument (prediction 2) was wrong. Normalising layers 1-2 only leaves layer 3 free to carry an S-code (R^2 0.41-0.43) and removes most but not all of the delay (2.2 times).

## C-177 (CONFIRMATORY; direct test of the C-176 reading: is the takeover driven by the other outputs' use of the magnitude channel?)
Design. Plain network (no normalisation), depth 3, n = 8, Adam 1e-3, seeds 0-4. Arms: alone; joint_orms (the eight other heads read the last hidden layer divided by its root mean square, bit 4's head reads it raw; everything else shared as in joint); joint_4rms (the reverse: only bit 4's head reads the normalised layer). Output c177/. Reference: plain joint 10.2 times alone.
Predictions (written before running). (1) joint_orms: joint/alone at most 2.5 (if the other outputs cannot use activation magnitude, they do not build the magnitude code and do not take over). (2) joint_4rms: at least 5 (normalising only bit 4's own read-out leaves the takeover in place).

### C-177 result (5 seeds; c177/confine.csv)
| arm | times, seeds 0-4 (median) | /alone | joint at 250: R^2 on S layer 2 / layer 3; corr(norm of layer-2 vector, S) |
|---|---|---|---|
| alone | 200 200 250 250 300 (250) | 1 | |
| others read the normalised last layer (joint_orms) | 1,300 1,350 1,150 1,400 1,250 (1,300) | 5.2 | 0.18-0.26 / 0.12-0.15; 0.37-0.62 |
| bit 4 reads the normalised last layer (joint_4rms) | 350 450 300 400 500 (400) | 1.6 | 0.19-0.54 / 0.39-0.68; 0.38-0.75 |
| plain joint (C-116) | (2,550) | 10.2 | 0.60-0.72 / 0.79-0.83; 0.60-0.75 |
Predictions. (1) joint_orms at most 2.5: MISSED (5.2). (2) joint_4rms at least 5: MISSED (1.6). Both wrong, in the informative direction.
Reading. Normalising only bit 4's OWN read-out of the last hidden layer removes almost all of the delay (1.6 times), even though the other outputs still take over the shared layers (layer-3 R^2 on S up to 0.68, layer-2 norm-S correlation up to 0.75). Normalising only the other outputs' read-out weakens the takeover but leaves half the delay (5.2 times). So the cost is not the takeover as such; it is that bit 4 has to read a target that flips sign about 31 times along S from features whose per-example size is dominated by S. Removing that size variation from bit 4's input alone is enough. [CORRECTED by C-187: a fixed sum-shaped size variation does not recreate the delay, so 'size variation' alone is not the obstacle; what the division does that matters is tested in C-188.] This sharpens the account of C-169 to C-176: the other outputs build an S-code that lives partly in activation magnitude; Adam's normalisation makes bit 4's progress slow (C-73, C-156, C-167); and the specific obstacle is the magnitude variation in bit 4's input, which per-example normalisation removes (anywhere on the path, C-176, or just at bit 4's read-out, here). Caveat: 5 seeds, n = 8, Adam only; the claim 'the obstacle is the magnitude variation in bit 4's input' is the simplest reading of these two arms, not a derivation.

## C-178 (CONFIRMATORY control for C-177): rescaling or centring of bit 4's read-out?
Design. As C-177, arm joint_4center: bit 4's head reads the last hidden layer with each example's mean over units subtracted (no rescaling); seeds 0-4; output c178/.
Prediction (written before running). joint/alone at least 5 (centring does not remove the magnitude variation; C-170 found centring useless when applied to all layers).

### C-178 result (5 seeds; c178/confine.csv)
joint_4center: 5,550 5,300 900 2,250 2,350 (median 2,350; alone 250): 9.4 times, about the plain joint value (10.2). Prediction (at least 5): HIT.
Reading. Subtracting the mean from bit 4's input does nothing; dividing by its size removes the delay (C-177). The obstacle is the per-example magnitude of the shared features that bit 4 reads, which in joint training tracks the numeric sum. [CORRECTED by C-187: not by itself; see C-187/C-188.]

## C-179 (EXPLORATORY-WITH-PREDICTION, R1 with a new quantity): does the S-dominance of what bit 4 reads predict the size of the joint delay across settings?
Idea source: C-177/C-178 (the obstacle is the S-dominated last hidden layer that bit 4 reads). C-141's eight settings have measured joint/alone ratios: width 64: 33.3; width 128: 10.2; width 512: 3.3; frozen 1,024 + 3 x 128: 33.0; initial scale 2: 2.7; scale 4: 1.8; lr 3e-4: 22.9; lr 1e-2: 3.7.
Design. c165_depth.py, joint arm only, 300 steps (metrics at step 250), seeds 0-4, the eight settings; primary metric: R^2 of the last hidden layer's features on S at step 250 (median over seeds); secondary: correlation of the last hidden layer's per-example norm with S. Output c179_<setting>/. Caveat stated in advance: step 250 is a fixed time, while the speed of everything differs between settings (lr 1e-2 is about ten times faster).
Prediction (written before running). Spearman(primary metric, measured ratio) over the eight settings at least +0.6. No prediction for the secondary metric (reported).

### C-179 result (8 settings x 5 seeds, joint, step 250; c179_*/, c179_score.py -> c179_scores.txt)
Median R^2 of the last hidden layer on S (setting: R^2, measured ratio): width 64: 0.780, 33.3; width 128: 0.810, 10.2; width 512: 0.756, 3.3; frozen: 0.825, 33.0; scale 2: 0.509, 2.7; scale 4: 0.105, 1.8; lr 3e-4: 0.834, 22.9; lr 1e-2: 0.405, 3.7. Spearman +0.76. Secondary: linear correlation of the last layer's norm with S, Spearman -0.40 (see C-180 for why a linear correlation is the wrong summary); second-to-last layer R^2 on S, Spearman +0.86.
Prediction (Spearman of the primary metric at least +0.6): HIT (+0.76).
Reading. Across eight settings, how strongly the hidden layers have become a code of S by step 250 orders the size of the joint delay (Spearman +0.76 for the last layer, +0.86 for the layer below). The width effect is only partly captured (widths 64, 128, 512 have similar R^2 but ratios 33, 10, 3), so this is an ordering, not a formula; R1 stays open, with a better-motivated quantity than C-140's gradient share. Caveat: one fixed step for settings that run at different speeds; 8 points.

## C-180 (DESCRIPTIVE, no prediction): how does the size of the last hidden layer depend on S?
Why: C-177/C-178 point to the per-example size of what bit 4 reads; the last layer's LINEAR correlation of size with S was only -0.11 to +0.42 (C-179), which seemed to contradict that.
Design. C-116 recipe, joint arm, 250 steps, seeds 0-4; the norm of each example's last-layer vector against S, in 16 bins of S (c180_normprofile.py -> c180_normprofile.txt).
Result. The size is U-shaped in S: smallest around S = 224-255 (about 21-25), largest at the extremes (34.5-52.4), and the 16-bin step function of S explains 75-83 percent of its variance in every seed (coefficient of variation 0.21-0.24), while the linear correlation is -0.11 to +0.42. So the per-example size of what bit 4 reads is indeed largely a function of S, just not a linear one; the C-177 reading stands, and the linear correlation used as the C-179 secondary metric is the wrong summary.

## C-181 (EXPLORATORY-WITH-PREDICTION; R1/R2 per run): does a run's S-dominance at step 250 predict its learning time?
Idea: C-179 found that S-dominance at step 250 orders the delay across settings. Within one setting, the 200 C-162 runs have known T.
Design. C-83 recipe (as C-162) for s = 100-299, 250 steps; at step 250 the R^2 of layer 2 and layer 3 on S and the share of the layer-3 norm variance explained by 16 S-bins (C-180); Spearman with T. Script c181_metric.py, output c181_metric.csv.
Prediction (written before running). Spearman(layer-3 R^2 on S at 250, T) at least +0.3 (more S-dominance, slower). (For comparison, the head start J(250) has Spearman -0.81 with T, C-162.)

### C-181 result (200 runs; c181_metric.csv, c181_scores.txt)
Spearman with T: layer-3 R^2 on S at 250 -0.08 (range 0.777-0.850); layer-2 R^2 -0.18 (0.544-0.773); share of layer-3 norm variance explained by S bins -0.04 (0.667-0.888).
Prediction (layer-3 R^2 Spearman at least +0.3): MISSED (-0.08).
Reading. Within one setting every run's shared layers are taken over to a similar degree by step 250 (layer-3 R^2 0.78-0.85), and how much does not predict which run is slow; the head start J does (-0.81). So S-dominance sets the size of the delay between settings (C-179) but not the run-to-run luck within a setting, consistent with R2: luck is in how fast bit 4's own signal grows inside an equally taken-over network.

## C-182 (CONFIRMATORY; is initialisation luck a property of escaping the takeover?)
Why: R2 asks what makes an initialisation lucky. With LayerNorm there is no takeover (C-169, C-170). If luck is about how fast bit 4 escapes a taken-over network, the spread of learning times between initialisations should shrink a lot with LayerNorm.
Design. C-83 recipe (split seed 500, batch stream 777, torch seed 10000 + s, s = 0-39) with LayerNorm before each ReLU (c125_trajectories.py with NORM=ln), full runs to the bit-4 time; output c182_ln/. Reference C-83 (no normalisation, same 40 initialisations): median 2,850, 10th-90th percentile ratio 3.67, max/min 6.7.
Predictions (written before running). (1) The 10th-90th percentile ratio of the bit-4 time with LayerNorm is at most 1.8 (at most half of 3.67). (2) Spearman between the 40 LayerNorm times and the 40 C-83 times below +0.3 (the luck does not carry over).

### C-182 result (40 initialisations with LayerNorm; c182_ln/, c182_scores.txt)
LayerNorm: all 40 learned, median 400 steps, range 300-450 (10th-90th percentile ratio 1.29, max/min 1.50; the evaluation grid is 50 steps, so this spread is close to the resolution). Same 40 initialisations without normalisation (C-83): median 2,850, ratio 3.67, max/min 6.7. Spearman between the two sets of times +0.28.
Predictions. (1) percentile ratio at most 1.8: HIT (1.29). (2) Spearman below +0.3: HIT, narrowly (+0.28).
Reading (R2). The large differences between initialisations exist only when the other outputs take over the shared layers: with LayerNorm the same 40 initialisations all learn bit 4 within 300-450 steps, and which ones were lucky without LayerNorm barely carries over. So a 'lucky first layer' is lucky in the race against the takeover, not in learning bit 4 as such. This does not give the compact static description R2 asked for, but it says what the luck is about.

## C-183 (check): numbers for C-175 to C-182 recomputed from the raw outputs (c183_verify.py -> c183_verify.txt)
All times, medians, ratios and R^2 ranges match; c179_score.py and c180_normprofile.py reproduce their saved outputs exactly. One correction: in the C-176 table the joint norm-S correlation for LayerNorm in layers 1-2 is 0.08-0.45, not -0.08 to 0.45 (I had taken the lower end from the alone arm).

## C-184 (CONFIRMATORY; what is the mild hump that survives LayerNorm?)
Idea source: C-12 (single-output nets: bit times rise with position, 100 to 800 steps, no hump) and C-172 (joint with LayerNorm: 100, 200, 250, 350, 400, 400, 400, 400, 200). If LayerNorm removes the interaction between bits, the joint-with-LayerNorm profile should look like the single-bit profile with LayerNorm.
Design. c184_single.py: one single-output net per sum bit (bits 0-8), C-172 recipe otherwise (seeds 0-2), with LayerNorm; output c184_ln/. (No-normalisation single-bit times are taken from C-12 for comparison, different batch sampling, so only the shape is compared.)
Predictions (written before running). (1) For bits 0-7, Spearman between the joint-with-LayerNorm medians (C-172) and the single-bit-with-LayerNorm medians at least 0.7. (2) For every bit 0-7, the joint-with-LayerNorm median is at most 2 times the single-bit-with-LayerNorm median. (No prediction for bit 8, which in C-12 was slower alone than jointly.)

### C-184 result (single-output nets with LayerNorm, 3 seeds per bit; c184_ln/bits.csv, c184_scores.txt)
Single-bit medians, bits 0-8: 50, 100, 100, 150, 250, 400, 700, 700, 300. Joint with LayerNorm (C-172): 100, 200, 250, 350, 400, 400, 400, 400, 200. Joint/single: 2.0, 2.0, 2.5, 2.3, 1.6, 1.0, 0.57, 0.57, 0.67.
Predictions. (1) Spearman over bits 0-7 at least 0.7: HIT (0.94). (2) joint at most 2 times single for every bit 0-7: MISSED (bits 2 and 3: 2.5 and 2.3).
Reading. With LayerNorm, the joint profile follows the single-bit profile (Spearman 0.94): each bit's own difficulty rises with position up to bit 7. In the joint net the low and middle bits (0-4) are 1.6-2.5 times slower than alone, and the upper bits 5-8 are as fast or faster (0.57-1.0) than alone, so with normalisation the bits share their training effort: the upper bits now gain from being trained together and the lower ones pay a moderate cost, without the 10-fold delay. The mild 'hump' under LayerNorm is mostly each bit's own difficulty (rising with position) plus a flat ceiling of about 400 steps for bits 4-7 in the joint net.

## C-185 (CONFIRMATORY; is the LayerNorm fix general across the settings with the largest delays?)
Why: C-169/C-170 tested LayerNorm at width 128, lr 1e-3 (delay 10 times without it). The largest delays were at width 64 (33 times), lr 3e-4 (23 times) and with a frozen random first layer (33 times) (C-141).
Design. c165_depth.py with NORM=ln, depth 3, n = 8, seeds 0-4, arms alone and joint: (a) W = 64; (b) LR = 3e-4 (cap 20,000); (c) FW = 1024 (frozen random layer of width 1,024 in front, as C-116). Outputs c185_W64/, c185_lr3e-4/, c185_FW1024/.
Prediction (written before running). joint/alone median at most 3 in each of the three settings.

### C-185 result (LayerNorm, 5 seeds per setting; c185_W64/, c185_lr3e-4/, c185_FW1024/)
| setting | alone (median) | joint (median) | joint/alone with LayerNorm | without (C-141 list) |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| width 64 | 300 300 300 350 300 (300) | 900 750 650 900 750 (750) | 2.5 | 33.3 |
| lr 3e-4 | 400 400 400 400 500 (400) | 850 1,050 750 1,100 1,050 (1,050) | 2.6 | 22.9 |
| frozen 1,024 + 3 x 128 | 300 350 250 300 400 (300) | 300 350 350 350 350 (350) | 1.17 | 33.0 |
Prediction (at most 3 in each): HIT (2.5, 2.6, 1.17).
Reading. LayerNorm removes almost all of the joint delay in the three settings where it was largest (from 23-33 times to 1.2-2.6 times). The fix is general across width, learning rate and a frozen random input layer at n = 8.

## C-186 (check): C-184 and C-185 recomputed from the raw CSV files (c186_verify.txt): all quoted medians, per-seed times and ratios match.

## C-187 (CONFIRMATORY, sufficiency test of the C-177 reading): does putting the sum-shaped size variation back into bit 4's input recreate the delay?
Idea. C-177/C-178 say the obstacle is that the features bit 4 reads vary in size with S (U-shaped, C-180). Sufficiency test: in a LayerNorm network (no takeover, no delay), let bit 4's head read the last hidden layer multiplied by a fixed function m(S) equal to the C-180 profile normalised to mean 1 (16 bins: 1.155 1.178 1.100 1.009 0.891 0.792 0.726 0.706 0.738 0.799 0.883 0.965 1.065 1.192 1.321 1.479; c187_profile.txt).
Design. c165_depth.py with NORM=ln, depth 3, n = 8, seeds 0-4; arms alone, alone_4mag (bit 4 alone, reading m(S) x h3), joint, joint_4mag (joint, only bit 4 reads m(S) x h3). Output c187/.
Prediction (written before running). (1) joint_4mag median at least 3 times the alone median (the size variation alone recreates a delay). No prediction for alone_4mag (reported: it separates 'size variation hurts by itself' from 'size variation hurts together with the other outputs and Adam').

### C-187 result (5 seeds; c187/confine.csv)
alone 250 200 250 250 300 (250); alone_4mag 250 200 250 250 300 (250, identical seed by seed); joint (LayerNorm) 350 450 350 400 450 (400); joint_4mag 400 400 350 450 500 (400). joint_4mag/alone 1.6.
Prediction (1) joint_4mag at least 3 times alone: MISSED (1.6, the same as plain LayerNorm joint).
Reading, and a correction of my C-177/C-178 reading. A fixed sum-shaped size variation in bit 4's input does nothing, alone or jointly. So 'the features bit 4 reads vary in size with S' is NOT by itself the obstacle, and the sentences in C-177, C-178, ANSWERS.md, README.md and STUDY_GUIDE.md that said so are withdrawn (corrected there). What stands from C-177/C-178: dividing bit 4's input by its own per-example size removes the delay in an un-normalised, taken-over network, and centring does not. What that division does beyond equalising size (it also changes the geometry of the features bit 4 reads, and the gradient bit 4 sends back into the shared layers loses its component along each example's own feature vector) is not yet separated; C-188 tests the gradient part.

## C-188 (CONFIRMATORY, follows C-187): is it the forward rescaling or the gradient bit 4 sends back that matters?
Design. Plain network (no normalisation), C-177 recipe, seeds 0-4: arm joint_4rms_sg: bit 4's head reads h3 / rms(h3) in the forward pass, but the rms is treated as a constant in the backward pass (stop-gradient), so the gradient bit 4 sends into the shared layers is only rescaled per example, not projected. Output c188/.
Prediction (written before running; my best guess after C-187). joint_4rms_sg at least 3 times alone (the projection in the backward pass is what removes the delay in C-177). If it is at most 2 times alone, the forward rescaling suffices and the projection is not needed.

### C-188 result (5 seeds; c188/confine.csv)
joint_4rms_sg: 350 450 350 450 750 (median 450; alone 250): 1.8 times. Prediction (at least 3 times): MISSED.
Reading. The forward division of bit 4's input by its own size is enough; the projection it adds to the gradient that bit 4 sends into the shared layers is not needed. With C-187 (a fixed S-shaped size variation added to an un-taken-over network creates no delay), the obstacle is in how the taken-over features look to bit 4's head through their per-example size, not in size variation as such. C-189 asks whether the S-dependent part of that size is the part that matters.

## C-189 (CONFIRMATORY, follows C-188): is it the S-dependent part of the feature size?
Design. Plain network, C-177 recipe, seeds 0-4: arm joint_4divS: bit 4's head reads h3 divided by the fixed C-180 S-profile m(S) (the part of the size predicted by S at step 250), instead of by its actual size. Output c189/.
Prediction (written before running). If the S-dependent part of the size is the obstacle in a taken-over network: joint_4divS at most 2.5 times alone. If it is at least 5 times, the part that matters is the size variation not explained by a fixed function of S (or its change during training).

### C-189 result (5 seeds; c189/confine.csv)
joint_4divS: 1,250 3,750 850 2,600 2,700 (median 2,600; alone 250): 10.4 times, as plain joint. Prediction (at most 2.5): MISSED.
Reading. Dividing bit 4's input by the S-predicted part of its size does nothing; dividing by its actual size removes the delay (C-177, C-188). With C-187, the S-shaped part of the size is neither needed nor enough. What remains of the per-example size is (i) variation not explained by S and (ii) the change of the overall size during training (the shared layers' activations grow as the other outputs push them, C-112). C-190 tests (ii).

## C-190 (CONFIRMATORY, follows C-189): is it the growth of the overall feature scale?
Design. Plain network, C-177 recipe, seeds 0-4: arm joint_4glob: bit 4's head reads h3 divided by a single number per minibatch (the root mean square over the batch and units, a constant in the backward pass). This removes the overall scale and its growth during training but keeps every per-example size difference. Output c190/.
Prediction (written before running). If the growth of the overall scale of the shared features relative to bit 4's head is the obstacle: joint_4glob at most 2.5 times alone.

### C-190 result (5 seeds; c190/confine.csv)
joint_4glob: 350 450 350 700 850 (median 450; alone 250): 1.8 times. Prediction (at most 2.5): HIT.
Reading. Dividing bit 4's input by a single number per batch, which removes only the overall scale of the shared last layer and its change during training and keeps every per-example difference, removes most of the delay (1.8 times against 10.2). Together with C-187 to C-189: the part of the obstacle that bit 4's read-out normalisation removes is the overall scale of the shared features, which the other outputs drive, not the S-shaped per-example size. C-191 measures how that scale changes in the different arms.

## C-191 (DESCRIPTIVE with predictions): how does the overall scale of the last hidden layer change during training?
Design. C-116 recipe, seeds 0-4, arms alone, joint, and joint with fixed-norm weight rows (C-171); the root mean square of the last hidden layer over 8,192 training inputs at steps 0, 50, 100, 250, 500, 1,000 (c191_scale.py -> c191_scale.txt).
Predictions (written before running). (1) In joint training the RMS at step 250 is at least 2 times its step-0 value, and at least 1.5 times the alone arm's step-250 value, in all 5 seeds. (2) With fixed-norm rows the joint RMS still grows by at least 1.5 times by step 250 (activations can grow by alignment even when weight norms cannot), which would reconcile C-171 with C-190.

### C-191 result (c191_scale.txt; RMS of the last hidden layer at steps 0 / 50 / 100 / 250 / 500 / 1,000)
alone: from 0.055-0.066 to 0.79-1.34 at 250 and 1.16-1.97 at 1,000. joint: 0.50-0.58 already at step 50, 2.40-2.73 at 250, 5.97-6.79 at 1,000. joint with fixed-norm rows: 1.35-1.45 at 250, 1.77-2.03 at 1,000.
Predictions. (1) joint RMS at 250 at least 2 times its start and at least 1.5 times alone's, all seeds: HIT (about 40 times its start; 2.0-3.4 times alone's). (2) fixed-norm joint grows at least 1.5 times by 250: HIT (about 25 times), but it grows much less than plain joint (1.4 against 2.6 at 250; 1.9 against 6.4 at 1,000), so the larger C-171 delay is NOT explained by a larger overall scale. C-171 remains unexplained.
Reading. In joint training the other outputs blow up the scale of the shared last layer about tenfold within 50 steps and keep growing it (to about 6 by step 1,000, against about 1.5 alone). C-190 shows that removing that overall scale from bit 4's input removes most of the delay. One mechanism that would produce this: under Adam each step moves bit 4's read-out weights by about the learning rate whatever the input scale, so its logit moves by an amount proportional to the input scale; with inputs 3-6 times larger than alone, bit 4's read-out takes effectively 3-6 times larger, noisier steps. C-192 tests that directly by shrinking only bit 4's read-out step size.

## C-192 (CONFIRMATORY, follows C-190/C-191): is the obstacle the effective step size of bit 4's read-out?
Design. Plain network, C-177 recipe, seeds 0-4. Bit 4's read-out is reparametrised as z4 = c (w4 . h3 + b4) with w4, b4 initialised to the head's row 4 divided by c (identical initial logits); under Adam this multiplies the effective step size of bit 4's read-out by c and changes nothing else. Arms: alone; c = 1 (control, must behave like plain joint); c = 0.25; c = 0.1. Output c192/.
Predictions (written before running). (1) c = 1: at least 5 times alone (control). (2) c = 0.25: at most 3 times alone. (3) c = 0.1: at most 3 times alone.

### C-191 result (c191_scale.txt; RMS of the last hidden layer at steps 0 / 50 / 100 / 250 / 500 / 1,000)
alone: from 0.055-0.066 to 0.79-1.34 at 250 and 1.16-1.97 at 1,000. joint: 0.50-0.58 already at step 50, 2.40-2.73 at 250, 5.97-6.79 at 1,000. joint with fixed-norm rows: 1.35-1.45 at 250, 1.77-2.03 at 1,000.
Predictions. (1) joint RMS at 250 at least 2 times its start and at least 1.5 times alone's, all seeds: HIT (about 40 times its start; 2.0-3.4 times alone's). (2) fixed-norm joint grows at least 1.5 times by 250: HIT, but it grows much less than plain joint (1.4 against 2.6 at 250; 1.9 against 6.4 at 1,000), so the larger C-171 delay is NOT explained by a larger overall scale; C-171 remains unexplained.
Reading. In joint training the other outputs blow up the overall scale of the shared last layer about tenfold within 50 steps and keep growing it (about 6 by step 1,000, against about 1.5 alone). With C-190 (removing that scale from bit 4's input removes most of the delay), a candidate mechanism is that bit 4's read-out takes effectively larger, noisier steps as its input grows; this was planned as C-192 (shrink only bit 4's read-out step size) and not run.

# Status at wrap-up (27 Oct 2026, 10:00)
Paused at the owner's request. Next steps when resumed: C-192 (bit 4's read-out step size), why fixed-norm weight rows make the delay worse (C-171), and a first-principles formula for the size of the delay (R1). All numbers up to C-191 are in this log; C-184 to C-190 were checked in C-186 and by the scripts named in each result; C-191 is a direct measurement (c191_scale.txt).

# Added on 28 Oct 2026, while writing the public README (no new training)
### C-192 result (scored now; the runs had finished on 27 Oct at 09:58, before the wrap-up note above, but were not scored; predictions as written above, before the run; c192/confine.csv, c192.log; script c165_depth.py with the joint_4lr arms)
Bit-4 time, seeds 0-4: alone 200 200 250 250 300 (median 250); c = 1 (control) 4,550 5,000 1,000 2,600 2,550 (2,600; 10.4 times alone); c = 0.25: 3,200 4,550 1,450 2,350 2,700 (2,700; 10.8 times); c = 0.1: 5,050 4,200 1,300 2,700 2,600 (2,700; 10.8 times).
Predictions. (1) c = 1 at least 5 times alone: HIT (10.4). (2) c = 0.25 at most 3 times alone: MISSED (10.8). (3) c = 0.1 at most 3 times alone: MISSED (10.8).
Reading. Shrinking only the effective step size of bit 4's read-out (by 4 or 10 times) does not change the delay at all. So the candidate mechanism written under C-191 (bit 4's read-out takes larger, noisier steps because its input has grown) is rejected. What remains established from C-187 to C-191: dividing bit 4's input by the overall size of the shared last layer removes most of the delay (C-190), and this does not work through the read-out's step size (C-192). Why it works stays open.

### Correction to C-74 (found while rebuilding the README numbers from c74_w512/sepmoments_n8.csv and c74_w512.log)
The W = 512 bit-4 times are 300 300 350 300 350, median 300, not '300 300 350 350 350, median 350'. Prediction (1) of C-74 (median at most 300) is therefore a HIT on the boundary, not a miss, and the ratio to the alone median is 2.0, not 2.3. The reading changes accordingly: with separate moments at W = 512 the bit-4 time (300) is within a factor 2 of the alone time (150), against 500 for ordinary Adam. The rest of C-74 (W = 64: 3,200; the width dependence of the residual) is unchanged.

### Checks R-1, V-1, R-5, V-4, R-8, R-9 re-implemented (part2_margin_checks.py)
The original one-off analysis code for these checks was not kept. part2_margin_checks.py re-implements them from the definitions above. R-1 (0 violations; Spearman 0.735 to 0.802), V-1 (zero mismatches for n = 3, 4, 5, 6, 8, 10), R-5 (all ten SMALL/LARGE error rates and ratios) and V-4 (all ten ratios, median 0.64) are reproduced exactly. R-8: the truncated-ripple rows are reproduced exactly; the noisy-threshold rows depend on the noise draw and come out close (V-4 ratios 18.4, 5.9, 2.8, 1.9 for sigma 0.5, 1, 2, 4 against 16.9, 7.0, 3.2, 1.8), with the same conclusion (every noisy-threshold V-4 ratio is above 1.8). R-9: the regression coefficients differ in detail from the numbers above (for example seed 100: 3.62 -> 4.33 instead of 3.68 -> 3.47) because the exact regression set-up was not recorded, but the conclusion is the same: the SMALL coefficient does not shrink by half in any seed, and the correlation between SMALL and the nearest-training distance is about 0 (-0.01 to +0.01).
